Example 1.35
.

Let
Y=T^{*}\mathbb{C}P^{1}
but now make
\mathbb{C}^{*}
act with weight one on both the fibres and the
\mathbb{C}P^{1}
base, using the twisted action described in
[
RŽ24
, Example 7.6]
.
The fixed locus consists of two points in the zero section, and the positive weights mentioned in (
5
) are
W^{+}=\{1,3\}
.

Let
(a,b)
be a free weight, equivalently
3a\notin b\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
.
Then

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}E^{-}\!SH^{*}(Y)\cong\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{2},&&E^{\infty}\!SH^{*}(Y)\cong\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{2},&&E^{+}\!SH^{*}(Y)=0,\end{array}

holds as before, but the
HF^{*}(H_{\lambda})
groups are more complicated.
Unlike the previous example, there is a
\mathbb{Z}/3
-torsion submanifold in
Y
, arising as the fiber over the fixed point that is not the minimum of the moment map. It gives rise to Morse–Bott manifolds diffeomorphic to
S^{1}
for fractional slope values
(3k+1)/3
and
(3k+2)/3
, for all
k\in\mathbb{N}.

The Morse–Bott–Floer spectral sequence that converges to
E^{-}SH^{*}(Y)
is shown in
Figure

3
; it has converged on the
E_{1}
-page. On the
E_{0}
-page, the fractional slope-valued columns are shifted copies of
H^{*}(S^{1})[\![u]\!]
, whereas the positive integer slope-valued ones are shifted copies of
H^{*}(\mathbb{R}P^{3})[\![u]\!]
(using
\mathrm{char}\,\mathbb{K}=0
). The dots on the
E_{1}
-page in columns with slope values
\leq p
are
\mathbb{K}
-linear generators of
HF^{*}(H_{p^{+}})
.

Figure 1.
The
E_{0}
,
E_{1}
pages for the spectral sequence converging to
E^{-}SH^{*}(T^{*}\mathbb{C}P^{1})
, using a twisted action on
T^{*}\mathbb{C}P^{1}.
The slope values are in bold. The zeroth column is
E^{-}QH^{*}(Y).
Each dot is a copy of
\mathbb{K}
. The fat dots are the
u^{0}
-contributions: these generate the non-equivariant spectral sequence which converges to
SH^{*}(Y)=0
.

The
E_{1}
-page in
Figure

3
shows how the rank of
HF^{*}(H_{\lambda})
steadily grows with
\lambda
, as it slowly converges to
\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{2}\cong E^{\infty}QH^{*}(Y)\cong E^{\infty}SH^{*}(Y)
.
How the new generators appearing in higher columns arrange themselves into the Young diagram
\mathcal{Y}_{p}
is difficult to extrapolate because the spectral sequence forgets the
u
-module action. We can however deduce the following properties of the slice polynomial.
Let
Z_{k},N_{k}
be as before, and let
X_{k}:=2+2t+\cdots+2t^{k}+t^{k+1}.
Then
s_{0}=Z_{0}=2
, and for
k\in\mathbb{N}
:

(19)

s_{k+\frac{1}{3}}=X_{2k},\quad\textrm{and either }\;(s_{k+\frac{2}{3}},s_{k+1})=(N_{2k+1},Z_{2k+2})\;\;\textrm{ or }\;\;(s_{k+\frac{2}{3}},s_{k+1})=(Z_{2k+1},N_{2k+2}).

This encodes the invariant factors of all
E^{-}c^{*}_{\lambda}
maps, and also determines the filtration polynomial.

1.8.
Comparing different weights via equivariant Seidel isomorphisms

Recall by
Remark

1.2
that the weight
(a,b)
singles out a specific choice of
S^{1}
-action
S^{1}\hookrightarrow S^{1}\times S^{1}
.
The Floer complexes

E^{\diamondsuit}_{(a,b)}HF^{*}(H_{\lambda})
are related by chain isomorphisms, called
Seidel isomorphisms
, which change the data
a,H_{\lambda}
.
These are discussed in detail in the Appendix. The main theorem about these is
Theorem

A.1
; here we just mention the simplest example, at cohomology level:

(20)

\mathcal{S}:E^{\diamondsuit}_{(a,b)}HF^{*}(H_{\lambda})\cong E^{\diamondsuit}_{(a-b,b)}HF^{*}(H_{\lambda-1})[2\mu],\qquad(\textrm{1-orbit }x)\mapsto\varphi^{*}x,

where
(\varphi^{*}x)(t)=\varphi_{-t}(x(t))
, and where
\mu
is the Maslov index of
\varphi
.
It follows that for
k\in\mathbb{N},

\mathcal{S}^{k}:E^{\diamondsuit}_{(a,b)}HF^{*}(H_{k^{+}})\cong E^{\diamondsuit}_{(a-kb,b)}QH^{*}(Y)[2k\mu],

which as a
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module is isomorphic to
H^{*}(Y)\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}E^{\diamondsuit}H^{*}(\mathrm{point})
, by
Lemma

1.4
.

Corollary 1.36
.

E_{(a,b)}^{\diamondsuit}SH^{*}(Y)
can be identified with a direct limit of
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module maps

(21)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{(a,b)}^{\diamondsuit}QH^{*}(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{(a-b,b)}^{\diamondsuit}QH^{*}(Y)[2\mu]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{(a-2b,b)}^{\diamondsuit}QH^{*}(Y)[4\mu]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{a,b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{a-b,b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{a-2b,b}}

The
u^{0}
-part of the
r_{a,b}
maps is the rotation map
r:QH^{*}(Y)\to QH^{*+2\mu}(Y)
from Ritter
[
Rit14
]
, which corresponds to
quantum product by the class
Q_{\varphi^{a}}\in QH^{2\mu}(Y)
described in
[
RŽ23
]
.

The continuation map
c_{k^{+}}:E^{\diamondsuit}_{(a,b)}QH^{*}(Y)\to E^{\diamondsuit}_{(a,b)}HF^{*}(H_{k^{+}})
, suitably composed with Seidel isomorphisms, defines a
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-linear map:

(22)

ER_{k}:=\mathcal{S}^{k}\circ c_{k^{+}}=r_{a-(k-1)b,b}\circ\cdots\circ r_{a,b}:E^{-}_{(a,b)}QH^{*}(Y)\to E^{-}_{(a-kb,b)}QH^{*}(Y)[2k\mu].

We call these
rotation maps
, to distinguish them from the Seidel isomorphisms: they need not be isomorphisms, nor injective in general, since we composed Seidel isomorphisms with continuation maps.
The rotation maps are the key ingredient for the proof of

Theorem

1.6
. The filtration and slice polynomials for the map
ER_{k}
equal

f_{k}
and
s_{k}
(since an isomorphism,
\mathcal{S}^{k}
, does not affect these).

We conclude with a description of how the rotation maps are compatible with the filtrations. We label the three filtrations in
Lemma

1.29
by
\mathscr{F}_{j,(a,b)}^{\diamondsuit,p}
for
\diamondsuit\in\{-,\infty,+\}.

Theorem 1.37
.

For all
\diamondsuit\in\{-,\infty,+\}
,
j\in\mathbb{Z}
,
p\in[0,\infty]
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the filtrations satisfy:

ER_{k}^{-1}(\mathscr{F}_{j,(a-kb,b)}^{\diamondsuit,p})=\mathscr{F}_{j,(a,b)}^{\diamondsuit,p+k}.\qquad\textrm{ In particular, }\;ER_{k}:\mathscr{F}_{j,(a,b)}^{\diamondsuit,p+k}\to\mathscr{F}_{j,(a-kb,b)}^{\diamondsuit,p}.

Remark 1.38
.

In this paper, for reasons of space, we have not used the machinery due to Liebenschutz-Jones
[
LJ20
,
LJ21
]
, called “intertwining relation”, which implies algebraic constraints on the
ER_{k}
maps. In examples, that relation can sometimes recursively determine
ER_{k}
from the
u^{0}
-theory (the non-equivariant quantum theory) and the
T^{0}
-theory (the non-quantum equivariant theory).

Acknowledgements.

We thank Mohammed Abouzaid and Paul Seidel
for helpful conversations.

2.
Algebra I: filtrations on
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-modules

Throughout the general algebra sections,
\mathbb{K}
denotes any field of characteristic zero. In Floer-theoretic applications, it will be the Novikov field.

2.1.
Basic prelimiaries about
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]

The
\mathbb{K}
-algebra of power series in
u
over
\mathbb{K}
is denoted
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
.
Its units,
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{\times}\subset\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
, are the series with non-zero
u^{0}
-coefficient, and any non-zero element of
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
can be written as
u^{k}\cdot\textrm{unit}
for a unique
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Indeed, there is a function

(23)

\nu:\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!):=\{\textrm{Laurent series}\}\to\mathbb{Z}\cup\{\infty\},\;\nu(u^{k}\cdot\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{\times})=k,\;\textrm{ and }\;\nu(0):=\infty,

which is a discrete valuation, and
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]=\{\nu\geq 0\}
is its valuation ring. Thus,

\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
is a discrete valuation ring (DVR) with maximal ideal
(u)
.
In particular,
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
is a principal ideal domain (PID), indeed its non-zero ideals are classified:
(u^{k})
for
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

For any
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-linear map
Q:V_{1}\to V_{2}
of free
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-modules of finite rank, the Smith normal form gives rise to a choice of bases for
V_{1},V_{2}
in which the matrix
Q
only has non-zero entries along the main diagonal,
\mathrm{diag}(u^{a_{1}},\ldots,u^{a_{r}},0,\ldots,0)
, with
0\leq a_{1}\leq\cdots\leq a_{r}
. The
u^{a_{j}}
are the
invariant factors
of
Q
: the
a_{j}
are unique, independent of choices, as invariant factors are unique up to rescaling by units, and

(24)

\begin{split}u^{a_{1}+\cdots+a_{j}}&=\mathrm{gcd}\{\textrm{all }j\times j\textrm{ minors of }Q\},\\
a_{1}+\cdots+a_{j}&=\min\{\nu(\text{det}A):A\textrm{ is a non-singular }j\times j\textrm{ submatrix of }Q\},\end{split}

noting that greatest common divisors are meaningful only up to rescaling by units (here,
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{\times}
). That relation follows from noting that the determinants of
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module automorphisms lie in
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{\times}
.

Example 2.1
.

When
V_{1}=V_{2}=\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{2}
, so
Q=(Q_{ij})
is a
2\times 2
matrix over
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
, the invariant factors are
q:=\mathrm{gcd}(Q_{ij})
and
\tfrac{1}{q}\text{det}Q
. For example, if
Q_{ij}\neq 0
then
Q_{ij}=\textrm{unit}\cdot u^{n_{ij}}
, so
q=u^{\min n_{ij}}
.

Since
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
is a PID, we often use the structure theorem for finitely generated
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-modules
W
:

(25)

\begin{split}&W\cong T\oplus\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{r}\quad\textrm{ and }\quad T\cong T_{k_{1}}\oplus\cdots\oplus T_{k_{s}}\;\;(T=\textrm{Torsion}(W),\textrm{ but }\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{r}\textrm{ is non-canonical})\\
&\textrm{where }T_{k}:=\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]/u^{k}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!].\qquad\textrm{Thus, }T_{1}=\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]/u\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\cong\mathbb{K}\textrm{ with the trivial $u$-action},\end{split}

where
T\subset W
is the torsion submodule,
r=\mathrm{rank}_{\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]}\,W
, and the
k_{1}\leq\cdots\leq k_{s}
in
\mathbb{N}
are unique.

We also need the following Lemma in
Section

14.2
. This technical result is needed because invariant factors of
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-linear maps between free
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-modules need not behave well under composition.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
I
be an identity matrix of size
t\times t
.
Suppose we have a sequence of square matrices

(26)

f_{j}:=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}I+A_{j}u&B_{j}u\\
C_{j}u&D_{j}u\end{smallmatrix}\right)

of the same size, for
j=1,2,\ldots,

where
A_{j},B_{j},C_{j},D_{j}
are matrices over
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
.
Then the invariant factors of the composite
F_{k}:=f_{k}\circ f_{k-1}\circ\cdots\circ f_{1}
are
1,\ldots,1,u^{\geq k},\ldots,u^{\geq k}
with
t
copies of
1
. Here we allow
u^{\infty}:=0
, and the number of zero invariant factors is the nullity,
\mathrm{rank}\,\ker F_{k}
.

Proof.

Let
w_{1},\ldots,w_{t},v_{1},\ldots,v_{s}
denote the standard
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module basis of
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{t+s}
, where
(t+s)\times(t+s)
is the size of
f_{k}
. Thus
f_{k}(w_{i})=w_{i}+u^{\geq 1}
-terms, and
f_{k}(v_{i})=u^{\geq 1}
-terms.
We change basis on the codomain of
f_{1}
and domain of
f_{2}
: the new basis will be
f_{1}(w_{1}),\ldots,f_{1}(w_{t}),v_{1},\ldots,v_{s}
. Similarly, on the codomain of
f_{2}
and domain of
f_{3}
we use:
f_{2}(f_{1}(w_{1})),\ldots,f_{2}(f_{1}(w_{t})),v_{1},\ldots,v_{s}
, etc. Notice these are still
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module bases for
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{s+t}
, because their
u^{0}
-part is still the original standard basis
w_{1},\ldots,v_{1},\ldots
.

In these new bases, for some new matrices
B_{j}^{\prime},D_{j}^{\prime},E_{k}
over
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
, we get

(27)

f_{j}:=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}I\;&B_{j}^{\prime}u\\
0\;&D_{j}^{\prime}u\end{smallmatrix}\right),\quad\textrm{ and }\quad F_{k}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}I\;\;&E_{k}u\\
0\;\;&D_{k}^{\prime}D_{k-1}^{\prime}\cdots\cdot D_{1}^{\prime}u^{k}\end{smallmatrix}.\right)

The fact that we changed the basis on the codomain of
F_{k}
does not affect its invariant factors.
8
8

8

the Smith normal form is not affected by applying automorphisms on the domain and/or codomain.
The invariant factors of the last matrix above are obviously
1,\ldots,1,u^{\geq k},\ldots,u^{\geq k}
, as required.
∎

Remark 2.3
.

The final expression of
F_{k}
implies that after changing
v_{1},\ldots,v_{s}
by a
\mathbb{K}
-linear change of basis, we can produce a
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module basis for
\ker F_{k}
of the the form
v_{t+s-n+1},v_{t+s-n+1},\ldots,v_{t+s}
, where
n=\mathrm{rank}\,\ker F_{k}
; whereas a basis on the domain that (for some basis on the codomain) will give the non-zero invariant factors
u^{\geq k},\ldots,u^{\geq k}
can be chosen to have
u^{0}
-parts
v_{1},\ldots,v_{t+s-n}
.

2.2.
Localisation and direct limits for
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-algebras

Let
V
be a unital
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-algebra. Let
Q:V\to V
be the
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module homomorphism given by multiplication by some
Q\in V
.
Then

(28)

V\stackrel{{\scriptstyle Q}}{{\longrightarrow}}V\stackrel{{\scriptstyle Q}}{{\longrightarrow}}V\stackrel{{\scriptstyle Q}}{{\longrightarrow}}\cdots

is a directed system, and we label the copies of
V
by
V_{0},V_{1},V_{2},\ldots
, so
Q:V_{k}\to V_{k+1}.

Remark 2.4

(Motivation)
.

When
V
is equivariant quantum cohomology, we show in
Section

8

that (
28
) can arise.
In the setting of
\mathbb{K}
-vector spaces, (
28
) arose in
[
Rit14
,
Rit16
]
for quantum cohomology
V:=QH^{*}(Y)
with a (typically non-invertible) element
Q
acting by quantum multiplication. In that setting, for dimension reasons the repeated images will stabilise, i.e. for sufficiently large
k
we have
Q^{k}V=Q^{k+1}V
and

Q:Q^{k}V\to Q^{k}V
is an isomorphism, in particular
Q
-localisation
V\to V_{Q}\cong\varinjlim V_{k}\cong Q^{k}V\cong V/\ker Q^{k}
is a surjective map that quotients out the generalised
0
-eigenspace
\ker Q^{k}
of
Q
.
This surjectivity may fail for
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-algebras
V
if the images fail to stabilise.

Example:

V=\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
and
Q=u
, then
V\to\varinjlim V=\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)
is the inclusion map.

Lemma 2.5
.

The direct limit
\varinjlim V_{k}
is the localisation
V_{Q}
of
V
at
Q
. In particular, the natural map
V_{0}\to\varinjlim V_{k}
induces an isomorphism
(V_{0})_{Q}\cong\varinjlim V_{k}
where
(V_{0})_{Q}
is the localisation at
Q
.

Proof.

Formally write
\tfrac{v}{Q^{k}}
to mean
v\in V_{k}
.
In the direct limit,
v\in V_{k},v^{\prime}\in V_{k^{\prime}}
are identified if they become equal after mapping both into
V_{k^{\prime\prime}}
for large enough
k^{\prime\prime}
.
Note
Q^{k^{\prime}}v,Q^{k}v^{\prime}\in V_{k+k^{\prime}}
, so that equality condition becomes
Q^{k^{\prime\prime}-k-k^{\prime}}(Q^{k^{\prime}}v-Q^{k}v^{\prime})=0
. Localisation of
V
at
Q
is also defined by formal symbols
\tfrac{v}{Q^{k}}
subject to the equivalence
\tfrac{v}{Q^{k}}\sim\tfrac{v^{\prime}}{Q^{k^{\prime}}}\Leftrightarrow Q^{l}(Q^{k^{\prime}}v-Q^{k}v^{\prime})=0
, some
l\in\mathbb{N}
.
This shows that
V_{Q}\cong\varinjlim V_{k}
are naturally identified, and the identification map is precisely the one induced by
V_{0}\to\varinjlim V_{k}
,
v\mapsto\tfrac{v}{Q^{0}}
.
The claim follows.
∎

Lemma 2.6
.

If
Q^{k}\in uV
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
, then
u\cdot 1
is a unit, thus multiplication by
u
on
\varinjlim V_{k}
is an invertible map and
\varinjlim V_{k}
is in fact a
\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)
-module.

Proof.

By
Lemma

2.5
,
Q
is invertible in
\varinjlim V_{k}
since it is invertible in
V_{Q}
with inverse
\tfrac{1}{Q}
, where
1\in V
denotes the unit of
V
. Thus
Q^{k}
is invertible, and in particular, any factor of
Q^{k}
is invertible. Finally,
Q^{k}\in uV
means that
u\cdot 1\in V
is a factor of
Q^{k}
.
∎

2.3.
Localisation and direct limits for
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-modules

Assume now the weaker condition that
V
is a
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module of finite rank
r
. Let

(29)

V\stackrel{{\scriptstyle Q_{0}}}{{\longrightarrow}}V\stackrel{{\scriptstyle Q_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}V\stackrel{{\scriptstyle Q_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\cdots

be a sequence of
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-linear homomorphisms
Q_{k}:V_{k}\to V_{k+1}
where we label the copies of
V
by
V_{k}
.

We first look at the case when the
Q_{j}
are injective, postponing the general case to
Section

2.4
.

Remark 2.7

(Motivation)
.

In general equivariant Floer settings, (
29
) arises rather than (
28
):
the maps can keep varying due to shifts in the equivariant weights.
In the
E^{-}
-equivariant setting, we often find that all
Q_{j}
are injective and their
u^{0}
-parts are the same nilpotent map.

Example:

V=\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{2}
and
Q_{j}=\mathrm{diag}(1,u)
, then
V\to\varinjlim V=\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\oplus\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)
is the inclusion map.

Definition 2.8

(Adjugates)
.

For any
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module homomorphism
Q:V\to V
(for
V
as above), the
adjugate
of
Q
is the
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module homomorphism
\mathrm{adj}(Q):V\to V
arising as the adjoint
9
9

9

\mathrm{adj}(Q)(v_{1})\wedge v_{2}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{r}=v_{1}\wedge(\Lambda^{r-1}Q)(v_{2}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{r})=v_{1}\wedge Qv_{2}\wedge\cdots\wedge Qv_{r}.
of the exterior power
\Lambda^{r-1}Q
for the pairing
V\times\Lambda^{r-1}V\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wedge}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Lambda^{r}V\cong\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
.
After a choice of basis of
V
, the adjugate is the transpose of the cofactor matrix of
Q
.
Moreover,

(30)

Q\circ\mathrm{adj}(Q)=\text{det}Q\cdot\mathrm{id}=\mathrm{adj}(Q)\circ Q.

We recall that
\mathrm{adj}(Q_{2}\circ Q_{1})=\mathrm{adj}(Q_{1})\circ\mathrm{adj}(Q_{2})
and
\mathrm{adj}(\lambda Q)=\lambda^{r-1}\mathrm{adj}(Q).

Also,
Q
is injective precisely if
\text{det}Q\in\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{\times}
, in which case (
30
) determines
\mathrm{adj}(Q)
: the localisation
Q_{u}=Q\otimes 1:V_{u}\to V_{u}
is a linear map on the
\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)
-vector space
V_{u}=V\otimes_{\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]}\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)
, and
\mathrm{adj}(Q)=\text{det}Q\cdot Q_{u}^{-1}
.

From
(
29
), we get adjugate maps

(31)

V_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{adj}Q_{0}}}{{\longleftarrow}}V_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{adj}Q_{1}}}{{\longleftarrow}}V_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{adj}Q_{2}}}{{\longleftarrow}}\cdots

For
a\leq b
, abbreviate by
Q^{a}_{b}:V_{a}\to V_{b+1}
the map

\qquad Q^{a}_{b}:=Q_{b}\circ Q_{b-1}\circ\cdots\circ Q_{a},\quad\textrm{ whose determinant is }\quad q^{a}_{b}:=\text{det}Q^{a}_{b}=\text{det}Q_{a}\cdots\text{det}Q_{b}.

By the properties of adjugates,
\mathrm{adj}(Q^{a}_{b})=\mathrm{adj}(Q_{a})\circ\cdots\circ\mathrm{adj}(Q_{b}):V_{b+1}\to V_{a}
, and
\mathrm{adj}(Q^{a}_{b})Q^{a}_{b}=q^{a}_{b}\cdot\mathrm{id}
.

Lemma 2.9
.

When all
Q_{j}
are injective, the direct limit of (
29
) can be identified with a
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-submodule of the
u
-localisation
(V_{0})_{u}:=V_{0}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]}\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)
,

\varinjlim V_{j}\subset(V_{0})_{u}\;\textrm{ with canonical maps }\;\mathrm{adj}(Q^{0}_{j})\otimes\tfrac{1}{q^{0}_{j}}=(Q^{0}_{j})_{u}^{-1}:V_{j+1}\to(V_{0})_{u},

and canonical map
\mathrm{id}\otimes 1:V_{0}\to\varinjlim V_{j}\subset(V_{0})_{u}
.

Proof.

The direct limit of the finite sequence of injective maps
V_{0}\to\cdots\to V_{n+1}
is identifiable with
V_{n+1}
, with canonical maps
Q^{j}_{n}:V_{j}\to V_{n+1}
. We compose this with the injective map
\mathrm{adj}(Q^{0}_{n}):V_{n+1}\to V_{0}
so that we can view the direct limit inside
V_{0}
. The canonical maps from
V_{j}
become
\mathrm{adj}(Q^{0}_{n})\circ Q^{j}_{n}=q^{j}_{n}\cdot\mathrm{adj}(Q^{0}_{j-1})
. To make this independent of
n
we rescale by
(q^{0}_{n})^{-1}
at the cost of changing the target
V_{0}
to
(V_{0})_{u}
. The canonical map
V_{j}\to(V_{0})_{u}
becomes
\mathrm{adj}\,Q^{0}_{j-1}\otimes\tfrac{1}{q^{0}_{j-1}}.

This procedure is equivalent to replacing (
29
) by a sequence of inclusions

(32)

V_{0}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{incl}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\;(\tfrac{1}{q^{0}_{0}}\mathrm{adj}(Q^{0}_{0})V_{1}\subset\tfrac{1}{q^{0}_{0}}V_{0})\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{incl}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\;(\tfrac{1}{q^{0}_{1}}\mathrm{adj}(Q^{0}_{1})V_{2}\subset\tfrac{1}{q^{0}_{1}}V_{0})\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{incl}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\;\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{incl}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)\otimes_{\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]}V_{0}.\qed

Example 2.10
.

Consider (
29
) but with
\mathbb{Z}
-modules
V_{j}=\mathbb{Z}
and multiplication maps
Q_{j}=(j+1)\cdot\mathrm{id}
.
The adjugates equal
1
and

q^{0}_{n}=(n+1)!
. So

\tfrac{1}{q^{0}_{n}}\mathrm{adj}(Q^{0}_{n})=\tfrac{1}{(n+1)!}\mathrm{id}:V_{n+1}=\mathbb{Z}\to\tfrac{1}{(n+1)!}\mathbb{Z}\subset\mathbb{Q}=V_{0}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
.
The analogue of (
32
) are the inclusions

\mathbb{Z}\subset\tfrac{1}{1}\mathbb{Z}\subset\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}\subset\tfrac{1}{3!}\mathbb{Z}\subset\cdots\subset\mathbb{Q}.

The direct limit is
\mathbb{Q}
.

Definition 2.11
.

For any
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module homomorphism
Q:V\to V
(with
V
free of finite rank), we can write
Q=P_{0}+uP_{1}+\cdots
, where
P_{i}
are endomorphisms not involving
u
. We call
P_{0}
the
u^{0}
-part
of
Q
.

Lemma 2.12
.

If
Q:V\to V
is injective with nilpotent
u^{0}
-part, then
\text{det}\,Q\in u^{k}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{\times}
for a unique
k\geq 1\in\mathbb{N}
, namely
k=\nu(\text{det}\,Q)
. We call
k=\nu(\text{det}\,Q)
the
order-factor
of
Q
.

Proof.

Composing
Q
enough times we see that some
Q^{N}
has zero
u^{0}
-part. Hence
\text{det}Q^{N}\notin\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{\times}
. As
Q
is injective,
\text{det}Q\neq 0
.
The claim follows by taking
k:=\nu(\text{det}\,Q)
in the notation of
Section

2.1
.
∎

Proposition 2.13
.

Suppose that the
Q_{j}
in (
29
) are injective, and their
u^{0}
-part is a nilpotent map independent of
j
.
Then the direct limit of (
29
) is isomorphic to
u
-localisation,

\varinjlim V_{j}\cong V_{u}:=V\otimes_{\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]}\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!),

with canonical maps
\mathrm{id}\otimes 1:V_{0}\to(V_{0})_{u}
and

(Q^{0}_{j})_{u}^{-1}=u^{-(k_{0}+\cdots+k_{j})}\cdot\mathrm{unit}_{j}\cdot\mathrm{adj}(Q^{0}_{j}):V_{j+1}\to(V_{0})_{u},

where
k_{j}=\nu(\text{det}\,Q_{j})
are the order-factors, and
q^{0}_{j}=\mathrm{unit}_{j}^{-1}\cdot u^{k_{0}+\cdots+k_{j}}
for some
\mathrm{unit}_{j}\in\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{\times}
.

Proof.

Lemma

2.12
yields order-factors
k_{j}:=\nu(\text{det}\,Q_{j})
.
Define
\mathrm{unit}_{j}:=u^{\nu(q^{0}_{j})}(q^{0}_{j})^{-1}\in\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{\times}
, and note
\nu(q^{0}_{j})=\nu(\text{det}Q_{0}\cdots\text{det}Q_{j})=k_{0}+\cdots+k_{j}
.
Now apply
Lemma

2.9
to view
\varinjlim V_{j}\subset(V_{0})_{u}
, with canonical maps as in the claim. It remains to show that this inclusion is an equality.
10
10

10

The condition
\nu(\text{det}\,Q_{j})\geq 1
, without the nilpotency assumption, is not enought to guarantee equality, e.g.
Q_{j}:=\mathrm{diag}(1,u^{2})
,
V=\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{2}
,
\nu(\text{det}\,Q_{j})=2
,
\varinjlim V_{j}=\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\oplus\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)\neq\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{2}
. Assuming that the
u^{0}
-parts of the
Q_{j}
are nilpotent (but not the same nilpotent map) is not enough: e.g.
Q_{\mathrm{odd}}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
u&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)

and
Q_{\mathrm{even}}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&u\\
1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)

have product
\mathrm{diag}(1,u^{2}).

Let
P
denote the
u^{0}
-part of
Q_{j}
, so
P^{b-a+1}
is the
u^{0}
-part of
Q^{a}_{b}
. Note
P^{r}=0
since
P
is nilpotent and
V
has rank
r
(e.g. by considering the
\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)
-vector space problem for the
u
-localised map
P_{u}
, using
\dim V_{u}=r
).
It follows that
Q^{0}_{r-1},Q^{r}_{2r-1},\ldots
are divisible by
u
. Let
m:=Nr-1
, then given
v\in V_{0}
we have
Q^{0}_{m}(v)=u^{N}\widetilde{v}
for some
\widetilde{v}\in V_{Nr}
. So
\mathrm{adj}(Q^{0}_{m})(u^{N}\widetilde{v})=q^{0}_{m}v
, therefore

\tfrac{1}{q^{0}_{m}}\mathrm{adj}(Q^{0}_{m})(\widetilde{v})=u^{-N}v\in\varinjlim V_{j}\subset(V_{0})_{u}
(using the map from
Lemma

2.9
). As
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
v\in V_{0}
were arbitrary,
\varinjlim V_{j}\subset(V_{0})_{u}
is thus an equality.
∎

We continue to assume
V
is a free
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module of rank
r
, and
V_{k}=V
for
k=0,1,\ldots
. The motivation for the following algebra machinery is illustrated in the simple example in
Remark

7.6
.

Lemma 2.14
.

Let
R_{k}:V_{0}\to V_{k}
be an injective
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module homomorphism.
Let
(u^{j_{1}},\ldots,u^{j_{r}})
be the invariant factors, where
0\leq j_{1}\leq\cdots\leq j_{r}
.
Then

(R_{k})_{u}^{-1}(V_{k})\cong u^{-j_{1}}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\oplus\cdots\oplus u^{-j_{r}}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\subset\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{r}\cong(V_{0})_{u}.

Proof.

Choose a basis of
V=V_{0}=V_{k}
,
then the Smith normal form applies automorphisms to the bases of
V_{0},V_{k}
to yield
R_{k}=\mathrm{diag}(u^{j_{1}},\ldots,u^{j_{r}})
. The result the follows immediately.
∎

Definition 2.15
.

Let
W
be a
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-submodule of
\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{r}
. Extend (
23
) to tuples:
\nu:\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{r}\to\mathbb{Z}\cup\{\infty\}
,

\nu(u^{j_{1}}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{\times},\ldots,u^{j_{r}}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{\times})=\min(j_{1},\ldots,j_{r}),

and omit
j_{i}
in the
\min
when there is a zero entry in place of
u^{j_{i}}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{\times}
.
If an element in
\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{n}
has
\nu
-value
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, then the
leading part
is obtained by forgetting all
u^{>m}
-terms in each entry. Call
leading entries
those entries which become non-zero entries in the leading part.
For example,
x:=(2u^{-2}+u^{-1},5u^{-3}+u^{2})
has
m=\nu(x)=-3
, leading part
(0,5u^{-3})
, and
x_{2}
is a leading entry.

For
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, the
u^{-n}
-slice
of
W\subset\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{r}
is
W\cap u^{-n}\mathbb{K}^{r}
; its dimension is the
u^{-n}
-slice dimension
:

\dim_{-n}\,W=\dim_{\mathbb{K}}W\cap u^{-n}\mathbb{K}^{r}=\dim_{\mathbb{K}}\,\{\textrm{leading parts of all }w\in W\textrm{ with }\nu(w)=-n\}.

Equivalently, it is
\dim_{\mathbb{K}}\,(W/u^{-n+1}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{r})\cap(u^{-n}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{r}/u^{-n+1}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{r})
; and it is also the number of
\mathbb{K}
-linearly independent elements of
W\cap\mathbb{K}^{r}\subset\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{r}
which are divisible in
W
by
u^{n}
.

Note
\dim_{-n}\,W\leq r
. In view of the injective
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module action,
\dim_{-n}\,W
cannot increase as we increase
n
, so it stabilises for large
n
, and we call it
slice dimension
,

\dim_{-\infty}W:=\lim_{n\to\infty}\dim_{-n}W\in\{0,1,\ldots,r\}.

Observe that
\dim_{-\infty}W=0
is equivalent to
W\subset u^{-n}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{r}
for large enough
n
, and so it is equivalent to the condition that
W
is free (note
u^{-n}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
is free, but
\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)
is not).

Corollary 2.16
.

Given injections
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{r}\hookrightarrow W\hookrightarrow W_{u}\cong\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{r}
, let
s:=\dim_{-\infty}\,W
, then

W\cong u^{-j_{1}}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\oplus\cdots\oplus u^{-j_{r-s}}\oplus\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{s},

so that
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{r}\hookrightarrow W
becomes an isomorphism
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{r}\to\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{r}\subset u^{-j_{1}}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\oplus\cdots\oplus u^{-j_{r-s}}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\oplus\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{s}.

Proof.

Pick
e_{1},\ldots,e_{s}\in W
,
with
\nu(e_{i})=n
, whose leading parts are a basis
for all leading parts of
w\in W
with
\nu(w)=n
(here we assume
n
is sufficiently large, so that this basis exists by definition of slice dimension).
Replace
e_{i}
by
u^{n}e_{i}
, so that
e_{i}\in\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{n}
with
\nu(e_{i})=0
.
After a
\mathbb{K}
-linear automorphism of
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{n}
, we may assume that the leading parts of
e_{1},\ldots,e_{s}
are the last
s
vectors of the standard basis of
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{r}.

By quotienting
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{r}
by
0\oplus\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{s}
, and
W
by
\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{s}
, we may now assume that
s=0
.
Therefore
W
is free, and in fact
W\cong\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{r}
since the
u
-localisation of the injections in the claim give isomorphisms of
\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{r}
.
Now apply
Lemma

2.14
to the given injection
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{r}\hookrightarrow W\cong\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{r}
, which yields the invariant factors
u^{-j_{i}}
appearing in the claim.
∎

Let
W:=u^{-j_{1}}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\oplus\cdots\oplus u^{-j_{r}}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
. Abbreviate

d_{j}:=\#\{\textrm{invariant factors }u^{\leq-j}\}=\#\{i:j_{i}\geq j\},

By definition,
d_{j}\geq d_{j+1}
, and
d_{0}=r
.
We let these be the coefficients of a polynomial:

s_{W}(t)=r\cdot 1+d_{1}t+\cdots+d_{j}t^{j}+\cdots

We study such “slice polynomials” in
Theorem

2.47
. See
Example

1.16
for a picture.

Lemma 2.17
.

d_{n}=\dim_{-n}\,W
for
n\geq 0
. In particular,
\deg s_{W}=\min\{n:\dim_{-n}\,W\neq 0\}
.
Moreover,
d_{0}+d_{1}+\cdots+d_{r}=r+j_{1}+j_{2}+\cdots+j_{r}.

Proof.

Assuming
j_{1}\leq j_{2}\leq\cdots\leq j_{r}
, and letting
i
be the smallest index with
j_{i}\geq j
, we see that the tail of the standard basis
e_{j_{i}},e_{j_{i}+1},\ldots,e_{r}
of
\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{r}
, rescaled by
u^{-j_{i}}
, is a basis for the leading parts of
W
with
\nu=-j_{i}
. The first claim follows.
For the final claim,
by inspection we see that
\dim_{0}\,W+\dim_{-1}\,W+\cdots+\dim_{-j_{r}}\,W=\dim_{\mathbb{K}}W/u\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{r}=r+j_{1}+\cdots+j_{r}
.
∎

Corollary 2.18
.

Given injections
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{r}\cong W_{0}\hookrightarrow W_{1}\hookrightarrow W_{2}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{r}
, for free
W_{k}
, then

(33)

W_{k}\cong u^{-j_{1}(k)}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\oplus\cdots\oplus u^{-j_{r}(k)}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!],

so that
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{r}\hookrightarrow W_{k}
becomes an isomorphism
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{r}\to\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{r}
. The
0\leq j_{1}(k)\leq\cdots\leq j_{r}(k)
satisfy
j_{i}(k)\leq j_{i}(k+1)
. Equivalently, the coefficients of
s_{W_{k}}=\sum d_{j}(k)t^{j}
satisfy
0\leq d_{j}(k)\leq d_{j}(k+1)\leq r
.

For some
s,j_{i}
, letting
k\to\infty
we have
j_{i}(k)\to j_{i}
for
i\leq r-s
,
j_{i}(k)\to\infty
for
i\geq r-s+1
, and

\varinjlim W_{n}\cong u^{-j_{1}}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\oplus\cdots\oplus u^{-j_{r-s}}\oplus\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{s}.

Similarly,
d_{j}(k)\to d_{j}=s+\#\{i:j_{i}\geq j\}=\dim_{-j}\,\varinjlim W_{n}
, yielding a series

\lim_{k\to\infty}s_{W_{k}}=r\cdot 1+d_{1}t+d_{2}t^{2}+\cdots+st^{j_{r-s}+1}+\cdots

Proof.

This follows from the observations preceding the claim and the proof of
Corollary

2.16
.
∎

Remark 2.19
.

Abbreviate by
W^{-}
the direct limit in (
33
),
W^{\infty}:=\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{r}
, and
W^{+}:=W^{\infty}/uW^{-}
. Then
W^{+}\cong\mathbb{F}^{s}
, where
\mathbb{F}:=\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)/u\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!].

This is the prototypical example of what happens in the short exact sequence of
Corollary

6.26
.

2.4.
Non-injective homomorphisms

Let
Q:V\to W
be a
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module homomorphism between free
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-modules. Abbreviate:

v=\mathrm{rank}\,V,\qquad w=\mathrm{rank}\,W,\qquad n=\mathrm{null}\,Q=\mathrm{rank}\,\ker Q,\qquad q=v-n=\mathrm{rank}\,Q.

By taking the Smith normal form,
Q
becomes

\mathrm{diag}(u^{j_{1}},\ldots,u^{j_{q}},0,\ldots,0):\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{q}\oplus\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{n}\to\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{q}\oplus\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{w-q},

where
0\leq j_{1}\leq\cdots\leq j_{q}
,
diag refers to the main diagonal of the (possibly non-square) matrix, and
n
invariant factors
u^{\infty}:=0
arose on the diagonal because of the kernel.
We can apply the previous machinery to the injective block
\mathrm{diag}(u^{j_{1}},\ldots,u^{j_{q}}):\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{q}\to\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{q},

to deduce that

V_{u}=V\otimes_{\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]}\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)\supset\mathrm{image}(\mathrm{adj}\,Q)\otimes\tfrac{1}{j_{1}\cdots j_{q}}\cong u^{-j_{1}}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\oplus\cdots\oplus u^{-j_{q}}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!].

Unlike the injective case discussed previously, that image is not a copy of
W
, it forgets the summand

\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{w-q}\cong\mathrm{coker}\,Q/\mathrm{Torsion}(\mathrm{coker}\,Q).

Let
R_{k}=Q_{k-1}\circ\cdots\circ Q_{0}:V_{0}\to V_{k}
be the composite of the first
k
maps in (
29
) (not assuming injectivity), where
V=V_{0}=V_{k}
is free of rank
r
.
Let
(u^{j_{1}(k)},\ldots,u^{j_{r}(k)})
be the invariant factors of
R_{k}
, where
0\leq j_{1}(k)\leq\cdots\leq j_{r}(k)\leq\infty
, where we allow
u^{\infty}:=0
.
Abbreviate the map

A_{k}:=\mathrm{adj}\,R_{k}\otimes\prod_{j_{i}(k)\neq\infty}u^{-j_{i}(k)}:V_{k}\to V_{0}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]}\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)=(V_{0})_{u}.

Corollary 2.20
.

There is a
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module isomorphism

V_{k}\cong\Bigg(\bigoplus_{j_{i}(k)\neq\infty}u^{-j_{i}(k)}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\Bigg)\oplus\ker R_{k}\cong\mathrm{im}\,A_{k}\oplus\ker R_{k}\subset(V_{0})_{u}.

The exponents satisfy
j_{i}(k)\leq j_{i}(k+1)
, and

\#\{i:j_{i}(k)=\infty\}=\mathrm{null}\,R_{k}
(
=\mathrm{null}\,\varinjlim R_{k}
for
k\geq r
).

Proof.

This follows by the previous discussion, noting:
v=w=\mathrm{rank}\,V
,
n(k)=\mathrm{rank}\,\ker R_{k}
,
and
q(k)=\mathrm{rank}\,R_{k}=v-n(k)
.
The kernels of the
R_{k}
maps are nested free
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-submodules of
V
, so for sufficiently large
k
(
k\geq r
suffices), the numbers
n(k),q(k)
become independent of
k
.
∎

Remark 2.21
.

The motivation for the next result, needed in
Section

7.4
,
is that for non-free weights
(a,b)\neq(0,0)
, only one of the Floer continuation maps that define the direct limit
E^{\infty}SH^{*}(Y)
can fail to be injective, in view of

Theorem

6.21
. The geometric reason is explained in
Remark

6.14
.

Consider the free
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module

C_{k}=\mathrm{coker}\,R_{k}/\mathrm{Torsion}(\mathrm{coker}\,R_{k}).

Note that
Q_{j}
induces a natural map

\mathrm{coker}\,Q_{j-1}\to\mathrm{coker}\,Q_{j}
since
Q_{j}\circ R_{j-1}=R_{j}.

If
Q_{j}
is injective, then that natural map will send torsion to torsion, so it descends to a map on quotients:
Q_{j}^{\prime}:C_{j-1}\to C_{j}
.

Suppose that only one map
Q_{p-1}:V_{p-1}\to V_{p}
in (
29
) fails to be injective.
Then
\ker R_{k}=\ker R_{p}
for
k\geq p
.
We can make a choice of identification
C_{p}\cong\ker R_{p}
, since they are both free of rank

n:=\mathrm{rank}\,C_{p}=\mathrm{rank}\,\ker R_{p}.

This also induces an isomorphism of the
u
-localisations
(C_{p})_{u}\cong(\ker R_{p})_{u}
.

The composite
R_{k}^{\prime}:=Q_{k-1}^{\prime}\circ\cdots\circ Q_{p}^{\prime}:C_{p}\to C_{k}
for
k>p
is an injective map of free
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-modules, so we can apply the results from the previous Subsection:

C_{p}\cong\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{n},\qquad C_{k}\cong u^{-\ell_{1}(k)}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\oplus\cdots\oplus u^{-\ell_{n}(k)}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!],

arising from
\mathrm{adj}\,R_{k}^{\prime}\otimes\tfrac{1}{\text{det}R_{k}^{\prime}}:C_{k}\to(C_{p})_{u}
, where
(u^{\ell_{1}(k)},\ldots,u^{\ell_{n}(k)})
are the invariant factors of
R_{k}^{\prime}
for
k\geq p
.
In particular,
0\leq\ell_{1}(k)\leq\cdots\leq\ell_{n}(k)<\infty
, and
\ell_{i}(k)\leq\ell_{i}(k+1)
.

Combining all that with the previous discussion, we may view
V_{k}
inside
(V_{0})_{u}
as follows:

V_{k}\cong V_{k}^{\prime}:=u^{-j_{1}(k)}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\oplus\cdots\oplus u^{-j_{r-n}(k)}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\oplus u^{-\ell_{1}(k)}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\oplus\cdots\oplus u^{-\ell_{n}(k)}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\subset\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{r}\cong(V_{0})_{u},

noting that
\#\{i:j_{i}(k)=\infty\}=\mathrm{rank}\,\ker R_{k}=n
.

Corollary 2.22
.

Assume only one map
Q_{p-1}:V_{p-1}\to V_{p}
in (
29
) fails to be injective. Then the direct limit of (
29
) can be identified with

\varinjlim Q_{j}\cong\bigcup_{k\geq p}V_{k}^{\prime}\subset(V_{0})_{u}.

For
k\geq p
, the canonical map
V_{k}\to\varinjlim R_{k}
is built from two maps, described previously:

(1)

the map
A_{k}:V_{k}\to\mathrm{im}\,A_{k}\cong\bigoplus_{j_{i}(k)\neq\infty}u^{-j_{i}(k)}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
, and

(2)

the composite map
V_{k}\to\mathrm{coker}\,R_{k}\to C_{k}\to(C_{p})_{u}\cong(\ker R_{p})_{u}
, with image
\oplus u^{-\ell_{i}(k)}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
.

In particular,
\varinjlim Q_{j}
viewed inside
(V_{0})_{u}\cong\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{r}
gets identified with

u^{-j_{1}}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\oplus\cdots\oplus u^{-j_{r-n-s}}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\oplus\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{s}\oplus u^{-\ell_{1}}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\oplus\cdots\oplus u^{-\ell_{n-s^{\prime}}}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\oplus\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{s^{\prime}}\subset\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{r},

where
s
is the number of limits
j_{i}=\lim j_{i}(k)
which equal
\infty
, and
s^{\prime}
is the number of limits
\ell_{i}=\lim\ell_{i}(k)
which equal
\infty
.

Proof.

This is now immediate from the previous discussion. ∎

2.5.
Canonical valuations for
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-modules, and induced filtration

Definition 2.23
.

A
valuation

\mathrm{val}_{u}:W\to\mathbb{Z}\cup\{\infty\}
on a
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module
W
means the following:

(1)

\mathrm{val}_{u}(w)=\infty
if and only if
w
is a
u
-torsion element (
u^{k}w=0
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
);

(2)

\mathrm{val}_{u}(u^{k}w)=k+\mathrm{val}_{u}(w)
;

(3)

\mathrm{val}_{u}(w+w^{\prime})\geq\min\{\mathrm{val}_{u}(w),\mathrm{val}_{u}(w^{\prime})\}
, with equality if
\mathrm{val}_{u}(w)\neq\mathrm{val}_{u}(w^{\prime})
.

A
filtration
of a
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module
W
is a collection of
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-submodules
(F_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}
satisfying

F_{j}\supset F_{j+1}\supset uF_{j}\qquad\textrm{ and}\qquad W=\cup F_{j}.

The filtration associated to a valuation is
F_{j}(W):=\{w\in W:\mathrm{val}_{u}(w)\geq j\}.

For any
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module homomorphism
c:V\to W
, a valuation on
W

induces
a valuation on
W
via
V\to W\to\mathbb{Z}\cup\{\infty\}
, and any filtration
F_{j}(W)
on
W

induces
a filtration on
V
via

(34)

F_{j}^{c}=F_{j}^{c}(V):=c^{-1}(F_{j}(W)).

We say
c
is
filtration-preserving
if
c(F_{j}(V))\subset F_{j}(W)
, equivalently
F_{j}(V)\subset F_{j}^{c}
.

Definition 2.24
.

Let
W
be a free
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module.
The
canonical valuation and filtration
are:
11
11

11

With some abuse of notation: the convention here is that
\mathrm{div}_{u}(0)=\infty
.

(35)

\begin{split}\mathrm{div}_{u}(x)&:=\max\{n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}:x=u^{n}y\textrm{ for some }y\in W\},\\
F_{j}(W)&:=\{w\in W:\mathrm{div}_{u}(w)\geq j\}=\{w\in W:w\textrm{ is }u^{j}\textrm{-divisible}\}.\end{split}

Equivalently: let
W_{n}:=u^{n}W
, so
W=W_{0}\supset W_{1}\supset W_{2}\supset\cdots
, then
\mathrm{div}_{u}(x)=n\Leftrightarrow x\in W_{n}\setminus W_{n+1}
.

The
canonical valuation and filtration
on a finitely generated
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module
W
are induced by the quotient map
W\to W/T
, for the
u
-torsion submodule
T:=\{w\in W:u^{k}w=0\textrm{ for some }k\in\mathbb{N}\}
, using the canonical valuation and filtration on the free
12
12

12

In this step, we are using the assumption that
W
is finitely generated. E.g. this fails for
\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)
.
module
W/T
. Explicitly:

\mathrm{val}_{u}(w)\geq j\Longleftrightarrow w=u^{j}x+t\textrm{ for some }x\in W,t\in T,

and equality occurs for the “maximal” such
j
, allowing infinity:
13
13

13

It was inevitable that
T
lies inside all
F_{j}(W)
, because “the” free part of
W
is not canonical. E.g. the difference of two elements with valuation
j
could yield a torsion element.

F_{\infty}(W):=\cap_{j}F_{j}(W)=T
.

The
canonically induced valuation and filtration
on the
u
-localisation
W_{u}:=W\otimes_{\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]}\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)
are

F_{j}(W_{u})=\{x\in W_{u}:\mathrm{val}_{u}(x)\geq j\},\;\textrm{ where }\;\mathrm{val}_{u}(x):=-m+\mathrm{val}_{u}(y)\textrm{ for any lift }y\in W\textrm{ of }u^{m}x,

i.e. via localisation
W\to W_{u}
,
y\mapsto u^{m}x
(such
y
exist for sufficiently large
m\in\mathbb{N}
depending on
x
).

Example 2.25
.

For
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
, we have

\mathrm{div}_{u}(u^{k}q_{k}+\textrm{higher powers of }u)=k\in\mathbb{N}
if
q_{k}\neq 0\in\mathbb{K}
. For
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{r}
,
\mathrm{div}_{u}
is the minimum of the canonical valuations on all factors. On a free
\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)
-module there is no
canonical
valuation, since everything is
u
-divisible, but if
\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{r}
arises from
u
-localising
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{r}
(or
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{r}\oplus\{u\textrm{-torsion}\}
) then the canonical valuation on
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{r}
induces a canonical valuation on
\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{r}
for which the
u
-localisation map
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{r}\to\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{r}
is valuation-preserving: we minimise over
\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)
-summands the obvious valuations
\mathrm{div}_{u}(u^{k}q_{k}+\textrm{higher powers of }u)=k\in\mathbb{Z}
if
q_{k}\neq 0\in\mathbb{K}
.

Lemma 2.26

(Functoriality)
.

Any
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module homomorphism is filtration-preserving for the canonical filtrations. Any
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module isomorphism preserves the canonical valuation and filtration.

Proof.

If
c:V\to W
is a homomorphism of
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-modules, and
v=u^{j}x+t
for
x\in V
and a torsion element
t\in V
, then
c(v)=u^{j}c(x)+c(t)
where
c(t)\in W
is torsion, thus
\mathrm{val}_{u}(c(v))\geq\mathrm{val}_{u}(v)
, proving the first claim.
If
c
is an isomorphism, then considering
c^{-1}
yields an equality on valuations.
∎

Remark 2.27
.

Let
W
be a finitely generated
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module
W
, so (
25
) defines
k_{j}\in\mathbb{N}
.
On the free
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module
W/T\cong\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{r}
the valuation and filtration are those from
Example

2.25
; and for
W
:

\mathrm{val}_{u}(w)=-m+\max\{n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}:u^{m}w=u^{n}y\textrm{ for some }y\in W\}\textrm{ for any }m\geq\max k_{j}.

Lemma 2.28
.

For an arbitrary
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module homomorphism
c:V\to W
, the filtration on
V
induced by the canonical filtration on
W
satisfies

(36)

\begin{split}F_{j}^{c}:=c^{-1}(F_{j}(W&))=\{v\in V:\mathrm{val}_{u}(c(v))\geq j\}\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{for }j\geq 0}}{{=}}\{v\in V:c(v)\textrm{ is }u^{j}\textrm{-divisible modulo torsion}\}\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{for }j\geq 0}}{{=}}\ker\left(V\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\longrightarrow}}W/(T+u^{j}W)\right).\end{split}

In particular,
F_{0}^{c}=F_{j\leq 0}^{c}=V
and
F_{\infty}^{c}=c^{-1}(T)=\ker(c:V\to W/T)
.

Proof.

This is immediate from unpacking Definitions
2.23
and
2.24
.
∎

Example 2.29
.

For the multiplication
c:\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u^{3}}}{{\to}}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
,

F_{j}^{c}:=\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
for
j\leq 3
, and
F_{j}^{c}=u^{j-3}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
for
j\geq 3.
The power
3
is detected a posteriori by the quotients
F^{c}_{j+1}/uF^{c}_{j}
, using the notation (
25
),

\ldots,\;\;\,F^{c}_{2}/uF^{c}_{1}=T_{1},\;\;\,F^{c}_{3}/uF^{c}_{2}=T_{1},\;\;\,F^{c}_{4}/uF^{c}_{3}=0,\;\;\,F^{c}_{5}/uF^{c}_{4}=0,\;\;\,\ldots

Note
d_{j}:=\dim_{\mathbb{K}}F_{j}^{c}/F_{j+1}^{c}
satisfy
d_{j\leq 3}=1
and
d_{j\geq 4}=0
.

Lemma 2.30
.

For any
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module homomorphism
c:\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{s}\to\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{r}
, the values

d_{j}:=\dim_{\mathbb{K}}F^{c}_{j}/uF^{c}_{j-1}
detect both the rank and the invariant factors of the
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module
\mathrm{coker}\,c
.

Proof.

We may
14
14

14

after changing bases on
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{s},\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{r}
, using automorphism-invariance of valuations/filtrations from
Lemma

2.26
.
assume
c
is in Smith normal form:
c=\mathrm{diag}(u^{j_{1}},\ldots,u^{j_{a}},0,\ldots,0)
for
j_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
f_{1},\ldots,f_{s}
be the standard basis for
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{s}
. Then
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{s}\cong\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{a}\oplus\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{b}
where
c^{-1}(0)=\mathrm{span}_{\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]}(f_{a+1},\ldots,f_{s})\cong\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{b}
,
b=s-a
.
So
F^{c}_{j}\supset c^{-1}(0)
and, in the notation (
25
),

(37)

F^{c}_{j}/uF^{c}_{j-1}=\;\;\Bigg(\;\;\bigoplus_{i\leq a\textrm{ with }j\leq j_{i}}\!T_{1}f_{i}\;\;\Bigg)\;\oplus\;T_{1}^{b},

where
T_{1}f_{i}\cong T_{1}
. For
j\gg 0
, (
37
) has rank
b
, so it detects
a=s-b
and
r-a=\mathrm{rank}_{\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]}\mathrm{coker}\,c
:

\mathrm{coker}\,c\cong T_{j_{1}}\oplus\cdots\oplus T_{j_{a}}\oplus\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{r-a}.

Finally, notice that the number of invariant factors
u^{k}
of
\mathrm{coker}\,c
arising in the list
u^{j_{1}},\ldots,u^{j_{a}}
is precisely the drop in
\dim_{\mathbb{K}}F^{c}_{j}/uF^{c}_{j-1}
when going from
j=k
to
j=k+1
(cf.
Example

2.29
).
∎

2.6.
Multiplicative properties

In this Subsection, we temporarily assume that our
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-modules are
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-algebras over the ring
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
, for some product
\bullet
.

Remark 2.31
.

The motivation is that
E^{-}QH^{*}(Y)
and
E^{-}SH^{*}(Y)
admit a unital product structure if we make
S^{1}
act only on the target manifold (not on the free loopspace).

A filtration is
multiplicative
if
F_{i}(W)\bullet F_{j}(W)\subset F_{i+j}(W)
, and the valuation is multiplicative if
\mathrm{val}_{u}(x\bullet y)\geq\mathrm{val}_{u}(x)+\mathrm{val}_{u}(y)
. If the filtration is multiplicative and
F_{0}(W)=W
, then
W\bullet F_{j}(W)=F_{0}(W)\bullet F_{j}(W)\subset F_{j}(W)
, so the
F_{j}(W)\subset W
are ideals.
Note
T=F_{\infty}(W)\subset W
is always an ideal.

Lemma 2.32
.

The canonical valuation and filtration on any
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-algebra
W
is multiplicative, and
yields ideals
F_{j}(W)\subset W
.

Proof.

For
t,s\in T
,
(u^{j}x+t)\bullet(u^{i}y+s)\in u^{j+i}x\bullet y+T

as
T
is an ideal. So the canonical valuation is multiplicative, hence the filtration is too. As
F_{0}(W)=W
, the
F_{j}(W)
are ideals.
∎

Corollary 2.33
.

Let
c:V\to W
be any
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-algebra homomorphism. For any multiplicative filtration on
W
, the filtration
F_{j}^{c}
on
V
is multiplicative. If in addition
F_{0}^{c}=V
, then the
F_{j}^{c}\subset V
are ideals. All these assumptions hold if we use the canonical filtrations on
V,W
.

Proof.

If
v\in F_{j}^{c},w\in F_{i}^{c}
then
v\bullet w\in F_{j+i}^{c}
since
c(v\bullet w)=c(v)\bullet c(w)\in F_{j}(W)\bullet F_{i}(W)\subset F_{j+i}(W).

∎

2.7.
Canonical valuation on cohomology

Let
(C^{*},d)
be a cochain complex of free
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-modules.
Choose a
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module basis for
C^{*}
, and take its
\mathbb{K}
-linear span
D^{*}\subset C^{*}
. Then
C^{*}\cong D^{*}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
as a
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module (not as a complex).
The differential
d
on
C^{*}
is a
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module homomorphism, so

d=\delta_{0}\otimes\mathrm{id}+\delta_{1}\otimes u\cdot\mathrm{id}+\delta_{2}\otimes u^{2}\cdot\mathrm{id}+\cdots

for some
\delta_{j}:D^{*}\to D^{*+1-j|u|}
, in particular
\delta_{0}:D^{*}\to D^{*+1}
is a cochain differential. More concretely, the basis determines identifications
C^{*}\cong\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{n}
,
D^{*}\cong\mathbb{K}^{n}\subset\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{n}
, and we simplify notation to:

(38)

d=\delta_{0}+u\delta_{1}+u^{2}\delta_{2}+\cdots:C^{*}\to C^{*+1},

where
\delta_{j}:C^{*}\to C^{*+1-j|u|}
is a
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-linear homomorphism whose matrix does not involve
u
.

Assume that
15
15

15

It would suffice to work degree-wise, and assume finite rank degree-wise. Most arguments generalise even further.

\mathrm{rk}\,C^{*}<\infty
. Let
\mathbf{chval}_{u}
be the canonical valuation on
C^{*}
.
The homology
W:=H(C^{*})
is a finitely generated
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module. We endow
W
with the canonical valuation and filtration.

Lemma 2.34
.

\ker d\subset C^{*}
is a free
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-submodule of rank
\leq\mathrm{rk}\,C^{*}
, such that the inclusion
\ker d\subset C^{*}
respects the canonical valuations.
16
16

16

The second claim is not a general property of submodules, e.g. consider
u\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\subset\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
.

Proof.

The first part of the statement is a general fact about submodules of free modules when working over PIDs. Secondly, if
x\in\ker d
were of the form
x=u^{k}y\in C^{*}
for
k\geq 1
, then
0=d(x)=u^{k}d(y)
forces
d(y)=0
since
C^{*}
is
u
-torsion-free, so
y\in\ker d
. The claim follows.
∎

Corollary 2.35
.

For any
w\in W=H(C^{*})
, the valuation maximises chain representative valuations

\mathrm{val}_{u}(w)=\max\{\mathrm{chval}_{u}(x):w=[x]\in W\}.

Proof.

We give a very explicit proof, as we will reuse these ideas later.
By the automorphism-invariance of the valuation in
Lemma

2.26
, we may consider the Smith normal form of the
m\times n
matrix
17
17

17

See

3.1
for a simple illustration.
In general, we use

2.34
to see that

and that the canonical valuation on

is the one inherited from
C^{*}
.

(39)

A:\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{n}\cong C^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}\ker d\cong\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{m}.

Say
18

, for some

.
Let

T\cong T_{k_{1}}\oplus\cdots\oplus T_{k_{m-r}}\qquad\textrm{ and }\qquad W\cong T\oplus\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{r},

to ignoring that

The claim follows.

Lemma 2.36

We can identify

.

2.8.
Filtration on cohomology induced by a chain map

Suppose
c:B^{*}\to C^{*}
is a (
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-linear) chain map defined on a free
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module
B^{*}
of finite rank, inducing

c:V:=H(B^{*})\to W:=H(C^{*})

on homology.
Assume
V
is free. So there is no
u
-torsion in
V
, but
W
may have
u
-torsion:
T\subset W
.

Remark 2.37
.

The motivation comes from the homomorphism
c
from (a suitable model of) equivariant quantum cohomology to equivariant Hamiltonian Floer cohomology for a given slope value
\lambda>0
.

Theorem 2.38
.

There are
f_{1},\ldots,f_{a+b}\in F_{0}^{c}(V)
giving summand-preserving isomorphisms

V\cong(\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]f_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]f_{a})\oplus(\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]f_{a+1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]f_{a+b})\cong\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{a}\oplus c^{-1}(T)\cong\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{a}\oplus\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{b},

and the submodule
F_{j}^{c}\subset V
is the following, when viewed inside the above summand notation,

F_{j}^{c}\cong(\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]u^{\phi(j-j_{1})}f_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]u^{\phi(j-j_{a})}f_{a})\oplus\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{b},

where
\phi(n)=\max\{n,0\}
and
j_{i}\geq 0
. Thus the
d_{j}:=\dim_{\mathbb{K}}F_{j}^{c}/uF_{j-1}^{c}
define a sequence which decreases from
d_{0}=d_{j\leq 0}=a+b
down to
d_{j>\max j_{i}}=b
, satisfying
20
20

20

The invariant factors
u^{j}
of the homomorphism
c
are those arising in the Smith normal form, so
j\geq 0
as
u^{0}=1
may arise, whilst the invariant factors of the f.g.
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module
\mathrm{coker}(c:V\to W)
are precisely the
u^{j}
with
j>0
.

\displaystyle d_{j}

\displaystyle=b+\#\{i:j\leq j_{i}\}\leq b+a=\mathrm{rank}_{\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]}V,

\displaystyle d_{j}-d_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\#\{i:j=j_{i}\}=\#\{\textrm{invariant factors }u^{j}\textrm{ of }c:V\to W\},\;\textrm{and }{\textstyle\sum}(d_{j}-d_{j+1})=a.

If the
u
-localisation
c_{u}:V_{u}\to W_{u}
is an isomorphism, then
b=0
.

Proof.

Submodules of a free module over a PID of rank
k
are free and of rank at most
k
. We apply that to the
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-submodule
c^{-1}(T)=c^{-1}(F_{\infty}(W))=\cap c^{-1}(F_{j}(W))=\cap F_{j}^{c}(V)\subset V
. Then we reduce to
Lemma

2.30
by considering the induced map
\widetilde{c}:\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{a}\cong V/c^{-1}(T)\hookrightarrow W/T\cong\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{r}
. The
j_{i}\geq 0
in the claim arise from the invariant factors in the Smith normal form
\mathrm{diag}(u^{j_{1}},\ldots,u^{j_{a}})
of
\widetilde{c}
.
The final claim about localisation is immediate as, compatibly with the above summand notation,

(41)

c_{u}:V_{u}\cong\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{a}\oplus c_{u}^{-1}(0)\cong\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{a}\oplus\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{b}\to\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{r}\oplus 0\cong W_{u}.\qed

2.9.
Finitely
u
-filtered
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-modules

Let
V
be a
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module, and
T\subset V
its torsion submodule.

Definition 2.39
.

Say that
V
is
finitely
u
-filtered
, if

V=F_{0}\supset F_{1}\supset F_{2}\supset\cdots

has a filtration by
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-submodules
F_{j}

satisfying:

(1)

P:=V/uV
is finite dimensional as a
\mathbb{K}
-vector space.

(2)

uF_{j}=F_{j+1}\cap uV
(a strengthening of the condition
uF_{j}\subset F_{j+1}
).

We abbreviate by

\mathrm{ev}_{0}:V\to P

the
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module homomorphism induced by quotienting
V
by
uV
.

Remark 2.40
.

If
V
is a free
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module of finite rank, then
V=\oplus\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]x_{j}
after a choice of basis
x_{j}\in V
, and we can identify
P:=\oplus\mathbb{K}x_{j}
and view
\mathrm{ev}_{0}
as evaluation at
u=0
. A change of basis for
V
induces a
\mathbb{K}
-linear change of basis on
P
.

Lemma 2.41
.

If
V
is finitely
u
-filtered, then
\mathrm{ev}_{0}
induces
\mathbb{K}
-linear embeddings
F_{j+1}/uF_{j}\hookrightarrow P
, yielding finite invariants
\dim_{\mathbb{K}}(F_{j+1}/uF_{j})\leq\dim_{\mathbb{K}}P
.

Proof.

The restriction
\mathrm{ev}_{0}:F_{j+1}\to P
has kernel
uV\cap F_{j+1}=uF_{j}
by the assumptions. ∎

Proposition 2.42
.

Let
c:V\to W
be a homomorphism of
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-modules, where
V
is finitely generated. Endow
W
with the canonical filtration. Then
V
is finitely
u
-filtered by
F_{j}^{c}
.

Proof.

By assumption, there is a surjection
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{N}\to V
for some
N\in\mathbb{N}
. It induces a surjection
u\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{N}\to uV
, so

P:=V/uV
has
\dim_{\mathbb{K}}P\leq N<\infty
. We now show

uF_{j}^{c}=uV\cap F_{j+1}^{c}
.
Note
uF_{j}^{c}\subset uV\cap F_{j+1}^{c}
as
c(uF_{j}^{c})=uc(F_{j}^{c})
. Conversely, suppose
uv\in uV\cap F_{j+1}^{c}
. Then
u^{j+1}w=c(uv)=uc(v)
for some
w\in W
. So
t:=c(v)-u^{j}w\in W
satisfies
ut=0
. Since
t
is a torsion element,
t\in F_{i}(W)
for all
i
. As
u^{j}w\in F_{j}(W)
, also
c(v)=u^{j}w+t\in F_{j}(W)
. Hence
v\in F_{j}^{c}
, which proves
uV\cap F_{j+1}^{c}\subset uF_{j}^{c}
.
∎

Lemma 2.43
.

If
V
is finitely
u
-filtered, and
T\subset F_{j}
for all
j
, then
u^{k}F_{j-k}=F_{j}\cap u^{k}V
for all
21
21

21

j-k\leq 0
is not problematic, put
F_{\leq 0}:=F_{0}
. The result also holds for
F_{j}
labelled by
\mathbb{Z}
, without assuming
V=F_{0}
.

j,k\in\mathbb{N}
. E.g. this applies to canonical filtrations and to
F_{j}^{c}
in
Proposition

2.42
.

Proof.

Note
u^{k}F_{j-k}\subset F_{j}\cap u^{k}V
, so we check the reverse inclusion. We explain the case
k=2
, as the general proof is then clear. Using self-explanatory notation, say
u^{2}v=f_{j}
. Then
u^{2}v\in uV\cap F_{j}=uF_{j-1}
, so
u^{2}v=uf_{j-1}.
Thus
uv-f_{j-1}\in T
. By assumption,
T\subset F_{j-1}
. So redefine
f_{j-1}
so that
uv=f_{j-1}
. Then
uv\in uV\cap F_{j-1}=uF_{j-2}
. So
uv=uf_{j-2}
. Thus
u^{2}v=u^{2}f_{j-2}\in u^{2}F_{j-2}
as required.
∎

2.10.
Associated graded for finitely
u
-filtered modules

The associated graded determined by a filtration on
W
is

(42)

\mathrm{gr}(W):=T\oplus\bigoplus_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}F_{j}(W)/F_{j+1}(W).

If the filtration is multiplicative, in the sense of
Section

2.6
, then this will be a graded ring, for the natural induced product (the
j
-summand times the
i
-summand lands in the
(j+i)
-summand; the torsion submodule
T
is preserved). In general, it is just a
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module.

Proposition 2.44
.

Let
V
be a finitely
u
-filtered
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module. Abbreviate
F_{j}:=F_{j}(V)
. Then there is a natural filtration on
P:=V/uV
given by

F_{j}(P):=F_{j}/uF_{j-1}=F_{j}/(uV\cap F_{j}),\textrm{ and }F_{\infty}(P):=T/uT,

where
d_{j}:=\dim_{\mathbb{K}}F_{j}(P)<\infty
,
whose associated graded as a
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module is naturally

\mathrm{gr}(P)\cong T/uT\oplus\bigoplus_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}F_{j}/(F_{j+1}+uF_{j-1}).

If the filtration
F_{j}
is multiplicative, then so is
F_{j}(P)
, and the above is a ring isomorphism.

Proof.

V
is filtered as
\cdots\supset F_{j}\supset F_{j+1}\supset\cdots
(with
T
inside all
F_{j}
).
Thus
P
is filtered as
\cdots\supset F_{j}/(uV\cap F_{j})\supset F_{j+1}/(uV\cap F_{j+1})\supset\cdots
(with
T/uT
inside all of those).
By definition of finitely
u
-filtered,
uV\cap F_{j+1}=uF_{j}
, so
F_{j+1}/(uV\cap F_{j+1})=F_{j+1}/uF_{j}
.
The summands of the associated graded of that filtration on
P
are therefore
T/uT
and the quotients

(F_{j}/uF_{j-1})/(F_{j+1}/uF_{j})\cong(F_{j}/uF_{j-1})/[(F_{j+1}+uF_{j-1})/uF_{j-1}]\cong F_{j}/(F_{j+1}+uF_{j-1}),

using the “second” and “third” isomorphism theorems respectively (this also holds for rings).
∎

Definition 2.45
.

The
slice series
of a finitely
u
-filtered
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module
V
is a formal series,

s_{V}(t):=\sum_{j\geq 0}\dim_{\mathbb{K}}(F_{j}(V)/uF_{j-1}(V))\;t^{j}=\sum_{j\geq 0}d_{j}t^{j}.

When we use the
F_{j}^{c}
filtration from
Proposition

2.42
for
c:V\to W
, we write
s_{c}:=s_{V}
.

For general filtrations on
V
, one may wish to keep track also of some or all of the terms
d_{j}t^{j}
for
j<0
. However, for the canonical filtration on
V
, or for
F_{j}^{c}(V)
for a
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module homomorphism
c:V\to W
and the canonical filtration on
W
, we have
d_{j}=d_{0}
for
j\leq 0
.

Example 2.46
.

In
Example

2.29
,
s_{c}(t)=1+t+t^{2}+t^{3}.

See also
Lemma

2.17
for an example.

Theorem 2.47
.

For
c:V\to W
as before, using the notation from
Theorem

2.38
,

(43)

s_{c}(t)=(a+b)\cdot 1+\sum_{1\leq j\leq\max j_{i}}(b+\#\{i:j\leq j_{i}\})t^{j}\;\;+t^{b}+t^{b}+\cdots

(1)

The constant term
s_{c}(0)=\mathrm{rank}_{\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]}V=\dim_{\mathbb{K}}P,
where
P:=V/uV.

(2)

The
t^{j}
-coefficient
d_{j}
is the number of invariant factors
u^{\leq j}
of
c
, so
d_{j}\leq d_{j+1}
.

(3)

b=\mathrm{rank}_{\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]}\ker c
.

Definition 2.48
.

We also associate to
c:V\to W
a
reduced slice polynomial

(44)

\widetilde{s}_{c}(t):=\sum_{j\geq 0}\dim_{\mathbb{K}}(F_{j}(V)/(uF_{j-1}(V)+\ker c))\;t^{j}=\sum_{j\geq 0}(d_{j}-b)t^{j}=a\cdot 1+\sum_{1\leq j\leq\max j_{i}}\#\{i:j\leq j_{i}\}t^{j}.

If
c
is injective, then
s_{c}=\widetilde{s}_{c}
, and we call it
slice polynomial
.

2.11.
Filtrations arising from
u
-local isomorphisms

Let
V
be a free
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module of finite rank.
Suppose
W=\varinjlim W_{\lambda}
is a direct limit of
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-modules, where
\lambda
is labelled by an unbounded subset of
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, whose directed system is compatible with
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module homomorphisms
c_{\lambda}:V\to W_{\lambda}
. Let
c:=\varinjlim c_{\lambda}:V\to W
be their direct limit.
We use the canonical filtrations on
V
and
W_{\lambda}
.
Abbreviate:

\displaystyle F_{j}^{\lambda}

\displaystyle:=F_{j}^{c_{\lambda}}=c_{\lambda}^{-1}(F_{j}(W_{\lambda})),\hskip 21.52771ptP_{j}^{\lambda}:=F_{j}^{\lambda}/uF_{j-1}^{\lambda},\hskip 21.52771ptd_{j}^{\lambda}:=\dim_{\mathbb{K}}P_{j}^{\lambda},

\displaystyle F_{j}^{\infty}

\displaystyle:=F_{j}^{c}=c^{-1}(F_{j}(W)),\hskip 26.69434ptP_{j}^{\infty}:=F_{j}^{\infty}/uF_{j-1}^{\infty},\hskip 15.93048ptd_{j}^{\infty}:=\dim_{\mathbb{K}}P_{j}^{\infty}.

Note that
F_{\infty}^{\lambda}=c_{\lambda}^{-1}(T(W_{\lambda}))
and
F_{\infty}^{\infty}=c^{-1}(T(W))
, where
T(\cdots)
means taking the torsion submodule.

Remark 2.49
.

The motivation is the canonical equivariant map
E^{-}c_{\lambda}^{*}:E^{-}QH^{*}(Y)\to E^{-}HF^{*}(H_{\lambda})
, where equivariant formality will ensure that
E^{-}QH^{*}(Y)
is a free
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module of finite rank.

Lemma 2.50
.

F_{j}^{\lambda}\subset F_{j}^{\mu}\subset V
for all
\lambda\leq\mu
, and those inclusions induce embeddings

P_{j}^{\lambda}\hookrightarrow P_{j}^{\mu}\hookrightarrow P:=V/uV,

so in particular
d_{j}^{\lambda}\leq d_{j}^{\mu}\leq\dim_{\mathbb{K}}P
.

Proof.

By compatibility, the composite
V\to W_{\lambda}\to W_{\mu}
equals
V\to W_{\mu}
.
By
Lemma

2.26
,
F_{j}(W_{\lambda})\to F_{j}(W_{\mu})
via
W_{\lambda}\to W_{\mu}
.
Thus, via that composite,
F_{j}^{\lambda}\to F_{j}(W_{\lambda})\to F_{j}(W_{\mu})
, so
F_{j}^{\lambda}\subset F_{j}^{\mu}
. By
Lemma

2.41
, we may embed
P_{j}^{\lambda}\hookrightarrow P
, so viewed inside
P
we have
P_{j}^{\lambda}\subset P_{j}^{\mu}\subset P
because
F_{j}^{\lambda}\subset F_{j}^{\mu}
.
∎

2.12.
The injectivity property, and the Young diagrams

Proposition 2.51

(Injectivity Property)
.

Suppose the
u
-localisation of
c
is an isomorphism. Then

c:V\to W
and all
c_{\lambda}:V\to W_{\lambda}
are injective.

Proof.

Recall
V_{u}=V\otimes_{\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]}\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)
denotes localisation at
u
.
Consider the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{\lambda}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{u}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(W_{\lambda})_{u}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(W)_{u}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{\lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\otimes 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varinjlim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\otimes 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\otimes 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{\lambda}\otimes\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c\otimes\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varinjlim\otimes\mathrm{id}}

where the left vertical arrow is injective as
V
is a free
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module; and the bottom composite horizontal arrow is an isomorphism by the hypothesis.
So the top horizontal composite is injective.
∎

In applications,
c_{\lambda}
will be an isomorphism for small
\lambda>0
; there is only a discrete sequence of slopes
0=\lambda_{0}<\lambda_{1}<\lambda_{2}<\cdots\to\infty
for which
c_{\lambda},W_{\lambda}
are not defined;
W_{\lambda}\to W_{\mu}
in the directed system is an isomorphism if
\lambda,\mu\in(\lambda_{k},\lambda_{k+1})
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
; and
Proposition

2.51
applies. Abusively write
d_{j}^{\lambda_{k}}
to mean
d_{j}^{\lambda}
for
\lambda_{k}<\lambda<\lambda_{k+1}
.
Abbreviate
a=\mathrm{rank}_{\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]}V
.
We obtain partitions

\begin{split}D^{\lambda_{1}}&:=d_{1}^{\lambda_{1}}+d_{2}^{\lambda_{1}}+\cdots\\
\cdots\\
D^{\lambda_{k}}&:=d_{1}^{\lambda_{k}}+d_{2}^{\lambda_{k}}+\cdots\end{split}

for a non-decreasing sequence
0=D^{\lambda_{0}}\leq D^{\lambda_{1}}\leq D^{\lambda_{2}}\leq\cdots
.

As in
Example

1.16
,
the
D^{\lambda_{k}}
-partition yields a
Young diagram
of shape
(d_{1}^{\lambda_{k}},d_{2}^{\lambda_{k}},\ldots)
, so row-lengths
d_{1}^{\lambda_{k}}\geq d_{2}^{\lambda_{k}}\geq\cdots\geq 0
where we ignore the tail of zeros at the end.
Let’s call
y_{k}
the number of columns in the Young diagram for
D^{\lambda_{k}}
. Swapping rows/columns defines the
dual Young diagram
(in French notation), with
y_{k}
rows of lengths, say,
j_{a-y_{k}+1}^{\lambda_{k}}\leq\cdots\leq j_{a-1}^{\lambda_{k}}\leq j_{a}
. These are the
j_{i}
indices that determine the invariant factors
u^{j_{i}}
; the missing indices are
j_{1}^{\lambda_{k}}=\cdots=j_{a-y_{k}}^{\lambda_{k}}=0
.

Lemma

4.19
will imply that the sum of the first
j
row-lengths of the Young diagram is:

d_{1}^{\lambda_{k}}+d_{2}^{\lambda_{k}}+\cdots+d_{j}^{\lambda_{k}}=\dim_{\mathbb{K}}F_{j}^{\lambda_{k}}/u^{j}V,

which stabilises for
j
large enough, and
Corollary

4.20
will describe the total size:

D^{\lambda_{k}}=d_{1}^{\lambda_{k}}+d_{2}^{\lambda_{k}}+\cdots=j_{1}^{\lambda_{k}}+\cdots+j_{a}^{\lambda_{k}}.

By
Lemma

2.50
,
d_{i}^{\lambda_{k}}\leq d_{i}^{\lambda_{k+1}}
, therefore the
Young diagrams are contained inside each other as we increase
k
. Therefore the dual Young diagrams are contained inside each other as well (in this case, due to the French notation, when
y_{k}
increases, new rows can be inserted at the top); this also follows from the definition of the invariant factors
u^{j_{i}}
: the
j_{i}
cannot decrease in the slope parameter, so
j_{i}^{\lambda_{k}}\leq j_{i}^{\lambda_{k+1}}
.
In each case, we get a directed path in Hasse’s diagram of Young’s lattice. We call these the
dimension lattice for
V
and the
invariant factors lattice for
V
.

3.
Algebra II: the models
V^{-}
,
V^{\infty}
, and
V^{+}

3.1.
The
W^{-},W^{\infty},W^{+}
models

Continuing with
C^{*}
as in
Section

2.7
, consider the
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-modules

(45)

\begin{split}W^{-}&:=W=H(C^{*}),\\
W^{\infty}&:=H(C^{*}_{u})\cong H(C^{*}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]}\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!))\cong W^{-}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]}\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)\cong W_{u}^{-},\\
W^{+}&:=H(C^{+})\qquad\textrm{ where }\quad C^{+}:=C^{*}_{u}/uC^{*}\cong C^{*}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]}\mathbb{F}\quad\textrm{ and }\quad\mathbb{F}:=\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)/u\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!].\end{split}

Example 3.1
.

For
d=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&0\\
u^{3}&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
on
C^{*}=\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\oplus\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
,
\ker d=0\oplus\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
and
\mathrm{im}\,d=0\oplus u^{3}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
.
Then (
39
) is
the row matrix
A=\left(u^{3}\,\;0\right):\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\oplus\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\to\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
. We get
W^{-}=T\cong T_{3}
and
W^{\infty}=0
. For
C^{+}
, we get
\ker d=\textrm{span}_{k}\{u^{-2},u^{-1},u^{0}\}\oplus\mathbb{F}
and
\mathrm{im}\,d=0\oplus\mathbb{F}
, so
W^{+}=\textrm{span}_{k}\{u^{-2},u^{-1},u^{0}\}\cong T_{3}
.

3.2.
The long and short exact sequences

In what follows, we keep track of gradings as if they were
\mathbb{Z}
-gradings, placing
u
in degree
|u|=2
, and
W^{-}[-2]
shifts gradings so that
(W^{-}[-2])_{m}=W^{-}_{m-2}
and
u\otimes 1:W^{-}[-2]\to W^{\infty}
is grading-preserving.

Lemma 3.2
.

There is a long exact sequence

(46)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{-}[-2]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{\infty}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{+}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{-}[-1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u\otimes 1}
connecting
map

The connecting map
W^{+}\to W^{-}[-1]
is formally
u^{-1}d
where
d
is the
C^{*}_{u}
-differential at chain level.

Explicitly, the LES (
46
) decomposes (non-canonically) into the exact sequence

(47)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{+}\cong T[-1]\oplus\mathbb{F}^{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T[-1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subset}
project

where the outside terms are the summands in (
40
), and
\mathbb{F}^{r}\cong\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{r}/u\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{r}
is
\mathrm{coker}\,(u\otimes 1)
.

In particular, if
W
is free then (
46
) is a short exact sequence
0\to W^{-}[-2]\to W^{\infty}\to W^{+}\to 0
.

Proof.

The short exact sequence
0\to\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u}}{{\to}}\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)\to\mathbb{F}\to 0
tensored with the free
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module
C^{*}
yields a short exact sequence of cochain complexes, which induces the LES in (
46
).

For
c\in C^{+}
,
c
is a cycle if and only if
d(c)\in uC^{*-1}
, meaning it lies in the image of
u:C^{*}\to C^{*}_{u}
. The connecting map is by definition the
C^{*-1}
-factor of that, i.e. formally
u^{-1}d(c)
.

The second claim essentially follows from
Remark

2.27
. We first describe
T
and the connecting map more explicitly. Let
x=q_{0}+uq_{1}+u^{2}q_{2}+\cdots
with
q_{j}\in\mathbb{K}
. If
x\in C^{*}
is a cycle whose homology class is
u^{k}
-torsion (for
k\geq 0
), then
u^{k}(q_{0}+uq_{1}+\cdots)=d(b_{0}+ub_{1}+\cdots)
for some
b_{j}\in\mathbb{K}
.
That exactness is detected earlier by
W^{\infty}
since
x=d(u^{-k}b_{0}+u^{1-k}b_{1}+\cdots)
in
C^{*}_{u}
. Via the
u^{-1}d
connecting map
W^{+}\to T[-1]\subset W^{-}[-1]
we have

u^{1-k}b_{0}+u^{2-k}b_{1}+\cdots\mapsto x
.
We can describe
W^{+}\to W^{-}[-1]
more explicitly. Recall the matrix expression of the chain differential in the proof of
Corollary

2.35
:
d=\mathrm{diag}(u^{k_{1}},\ldots,u^{k_{m-r}},0,\ldots,0):\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{n}\to\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{m}
.
By considering the induced map
d:\mathbb{F}^{n}\to\mathbb{F}^{m}
, we see that the last
r
coordinates yield an
\mathbb{F}^{r}\cong\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{r}/u\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{r}
summand in
W^{+}
as in the claim, and it remains to discuss the first
n-r
coordinates.
By the proof of
Corollary

2.35
,

d(e_{i})=u^{k_{i}}\widetilde{e}_{i}
yields a
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]/(u^{k_{i}})
-summand for
W^{+}
generated by
u^{-k_{i}+1}e_{i}
.
The connecting map
u^{-1}d
maps
u^{-k_{i}+1}e_{i}
to
\widetilde{e}_{i}\in T
, which is the generator of the
T_{k_{i}}
-summand in (
40
) for
W^{-}=W
.
∎

Lemma 3.3
.

The
\mathbb{F}^{r}
-summand in (
47
) is canonically identifiable with the
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-submodule

(48)

W^{+}_{\mathbb{F}}:=\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}u^{n}W^{+}=\mathrm{im}(W^{\infty}\to W^{+})\subset W^{+},

and (
46
) yields the short exact sequence
0\to(W^{-}/T)[-2]\to W^{\infty}\to W^{+}_{\mathbb{F}}\to 0.

Proof.

Note
u:\mathbb{F}\to\mathbb{F}
is surjective, so
\mathbb{F}^{r}\subset T[-1]\oplus\mathbb{F}^{r}
can be canonically recovered as the collection of elements that admit
u^{n}
-preimages for arbitrary
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
∎

Remark 3.4
.

Note that
W^{+}
is entirely
u
-torsion, in particular there is no interesting valuation on
W^{+}
.
Unlike
W^{+}_{\mathbb{F}}
, the
T[-1]
-summand in
W^{+}
has no intrinsic description: it involves choices.
22
22

22

One could consider a splitting of
0\to\ker d\to C^{*}\to\mathrm{im}\,d\to 0
by lifting a basis from
\mathrm{im}\,d
to produce a free submodule
D^{*}\subset C^{*}
complementary to
\ker d
, and finally require
T[-1]
to have its representatives in
D^{*}
.
This would allow us to define a filtration on
W^{+}
by declaring this choice of
T[-1]
to lie inside
F_{j}(W^{+})
for all
j\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Let
W_{\lambda}=H^{*}(C^{*}_{\lambda})
, labelled by an unbounded subset of
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, together with chain maps
c_{\mu,\lambda}:C^{*}_{\lambda}\to C^{*}_{\mu}
for
\lambda<\mu
so that the induced maps
W_{\lambda}\to W_{\mu}
are compatible under composition. Abbreviate
W:=\varinjlim W_{\lambda}
.
Let
T,T_{\lambda}
denote the torsion of
W,W_{\lambda}
respectively.
By the construction in
Section

3.1
, using the obvious notation, we obtain
W=W^{-}=\varinjlim W^{-}_{\lambda}
,
W^{\infty}=\varinjlim W^{\infty}_{\lambda}
, and
W^{+}=\varinjlim W^{+}_{\lambda}.

Corollary 3.5
.

The torsion satisfies
T=\varinjlim T_{\lambda}
.
There is a long exact sequence

(49)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{-}[-2]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{\infty}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{+}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{-}[-1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u\otimes 1}
connecting
map

and a short exact sequence

(50)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{-}[-2]/T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{\infty}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varinjlim W^{+}_{\lambda,\mathbb{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u\otimes 1}

where
W^{+}_{\lambda,\mathbb{F}}:=\cap u^{-n}(W^{+}_{\lambda})
, and

\varinjlim W^{+}_{\lambda,\mathbb{F}}\subset W^{+}_{\mathbb{F}}=\cap u^{-n}(W^{+})
, which is not necessarily an equality.

Proof.

If
t\in T
, then
rt=0
for some
r\neq 0\in\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
. Say
w_{\lambda}\in W_{\lambda}
represents
t
(some
\lambda
).
Then
rw_{\lambda}
represents
rt=0
, so
rw_{\mu}=0
for some
\mu\geq\lambda
, where
w_{\mu}:=c_{\mu,\lambda}(w_{\lambda})=0
via some map
c_{\mu,\lambda}:W_{\lambda}\to W_{\mu}
of the directed system. Thus,
t
is represented by
w_{\mu}\in T_{\mu}.

Conversely, if
w_{\mu}\in T_{\mu}
, then
rw_{\mu}=0
for some
r\neq 0\in\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
, thus
rt=0
for the class
t\in T
represented by
w_{\mu}.

Thus,
T=\varinjlim T_{\lambda}
.

The long exact sequence follows by taking the direct limit of (
46
).

The short exact sequence follows from (
47
), using that
T=\varinjlim T_{\lambda}.

∎

Remark 3.6
.

The directed system
W_{n}=\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]/u^{n}
with multiplication maps
u:W_{n}\to W_{n+1}
will yield
W=\varinjlim W_{n}=\mathbb{F}
, with
W_{\mathbb{F}}=W
even though
W_{n,\mathbb{F}}=0
, in particular
\varinjlim W_{n,\mathbb{F}}\subsetneq W_{\mathbb{F}}
.

3.3.
The
u
-adic filtration, and the
W^{+}
-spectral sequence

Recall
C^{*}
is a free
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module with a differential
d=\delta_{0}+u^{>0}
of the form (
38
).
The
ordinary complex
is the quotient chain complex

\mathrm{ord}\,C:=C^{*}/uC^{*}\textrm{ with differential }\mathrm{ord}\,d:=[\delta_{0}]\textrm{ and cohomology }\mathrm{ord}\,W:=H(\mathrm{ord}\,C,\mathrm{ord}\,d).

We call
\mathrm{ord}\,W
the
ordinary cohomology
, as opposed to
W:=H(C^{*},d)
.

Lemma 3.7
.

There is a natural long exact sequence

\cdots\to\mathrm{ord}\,W[-1]\to W[-2]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u}}{{\to}}W\to\mathrm{ord}\,W\to\cdots

Proof.

This uses
[d]=[\delta_{0}]:C^{*}/uC^{*}\to C^{*}/uC^{*}
, and that
0\to C^{*}[-2]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u}}{{\to}}C^{*}\to C^{*}/uC^{*}\to 0
is a short exact sequence (using that
C^{*}
is free).
∎

We use the notation
D^{*}\subset C^{*}
from
Section

2.7
, so
C^{*}=D^{*}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
as
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-modules (not as chain complexes).
The chain complex
(D^{*},\delta_{0})
yields a canonical chain isomorphism
D^{*}\to\mathrm{ord}\,C^{*}
(induced by the quotient map
C^{*}\to C^{*}/uC^{*}=\mathrm{ord}\,C^{*}
). So
H^{*}(D^{*},\delta_{0})\cong\mathrm{ord}\,W
.
Two such choices
D_{1}^{*},D_{2}^{*}
of
D^{*}
are related by a
\mathbb{K}
-linear chain isomorphism
\psi_{0}:D_{1}^{*}\to D_{2}^{*}
compatible with the canonical isomorphisms to
\mathrm{ord}\,C^{*}
, where
\psi_{0}
is the
u^{0}
-part of the relevant change of basis isomorphism
\psi:C^{*}\to C^{*}
,
\psi=\psi_{0}+u\psi_{1}+u^{2}\psi_{2}+\cdots
, where
\psi_{j}
are
\mathbb{K}
-linear maps that preserve the order of
u
-factors.

Lemma 3.8
.

The
u
-adic filtration induces a convergent spectral sequence of
\mathbb{K}
-vector spaces

(51)

E_{1}^{pq}=\left\{\begin{smallmatrix}\displaystyle H^{q-p}(D^{*},\delta_{0})\otimes u^{p},&\displaystyle\textrm{ if }p\leq 0\\
\displaystyle 0,&\displaystyle\textrm{ if }p>0\end{smallmatrix}\right.\Longrightarrow W^{+}:=H^{*}(C^{+},d).

In particular,
\mathrm{ord}\,W=0
implies
W^{+}=0
. (The analogous statement often fails for
W^{-}
and
W^{\infty}
)

Proof.

By the same observation as in
[
Sei08
, Sec.(8b)]
, the
u
-adic filtration on
C^{+}
(which as a
\mathbb{K}
-vector space is identifiable with
\oplus_{i\leq 0}D^{*}u^{i}
) is bounded below and exhausting, so the associated spectral sequence on cohomology converges to the cohomology of
C^{+}
.
∎

3.4.
Specialisation to ordinary cohomology

Recall
C^{-}:=C^{*}
,
C^{+}:=C^{*}_{u}/uC^{*}_{u}
.
We have two chain maps, a quotient map and a subcomplex inclusion:

\mathrm{ev}_{0}:C^{-}\to\mathrm{ord}\,C\qquad\textrm{ and }\qquad\mathrm{inc}:\mathrm{ord}\,C\to C^{+},

induced respectively by the
quotient
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\to\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]/u\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\cong\mathbb{K}
,
and the inclusion of the
u^{0}
-part
\mathbb{K}\cong\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]/u\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\hookrightarrow\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)/u\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]=\mathbb{F}
. Via
Lemma

3.7
, and identifying
C^{*}/uC^{*}\cong D^{*}\cong\oplus\mathbb{K}\subset\oplus\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\cong C^{*}
, the map
\mathrm{ev}_{0}
can be thought of as an evaluation at
u=0
map
\oplus\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\to\oplus\mathbb{K}.

.

Let

and

.
Surjectivity of the top

, using that

Corollary 3.10
.

\mathrm{ord}\,W=0\Leftrightarrow W^{+}=0
(so also
\mathrm{ord}\,W\neq 0\Leftrightarrow W^{+}\neq 0
).

Proof.

If
\mathrm{ord}\,W=0
,

Lemma

3.8
implies
W^{+}=0
.
If
W^{+}=0
,
Lemma

3.9
implies
\mathrm{ord}\,W=0
.
∎

4.
Algebra III: filtrations on
V^{-}
,
V^{\infty}
, and
V^{+}

4.1.
Filtration of the exact sequence

Filter
W^{-},W^{\infty}
by the canonical filtrations (
Definition

2.24
).

Definition 4.1
.

The
\mathbb{F}
-filtration
on
W^{+}_{\mathbb{F}}
is the filtration

(52)

F_{j}(W^{+}_{\mathbb{F}}):=\ker(u^{|j|+1}:W^{+}_{\mathbb{F}}\to W^{+}_{\mathbb{F}})\;\textrm{ for }\,j\leq 0,\quad\textrm{ and }\quad F_{j}(W^{+}_{\mathbb{F}})=\{0\}\;\textrm{ for }\,j\geq 1.

Lemma 4.2
.

uF_{j}(W^{+}_{\mathbb{F}})=F_{j+1}(W^{+}_{\mathbb{F}})\subset F_{j}(W^{+}_{\mathbb{F}})
.

Proof.

The second inclusion is obvious.
The inclusion
uF_{j}(W^{+}_{\mathbb{F}})\subset F_{j+1}(W^{+}_{\mathbb{F}})
is immediate.
The first equality holds for
j=0
:
F_{0}(W^{+}_{\mathbb{F}})=\ker u
, so
uF_{0}(W^{+}_{\mathbb{F}})=0=F_{1}(W^{+}_{\mathbb{F}})
.
Now suppose
j\leq-1
and
w\in F_{j+1}(W^{+}_{\mathbb{F}})
, so
u^{|j|}w=0
. As
u:W^{+}_{\mathbb{F}}\to W^{+}_{\mathbb{F}}
is surjective, we may write
w=uw^{\prime}
. Thus
u^{|j|+1}w^{\prime}=0
, so
w^{\prime}\in F_{j}(W^{+}_{\mathbb{F}})
, so
w=uw^{\prime}\in uF_{j}(W^{+}_{\mathbb{F}})
, as required.
∎

Lemma 4.3

(Functoriality)
.

Any
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module homomorphism
\psi:W_{1}^{+}\to W_{2}^{+}
preserves
\mathbb{F}
-filtrations:
\psi(F_{-j}(W_{1,\mathbb{F}}^{+}))\subset F_{-j}(W_{2,\mathbb{F}}^{+}).
This is an equality when
\psi_{\mathbb{F}}:\psi:W_{1,\mathbb{F}}^{+}\to W_{2,\mathbb{F}}^{+}
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

This is the analogue of
Lemma

2.26
, and it is straightforward.
∎

To simplify the notation, we will from now on omit the grading shifts from the previous section.

Lemma 4.4
.

Equation

46
induces
a filtered short exact sequence: for all
j\in\mathbb{Z}
,

(53)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{j-1}(W^{-})/T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{j}(W^{\infty})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{j}(W^{+}_{\mathbb{F}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u\otimes 1}

After making explicit choices of bases as in
Lemma

3.2
, this becomes explicitly:

(54)

\displaystyle\textrm{For }j\geq 1:\qquad

\displaystyle 0\longrightarrow u^{j-1}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{r}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u}}{{\longrightarrow}}u^{j}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{r}\longrightarrow 0\longrightarrow 0.

(55)

\displaystyle\textrm{For }-j\leq 0:\qquad

\displaystyle 0\longrightarrow\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{r}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u}}{{\longrightarrow}}u^{-j}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{r}\longrightarrow u^{-j}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{r}/u\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{r}\longrightarrow 0.

Proof.

These claims follow immediately from the proof of
Lemma

3.2
.
∎

4.2.
Exact sequence for induced filtrations

Continue with the notation from
Section

2.8

and
Section

. So for some

, we have
V^{-}=V\cong\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{s}
,
V^{\infty}=V_{u}\cong\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{s}
, and
V^{+}\cong\mathbb{F}^{s}.

Corollary 4.5
.

We have
commutative diagrams of
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-modules, with exact horizontal rows,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{-}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{\infty}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{+}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{+}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{+}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u\otimes\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u\otimes\mathrm{id}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u\otimes\mathrm{id}}

Proof.

This follows by

and

.
∎

Remark 4.6
.

The motivation comes from the three models of constructing
S^{1}
-equivariant cohomology. The top row will be the equivariant quantum cohomologies, the lower row is part of the long exact sequence for the three models of equivariant Hamiltonian Floer cohomology for some slope
\lambda>0
.

Lemma 4.7
.

[c_{u}]:V^{+}\to W^{+}
lands in
W^{+}_{\mathbb{F}}
. Explicitly,
W^{+}\cong T[-1]\oplus\mathbb{F}^{r}
,
\mathrm{image}\,[c_{u}]\subset 0\oplus\mathbb{F}^{r}
, and
\ker[c_{u}]
contains a canonical submodule
K_{c}^{+}:=(\ker c)_{u}/u\ker c\cong\mathbb{F}^{b}

(
and
b=\mathrm{rank}_{\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]}\ker c
, see (
41
)
)
.

Proof.

Since
V^{+}\cong\mathbb{F}^{s}
, the elements of
V^{+}
have arbitrary
u
-preimages: for
v\in V^{+}
,
v\in u^{n}V^{+}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}.
Thus
c(v)\in u^{n}W^{+}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
The rest follows easily, noting
\ker c\cong\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{b}
.
∎

Definition 4.8
.

The filtrations
in
Definition

2.24
and (
52
) induce filtrations on
V^{-},V^{\infty}
, as in (
36
):

F_{j}(V^{-}):=c^{-1}(F_{j}(W^{-}))\qquad\textrm{ and }\qquad F_{j}(V^{\infty}):=c_{u}^{-1}(F_{j}(W^{\infty})).

For
V^{+}
we define
F_{j}(V^{+}):=[c_{u}]^{-1}(F_{j}(W^{+}_{\mathbb{F}}))
for
j\leq 1
. For
j\geq 1
this would constantly give
F_{1}(V^{+})=\ker[c_{u}]
, which would ruin later results when
j\geq 2
. The definition, adjusted for
j\geq 2
, is:

F_{j\leq 0}(V^{+}):=[c_{u}]^{-1}(F_{j}(W^{+}_{\mathbb{F}}))=\ker(u^{|j|+1}[c_{u}])\qquad\textrm{ and }\qquad F_{j\geq 0}(V^{+}):=u^{j-1}\ker[c_{u}].

Example 4.9
.

Suppose
c:=u^{3}:V^{-}=\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\to\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]=W^{-}
,
V^{\infty}=W^{\infty}=\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)
,
V^{+}=W^{+}=\mathbb{F}
.

\small\begin{array}[]{|c|c|c|c|c|}\hline\cr&j\leq 0&1\leq j\leq 3&j>3\\
\hline\cr F_{j-1}(V^{-})&\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]&\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]&\;u^{j-1-3}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\\
