7.4.
Growth rate of the slice series (non-free weight case)

Consider the case discussed in
Remark

6.14
:
(a,b)\neq(0,0)
which is not free, so
p=a/b
causes contributions from Morse-Bott manifolds
B_{p,\beta}
at slope
p
which can cause
E^{\infty}c^{*}:E^{\infty}QH^{*}(Y)\to E^{\infty}SH^{*}(Y)
to fail being injective and/or surjective. We apply the machinery from
Section

2.4
.
Only one of the continuation maps

E^{-}QH^{*}(Y)[2(k-1)\mu]\cong E^{-}HF^{*}(H_{(k-1)^{+}})\to E^{-}HF^{*}(H_{k^{+}})\cong E^{-}QH^{*}(Y)[2k\mu]

could fail to be injective: the one for
k=\wp:=\lfloor p\rfloor
, and it plays the role of the map
Q_{p}
in
Corollary

2.22
(in addition, we will see grading shifts of
2k\mu=2\wp\mu
appearing below because of the grading shifts of the
E^{-}QH^{*}(Y)
above). We deduce from
Corollary

2.22
that, for
k\geq p
,

\begin{split}&E^{-}HF^{*}(H_{k^{+}})\cong\left(u^{-j_{1}(k)}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\oplus\cdots\oplus u^{-j_{r-n}(k)}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\right)\oplus\left(u^{-\ell_{1}(k)}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\oplus\cdots\oplus u^{-\ell_{n}(k)}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\right)[2\wp\mu],\\
&E^{-}\!SH^{*}(Y)\!\cong\!\left(u^{-j_{1}}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\!\oplus\!\cdots\!\oplus\!u^{-j_{r-n-s}}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\!\oplus\!\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{s}\right)\!\oplus\!\Big(u^{-\ell_{1}}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\!\oplus\!\cdots\!\oplus\!u^{-\ell_{n-s^{\prime}}}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\!\oplus\!\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{s^{\prime}}\Big)[2\wp\mu],\\
&E^{\infty}SH^{*}(Y)\cong E^{-}SH^{*}(Y)_{u}\cong\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{r-n}\oplus\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{n}[2\wp\mu]\cong\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{r},\end{split}

where
r=\mathrm{rank}\,E^{-}QH^{*}(Y)=\dim_{\mathbb{K}}H^{*}(Y)
, and
n=\mathrm{rank}\ker E^{-}c_{p^{+}}^{*}=\dim_{\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)}\ker E^{\infty}c^{*}
.

Viewing the above presentation of
E^{-}HF^{*}(H_{k^{+}})
as embedded in
\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{r-n}\oplus\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{n}
, there is an identification
E^{-}QH^{*}(Y)\cong\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{r-n}\oplus\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{n}
so that
E^{-}c_{k^{+}}^{*}:E^{-}QH^{*}(Y)\to E^{-}HF^{*}(H_{k^{+}})
becomes

(92)

\mathrm{incl}\oplus 0:\;\;\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{r-n}\oplus\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{n}\;\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{incl}\oplus 0}}{{\longrightarrow}}\;\;\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r-n}u^{-j_{i}(k)}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\oplus\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}u^{-\ell_{i}(k)}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!][2\wp\mu]\;\;\subset\;\;\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{r-n}\oplus\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{n}.

Localising at
u
, we get that
E^{\infty}c^{*}:E^{\infty}QH^{*}(Y)\to E^{\infty}SH^{*}(Y)
gets identified with the map

\mathrm{id}\oplus 0:\;\;\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{r-n}\oplus\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{n}\;\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{id}\oplus 0}}{{\longrightarrow}}\;\;\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{r-n}\oplus\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{n}\cong E^{\infty}SH^{*}(Y).

By
Theorem

6.25
,
E^{+}SH^{*}(Y)
is the cokernel of the map
u\otimes 1:E^{-}SH^{*}(Y)\to E^{\infty}SH^{*}(Y)
, so

E^{+}SH^{*}(Y)\cong\mathbb{F}[2j_{1}]\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathbb{F}[2j_{r-n-s}]\oplus\mathbb{F}[2\ell_{1}+2\wp\mu]\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathbb{F}[2\ell_{n-s^{\prime}}+2\wp\mu],

and the map
E^{+}c^{*}:E^{+}QH^{*}(Y)\to E^{+}SH^{*}(Y)
becomes the obvious one,
30
30

30

it vanishes on the
\mathbb{F}^{s}
and
\mathbb{F}^{s^{\prime}}
summands, and the “obvious map”
\mathbb{F}\to\mathbb{F}[2m]
is the one sending
u^{-m+1},\ldots,u^{0}
to zero.

after we use the induced identification
E^{+}QH^{*}(Y)=E^{\infty}QH^{*}(Y)/uE^{-}QH^{*}(Y)\cong\mathbb{F}^{r-n-s}\oplus\mathbb{F}^{s}\oplus\mathbb{F}^{n-s^{\prime}}\oplus\mathbb{F}^{s^{\prime}}
.

See
Example

1.11
for an explicit example (
Y=\mathbb{C}^{n}
with
(a,b)=(1,1)
).

The slice series (see
Theorem

2.47
) for the map
E^{-}c_{k^{+}}^{*}
can be read off from (
92
):

s_{k}(t)=r\cdot 1\;\;+\;\;\sum_{j=1}^{j_{r-n}(k)}(n+\#\{i:j\leq j_{i}(k)\})\,t^{j}+\quad t^{n}+t^{n}+\cdots

7.5.
Dependence of the slice series on the
(a,b)
-weight

Denote by
s_{\lambda}^{(a,b)}=\sum d_{j}^{(a,b)}(\lambda)\,t^{j}
the slice series for
c_{\lambda}^{(a,b)}:E^{-}_{(a,b)}QH^{*}(Y)\to E^{-}_{(a,b)}HF^{*}(H_{\lambda})
(
Theorem

2.47
), where we now keep track of
(a,b)
in the notation. By (
115
), we obtain a commutative diagram:

(93)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{-}_{(a,b)}QH^{*}(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{-}_{(a,b)}HF^{*}(H_{1^{+}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{-}_{(a,b)}HF^{*}(H_{\lambda})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{-}_{(a-b,b)}QH^{*}(Y)[2\mu]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{-}_{(a-b,b)}HF^{*}(H_{\lambda-1})[2\mu]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{\lambda}^{(a,b)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1^{+}}^{(a,b)}}
continuation
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{\lambda-1}^{(a-b,b)}}

This diagram establishes constraints about how much
s_{\lambda}^{(a,b)}
can differ from
s_{\lambda-1}^{(a-b,b)}
.
There is no clean statement, because the invariant factors of a composition of two maps are not determined by the invariant factors of the individual maps.
Using the
j_{i}(k)
-notation from
Section

7.3
,

j_{i}^{(a,b)}(\lambda)=j_{i}^{(a-b,b)}(\lambda-1)+\gamma_{i},\qquad\textrm{ where }\qquad\alpha:=j_{1}^{(a,b)}(1)\leq\gamma_{i}\leq j_{r}^{(a,b)}(1):=\beta,

in words: the least & biggest invariant factors of
c_{1^{+}}^{(a,b)}
give crude bounds on the invariant factors of the top composite horizonal map.
It follows that
d_{\alpha+j}^{(a,b)}(\lambda)\geq d_{j}^{(a-b,b)}(\lambda-1)
and
d_{\beta+j}^{(a,b)}(\lambda)\leq d_{j}^{(a-b,b)}(\lambda-1)
.

In the case
b=0
, the notation simplifies as all weights in play become
(a,0)
, and we will see in
Section

8
that for
\lambda=k^{+}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the map
c_{k^{+}}
is the
k
-fold product by a certain class
EQ
. The crude estimate above says that the change in indices is bounded
\alpha\leq j_{i}(k+1)-j_{i}(k)\leq\beta
in terms of the lowest and largest indices for the map given by multiplication by
EQ
.

7.6.
Seidel isomorphisms and rotation maps are compatible with the filtration

Corollary 7.7
.

The Seidel isomorphisms in
Theorem

A.1
preserve filtrations, e.g. (
114
) yields

\mathcal{S}\left(F_{j}(E_{(a,b)}^{\diamondsuit}HF^{*}(H_{\lambda}))\right)=F_{j}(E_{(a-b,b)}^{\diamondsuit}HF^{*}(H_{\lambda-1})).

Proof.

This follows by
Lemma

2.26
and
Lemma

4.3
, since
\mathcal{S}:=\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}
is a
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module isomorphism.
∎

Recall from
Corollary

7.7
the filtration
\mathscr{F}_{j,(a,b)}^{\diamondsuit,\lambda}\subset E^{\diamondsuit}_{(a,b)}QH^{*}(Y)
.
Recall the maps (
72
) and (
84
):

(94)

\begin{split}r_{a,b}&:=\mathcal{S}\circ c_{1^{+}}:E_{(a,b)}^{\diamondsuit}QH^{*}(Y)\to E_{(a-b,b)}^{\diamondsuit}QH^{*}(Y)[2\mu],\\
ER_{k}&:=\mathcal{S}^{k}\circ c_{k^{+}}=r_{a-(k-1)b,b}\circ\cdots\circ r_{a,b}:E^{-}_{(a,b)}QH^{*}(Y)\to E^{-}_{(a-kb,b)}QH^{*}(Y)[2k\mu].\end{split}

Proposition 7.8
.

The map
r_{a,b}
and the rotation map
ER_{k}
satisfy

r_{a,b}^{-1}\left(\mathscr{F}_{j,(a-b,b)}^{\diamondsuit,p-1}\right)=(\mathscr{F}_{j,(a,b)}^{\diamondsuit,p})[-2\mu]\qquad\textrm{ and }\qquad ER_{k}^{-1}\left(\mathscr{F}_{j,(a-kb,b)}^{\diamondsuit,p-k}\right)=\mathscr{F}_{j,(a,b)}^{\diamondsuit,p}[-2k\mu],

in particular

r_{a,b}:\mathscr{F}_{j,(a,b)}^{\diamondsuit,p}\to\mathscr{F}_{j,(a-b,b)}^{\diamondsuit,p-1}[2\mu]\qquad\textrm{ and }\qquad ER_{k}:\mathscr{F}_{j,(a,b)}^{\diamondsuit,p}\to\mathscr{F}_{j,(a-kb,b)}^{\diamondsuit,p-k}[2k\mu].

Proof.

Consider the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\diamondsuit}_{(a,b)}QH^{*}(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\diamondsuit}_{(a,b)}HF^{*}(H_{k^{+}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\diamondsuit}_{(a,b)}HF^{*}(H_{p^{+}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{-}_{(a-kb,b)}QH^{*}(Y)[2k\mu]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\diamondsuit}_{(a-kb,b)}HF^{*}(H_{(p-k)^{+}})[2k\mu]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{k^{+}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces ER_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{p^{+}}}
continuation
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}^{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}^{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{(p-k)^{+}}}

where the top three maps commute because continuation maps are compatible on cohomology;
the triangle commutes by definition of
ER_{k}
; and the square commutes by (
115
).
This diagram preserves filtrations in the following sense:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{F}_{j,(a,b)}^{\diamondsuit,p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}^{-k}(\mathscr{F}_{j,(a-kb,b)}^{\diamondsuit,p-k}[2k\mu])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{j}(E^{\diamondsuit}_{(a,b)}HF^{*}(H_{p^{+}}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{F}_{j,(a-kb,b)}^{\diamondsuit,p-k}[2k\mu]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{j}(E^{\diamondsuit}_{(a-kb,b)}HF^{*}(H_{(p-k)^{+}}))[2k\mu]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{k^{+}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces ER_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{p^{+}}}
continuation
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}^{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}^{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{(p-k)^{+}}}

where we start in the bottom-left corner and move anti-clockwise; in the right-vertical arrow we use
Corollary

7.7
applied
k
times; and we use the definition
(c_{p^{+}})^{-1}(F_{j}(E^{\diamondsuit}_{(a,b)}HF^{*}(H_{p^{+}})))=\mathscr{F}_{j,(a,b)}^{\diamondsuit,p}
to deduce the top-left entry, proving the second claim. The first claim is the case
k=1
.
∎

7.7.
The minimal classes
x_{k}
generated by the unit

The unit
x_{0}\in QH^{0}_{\varphi^{a}}(Y)=E^{-}_{(a,b)}QH^{0}(Y)
was described in
Corollary

6.2
.
Although there is such an
x_{0}\in QH^{0}_{\varphi^{a}}(Y)\cong E^{-}HF^{0}_{(a,b)}(H_{0^{+}})
for each weight
(a,b)
, they have the same representative at chain level and they all map to the unit
1\in QH^{0}(Y)
via the specialisation map

\mathrm{ev}_{0}
from
Lemma

6.3
.

We now apply the Seidel isomorphisms from the Appendix to define the

minimal classes

x_{k}
,

(95)

x_{k}:=\mathcal{S}^{-k}(x_{0})\in E^{-}HF^{*}_{(a,b)}(H_{k^{+}})[2k\mu],

by repeatedly using (
114
): each
\mathcal{S}:=\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}
changes
(a,b)
, so that
x_{0}
actually involved weight
(a-kb,b)
.

If
c_{1}(Y)=0
, then we have a
\mathbb{Z}
-grading on Floer cohomologies and the Novikov field
\mathbb{K}
lies in degree zero,
so
x_{0},x_{k}
can be characterised uniquely, up to
\mathbb{K}^{\times}
-rescaling, as the minimal degree classes of
E^{-}HF^{*}_{(a,b)}(H_{0^{+}})
and
E^{-}HF^{*}_{(a,b)}(H_{k^{+}})[2k\mu]
respectively, in degrees
|x_{0}|=0
,
|x_{k}|=-2k\mu.

Recall that the sequence of Hamiltonians
H_{k^{+}}
can be built so that the continuation maps are inclusions at chain level (the non-equivariant argument in
[
RŽ24
]
holds essentially verbatim for the equivariant case). So we may abusively write
x_{j}\in E^{-}HF^{*}_{(a,b)}(H_{k^{+}})
for
k\geq j
, when we actually mean that we applied to
x_{j}
the continuation map going from
H_{j^{+}}
to
H_{k^{+}}
.

Lemma 7.9
.

For
x_{j}\in E^{-}HF^{*}_{(a,b)}(H_{k^{+}})
we have
x_{j+1}=\mathcal{S}^{-1}(x_{j})\in E^{-}HF^{*}_{(a+b,b)}(H_{(k+1)^{+}})
.

Proof.

By construction,
\mathcal{S}^{-1}x_{j}=x_{j+1}
if we view
x_{j}\in E^{-}HF^{*}_{(a,b)}(H_{j^{+}})
.
Now apply (
115
), for
\lambda=j
and
\lambda^{\prime}=k-j
, to get the equation
\mathcal{S}^{-1}x_{j}=x_{j+1}

when viewing
x_{j}\in E^{-}HF^{*}_{(a,b)}(H_{k^{+}})
.
∎

Corollary 7.10
.

If
x_{0}\in\mathscr{F}_{j,(a,b)}^{p}
,
then
x_{k}\in\mathscr{F}_{j,(a+kb,b)}^{p+k}
.
In particular, if
c_{1}(Y)=0
and
b=0
, then

(96)

x_{0}\in\mathscr{F}_{\mu k,(a,b)}^{k}\Longrightarrow x_{0}\in\mathscr{F}_{\mu Nk,(a,b)}^{Nk}\;\textrm{ for all }N\geq 1\in\mathbb{N},

so in words: if
x_{0}
minimises the valuation at slope
k^{+}
then it continues to do so at slope
(Nk)^{+}
.

If we just assume
c_{1}(Y)=0
, then

(97)

x_{0}\in\mathscr{F}_{\mu k,(a,b)}^{k}\cap\mathscr{F}_{\mu k,(a-kb,b)}^{k}\cap\cdots\cap\mathscr{F}_{\mu k,(a-Nkb,b)}^{k}\Longrightarrow x_{0}\in\mathscr{F}_{\mu Nk,(a,b)}^{Nk}.

Proof.

Corollary

7.7
implies the first claim, by applying
\mathcal{S}^{-k}
.
For the second claim, we can exploit the characterisation of
x_{0},x_{k}
as grading-minimisers since
c_{1}(Y)=0
. The assumption implies
x_{0}\in\mathbb{K}^{\times}\cdot u^{\mu k}x_{k}\subset E^{-}_{(a,0)}HF^{*}(H_{k^{+}})
.
Thus
x_{j}\in\mathbb{K}^{\times}\cdot u^{\mu k}x_{k+j}\subset E^{-}_{(a+kb,b)}HF^{*}(H_{(k+j)^{+}})
by applying
\mathcal{S}^{-j}
.
For
j=k
we get
x_{k}=\mathrm{unit}\cdot u^{\mu k}x_{2k}
. When
b=0
, this
x_{k}
and the original
x_{k}
coincide, as the weight
(a,0)
agrees.
So
x_{0}\in\mathbb{K}^{\times}\cdot u^{\mu k}x_{k}\subset\mathbb{K}^{\times}\cdot u^{2\mu k}x_{2k}
. Now repeat the argument for
j=2k
,
j=3k
, etc.
When
b\neq 0
, we adjust the argument: we use the assumption

x_{0}\in\mathscr{F}_{\mu k,(a-mkb,b)}^{k}
to ensure that the inductive equation
x_{mk}=\mathrm{unit}\cdot u^{\mu k}x_{(m+1)k}
holds for weight
(a,b).

∎

8.
Product structure in equivariant Floer theory for weights
(a,0)

8.1.
The lack of an equivariant Floer product in general

For weights
(a,b)
with
b\neq 0
there is an obstruction to constructing a pair-of-pants product in equivariant Floer theory. At the domain level one would need an
S^{1}
-action on a three-punctured sphere which near the three punctures acts with winding number
b,b,-b
respectively, which is impossible for homotopy reasons unless
b=0
. The weaker requirement of a
QH^{*}_{S^{1}}(Y)
-module structure on equivariant Floer cohomology is also obstructed for
b\neq 0
: it requires a non-trivial
S^{1}
-action on a twice-punctured sphere with an
S^{1}
-fixed marked point.

8.2.
The Floer theory product in the weight
(a,0)
case

Proposition 8.1
.

For the (free) weight
(a,b)=(a,0),

the models
\diamondsuit\in\{-,\infty\}
admit a unital pair-of-pants product, compatibly with equivariant quantum product, and additive in grading:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\diamondsuit}QH^{*}(Y)\otimes E^{\diamondsuit}QH^{*}(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\diamondsuit}QH^{*}(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\diamondsuit}QH^{*}(Y)^{\otimes 2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\diamondsuit}QH^{*}(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\diamondsuit}HF^{*}(H_{\lambda_{1}})\otimes E^{\diamondsuit}HF^{*}(H_{\lambda_{2}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\diamondsuit}HF^{*}(H_{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\diamondsuit}SH^{*}(Y)^{\otimes 2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\diamondsuit}SH^{*}(Y),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{\lambda_{1}}\otimes c_{\lambda_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c\otimes c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}

where the diagram on the right is the direct limit of the diagrams on the left.

E^{\diamondsuit}HF^{*}(H_{\lambda})
is an
E^{\diamondsuit}QH^{*}(Y)
-module, and
c^{*}_{\lambda}:E^{\diamondsuit}QH^{*}(Y)\to E^{\diamondsuit}HF^{*}(H_{\lambda})
is an
E^{\diamondsuit}QH^{*}(Y)
-module homomorphism.
The direct limit of
c^{*}_{\lambda}(1)\in E^{\diamondsuit}HF^{*}(H_{\lambda})
is the multiplicative unit in
E^{\diamondsuit}SH^{*}(Y)
.

The diagram in
Corollary

5.2
is compatible with the above ring structures.

The specialisation diagram in
Lemma

6.3
respects the ring structures.

Proof.

This is the equivariant analogue of the constructions from
[
Rit13
]
, and the proof is essentially the same up to the additional bookkeeping of the auxiliary equivariant data defining the complexes.

For the second claim, we take
\lambda_{1}=0^{+}
so that
E^{\diamondsuit}HF^{*}(H_{\lambda_{1}})\cong E^{\diamondsuit}QH^{*}(Y)
, and we take
\lambda_{2}=\lambda
(and it is understood that we work with generic slopes
\lambda>0
).

The third claim follows by observing the following detail about the construction for the three models.
Analogously to (
38
),
the equivariant pair-of-pants product at chain level is a
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-bilinear map

\mu^{2}(\cdot,\cdot)=\mu^{2}_{0}+u\mu^{2}_{1}+u^{2}\mu^{2}_{2}+\cdots

for
\mathbb{K}
-bilinear maps
\mu^{2}_{j}
not involving
u
, where
\mu^{2}_{0}
is the non-equivariant pair-of-pants product.
As usual, this is constructed first on the original chain complex
C^{-}:=C^{*}
, then it is
u
-localised to a map on the chain complex
C^{\infty}:=C^{*}_{u}
, and finally it descends to the chain complex
C^{+}:=C^{*}_{u}/uC^{*}_{u}.

The fourth claim follows by noting that the equivariant quantum product in (
64
)
is
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-bilinear by construction, and the equivariant
c^{*}
-map is
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-linear and has an expansion like (
64
) with
u^{0}
-part
c_{\mathrm{ne}}^{*}
given by the non-equivariant
c^{*}
-map. So the
u^{0}
-part of the
c^{*}
-image of an equivariant quantum product,
c^{*}(x\star y)
, is
c_{\mathrm{ne}}^{*}(x_{0}\star_{0}y_{0})
, where
x_{0}=\mathrm{ev}_{0}(x),y_{0}=\mathrm{ev}_{0}(y)
.
∎

8.3.
Computation of
E^{-}SH^{*}(Y)
in the weight
(a,0)
case

For the (free) weight
(a,b)=(a,0),
the maps in (
80
) are repeated application of the “same” injective
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module homomorphism
r_{a,0}
up to degree-shifts (cf.
Corollary

6.19
), which we now describe.

Proposition 8.2
.

For
(a,b)=(a,0)
, (
80
) is equivariant quantum multiplication by a class
EQ
,

E^{-}QH^{*}(Y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle EQ\,}}{{\longrightarrow}}E^{-}QH^{*}(Y)[2\mu]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle EQ\,}}{{\longrightarrow}}E^{-}QH^{*}(Y)[4\mu]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle EQ\,}}{{\longrightarrow}}\cdots

The element
EQ\in E^{-}QH^{2\mu}(Y)
satisfies
EQ^{k}\neq 0
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and has the form

EQ=Q_{\varphi}+qu^{m}\neq 0\in E^{-}QH^{2\mu}(Y),\qquad\textrm{ for some }m\geq 1,q\in E^{-}QH^{2\mu-2m}(Y),

where
Q_{\varphi}
is the non-equivariant rotation class from
[
RŽ23
]
(cf.
Remark

6.20
).

The
x_{k}
from (
95
) satisfy
x_{k}\star x_{\ell}=x_{k+\ell}
.
If
c_{1}(Y)=0
, then
x_{k}=n_{k}\cdot EQ^{k}
for some
n_{k}\in\mathbb{K}^{\times}
.

If
c_{1}(Y)=0
, then
Q_{\varphi}
is nilpotent, whereas

EQ
becomes invertible in
E^{-}QH^{*}(Y)_{u}\cong E^{\infty}QH^{*}(Y)
.

Proof.

We use a general property of Seidel isomorphisms: in the presence of a pair-of-pants product in Floer theory, the Seidel isomorphism acts as a module isomorphism (so
\mathcal{S}(x*y)=\mathcal{S}(x)*y
) given by quantum product by the image
\mathcal{S}(x_{0})
of the identity element
x_{0}
, and
\mathcal{S}(x_{0})
is often called the Seidel element (see
[
Sei97
, Prop.6.3]
and
[
Rit14
, Thm.23]
).
In our case,
EQ:=\mathcal{S}(x_{0}).

Note
EQ^{k}
corresponds to
c_{k^{+}}(x_{0})\in E^{-}_{(a,0)}HF^{*}(H_{k^{+}})
, so
EQ^{k}\neq 0
follows by injectivity of
c_{k^{+}}
(cf.
Theorem

6.16
).

Using the property
\mathcal{S}(x*y)=\mathcal{S}(x)*y,

the
x_{k}
from (
95
) satisfy:

x_{k}*x_{\ell}=\mathcal{S}^{-k}(x_{0})*\mathcal{S}^{-\ell}(x_{0})=\mathcal{S}^{-k}(x_{0}*\mathcal{S}^{-\ell}(x_{0}))=\mathcal{S}^{-k}(\mathcal{S}^{-\ell}(x_{0}))=\mathcal{S}^{-k-\ell}(x_{0})=x_{k+\ell}.

Suppose
c_{1}(Y)=0
. Both
x_{k}
and
EQ^{k}
lie in minimal grading so they differ by
\mathbb{K}^{\times}
-rescaling.
Nilpotency of
Q_{\varphi}
is a consequence of a grading argument due to Ritter
[
Rit14
]
, see also
[
RŽ23
]
. The last claim uses that
u
-localisation and
EQ
-localisation agree:
E^{-}QH^{*}(Y)_{u}\cong E^{-}SH^{*}(Y)\cong E^{-}QH^{*}(Y)_{EQ}
.
∎

Corollary 8.3
.

E^{-}SH^{*}(Y)\cong E^{-}QH^{*}(Y)_{EQ}
, so that
E^{-}c^{*}:E^{-}QH^{*}(Y)\to E^{-}SH^{*}(Y)
becomes the
EQ
-localisation map
E^{-}QH^{*}(Y)\to E^{-}QH^{*}(Y)_{EQ}
.

Proof.

This follows from
Proposition

8.2
by using
Lemma

2.5
.
∎

8.4.
The filtration by ideals in the weight
(a,0)
case

Corollary 8.4
.

For
\diamondsuit\in\{-,\infty\}
the filtration

\mathscr{F}_{j,(a,0)}^{\diamondsuit,p}\subset E^{-}_{(a,0)}QH^{*}(Y)
consists of ideals for equivariant quantum product. It also defines ideals
\mathrm{ev}_{0}(\mathscr{F}_{j,(a,0)}^{-,p})\subset QH^{*}(Y)
for ordinary quantum product.

Proof.

The first claim holds because
c_{\lambda}
is a
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-linear
E^{-}QH^{*}(Y)
-module homomorphism by
Proposition

8.1
.
The second claim follows, as
\mathrm{ev}_{0}:E^{-}QH^{*}(Y)\to QH^{*}(Y)
is a surjective ring homomorphism by combining
Lemma

6.3
and
Proposition

8.1
.
∎

9.
Properties of the equivariant spectral sequences

9.1.
Conventions and notation

We assume the reader has familiarity with the discussion of Morse–Bott–Floer spectral sequences from
[
RŽ24
]
, which readily adapts to the equivariant setting.

Abbreviate
E_{r}^{-}
-page
to mean the
E_{r}
-page of the Morse–Bott–Floer spectral sequence converging to
E^{-}SH^{*}(Y)
(or
E^{-}HF^{*}(H_{k^{+}})
, if we ignore columns above the
k
-th one). Similarly, define the
E_{r}^{\infty}
- and
E_{r}^{+}
-page.
The zeroth column is the one involving the generators of equivariant quantum cohomology.
We call
higher columns
all columns after (and excluding) the zeroth column.

Observe
Figure

2
in
Section

10
: when we draw equivariant spectral sequences, a bold dot will denote a
\mathbb{K}
-summand with
u^{0}
-factor. A smaller dot denotes a
\mathbb{K}
-summand with a
u^{\neq 0}
-factor.

9.2.
Structural results for the Morse–Bott–Floer spectral sequences

Proposition 9.1
.

Let
(a,b)
be a free weight.
31
31

31

Many of the claims extend to general weights when considering columns of period
p\neq\tfrac{a}{b}
; compare the proof of
Theorem

6.21
. We omit these statements.

The following apply to the Morse–Bott–Floer spectral sequences for equivariant symplectic cohomology as well as for equivariant Floer cohomology of
H_{k^{+}}
.

(1)

On any
E_{r}^{-},E_{r}^{\infty}
page, for
r\geq 1
, all edge differentials landing in the zeroth column must vanish.

More generally, for any
k\in\mathbb{N},
on the
E_{r}^{-}
page for
r\geq 1
all edge differentials from columns with slopes
\lambda>k
landing in the columns with slopes
\leq k
must vanish.

(2)

The edge differential on the
E_{0}^{\infty}
-page kills all higher columns, and its arrows cannot decrease
u
-orders
(this can fail on higher pages,
Section

9.3
).
Thus, the higher columns of
E_{1}^{\infty}
are zero. Therefore
E_{r}^{\infty}
has already converged to its zeroth column:
E^{\infty}_{1}\cong E^{\infty}QH^{*}(Y)\cong E^{\infty}SH^{*}(Y)
.

(3)

Forgetting smaller dots in
E_{0}^{\infty}
, the page of bold dots with all arrows between them is the
E_{0}
-page of the non-equivariant spectral sequence converging to
SH^{*}(Y)

(
which is
0
if
c_{1}(Y)=0)
.

(4)

The differential on higher columns for the
E_{0}^{-}
,
E_{0}^{+}
pages is determined by the
E_{0}^{\infty}
-page.

(5)

The following commutative diagram is induced by
E_{0}
-pages. The rows are long exact sequences. The numbers on the arrows indicate the degree of the map when it is a non-zero degree.

\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E_{1}^{+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{-1}
\scriptstyle{d_{1}}
\textstyle{E_{1}^{-}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{+2}
\scriptstyle{u\otimes 1}
\scriptstyle{d_{1}}
\textstyle{E_{1}^{\infty}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d_{1}=0}
\textstyle{E_{1}^{+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{-1}
\scriptstyle{d_{1}}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E_{1}^{+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{-1}
\textstyle{E_{1}^{-}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{+2}
\scriptstyle{u\otimes 1}
\textstyle{E_{1}^{\infty}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E_{1}^{+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{-1}
\textstyle{\cdots}

Warning: it is a LES of
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-modules, but the
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module structure on
E_{1}
-pages often does not agree with that on the modules that the spectral sequence converges to (see
Section

10
).

(6)

The zeroth column of
E_{1}^{+}
is identifiable with
\mathrm{coker}\,(u\otimes 1:E_{1}^{-}\to E_{1}^{\infty})
.

The higher columns of
E_{1}^{+}
are identifiable with the higher columns of
E_{1}^{-}
shifted up by
1
degree, via the explicit
\mathbb{K}
-linear isomorphism
u^{-1}d_{0}^{\infty}

using the edge differential
d_{0}^{\infty}
of
E_{0}^{\infty}
.

The higher columns of
E_{1}^{-}
are precisely the
u
-torsion part of the
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module
E_{1}^{-}
.

Proof.

The first part of Claim 1 follows by
Theorem

6.16
, because inclusion of the zeroth column corresponds (after taking the limit of the spectral sequence) to the continuation map.
The second part of Claim 1 essentially follows by
Theorem

6.21
parts (1) and (4). The columns with slopes
\leq k
compute
HF^{*}(H_{k^{+}})
, and the columns with slopes
<\lambda
compute
HF^{*}(H_{\lambda})
. Just like in the non-equivariant setup from
[
RŽ24
]
, the continuation map
HF^{*}(H_{k^{+}})\to HF^{*}(H_{\lambda})
can be built as the inclusion of the subcomplex of the first set of columns into the second set of columns (on the zero-page, but using the original differential of the chain complex).
Suppose there is a chain
y
in a column with slope
>k
on the
E_{r}^{-}
page, with edge differential
d_{r}(y)=x
landing in a column with slope
\leq k
.
There are two cases: (1) either
x
was going to survive to the limit
E_{\infty}^{-}\cong HF^{*}(H_{k^{+}})
in the spectral sequence where we ignore columns with slopes
>k
; or (2) there was going to be a non-trivial edge differential
d_{r^{\prime}}(x)=z\neq 0
on a later page
r^{\prime}\geq r
(where
z
also lies in a column of slope
\leq k
). In case (1),
x
represents a class of
HF^{*}(H_{k^{+}})
that is killed in
HF^{*}(H_{\lambda})
, contradicting
Theorem

6.21
.(1). In case (2), we get
d_{r^{\prime}}(d_{r}(y))=d_{r^{\prime}}(x)=z\neq 0
: if
r^{\prime}=r
then this contradicts that
d_{r}\circ d_{r}=0
; if
r^{\prime}>r
then this contradicts the well-definedness of
d_{r^{\prime}}
on the full spectral sequence page
E_{r^{\prime}}^{-}
since
[x]
represents the zero class on
E_{r^{\prime}}^{-}
(in view of
x
having been killed by
y
on the earlier page
E_{r}^{-}
).

Claim 2 follows by
Proposition

6.12
,
using that
Equation

38
does not increase the
u
-order, thus the same holds on quotients when building the
E_{0}^{\infty}
-edge differential.
For claim 3, we use
Lemma

3.9
.
Claim 4 follows from naturality of the construction of the
E_{0}
page, and the fact that at the chain complex level the equivariant differential is the “same” in all three models except for the fact that different coefficients are used, respectively:
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!],\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!),\mathbb{F}
(and over
\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)
all information about the differential is known), see (
45
).
Claim 5 follows by naturality of the construction of spectral sequences, as follows. We consider the long exact sequence in
Lemma

3.2
in the case where
C^{*},C^{*}_{u},C^{+}
are the
E_{0}
-pages with their differentials (thus the
W^{-},W^{\infty},W^{+}
are the
E_{1}
-pages).

To prove Claim 6, observe that the map
E_{1}^{-}\to E_{1}^{\infty}
is induced by the natural inclusion on
E_{0}
-pages after multiplying by
u
(using the natural
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module action on the
E_{1}^{-}
-page, viewed as the cohomology of the
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module
E_{0}^{-}
, so
u
acts as a degree
2
vertical map in the spectral sequence).
Note that the higher columns of
E_{1}^{-}
are
u
-torsion, because in high degrees the
E_{0}^{-}
-page coincides with the
E_{0}^{\infty}
-page, and we showed that the higher columns of the
E_{1}^{\infty}
-page vanish. Whereas the zeroth column of
E_{1}^{-}
, which is
E^{-}QH^{*}(Y)
, is a free
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module by
Proposition

6.1
.
Therefore, via
E_{1}^{-}\to E_{1}^{\infty}
the zeroth column will inject (after
u
-shifting), whereas higher columns of
E_{1}^{-}
map to zero in
E_{1}^{\infty}
as the higher columns of
E_{1}^{\infty}
vanish.
By exactness of the LES, those (torsion) higher columns of
E_{1}^{-}
that map to zero must come from the higher columns of
E_{1}^{+}
(shifted down in degree by
1
).
Note this is consistent with the general discussion of torsion in
Equation

47
.
The claim about
u^{-1}d_{0}^{\infty}
comes from the description of the connecting map in
Lemma

3.2
.
∎

9.3.
Spectral sequence edge differentials viewed as zig-zags

We briefly remind the reader how edge differentials arise as
zig-zags
in the spectral sequence associated to a filtration
F_{0}\subset F_{1}\subset\cdots\subset C^{*}
by subcomplexes. We work with Massey’s exact couples as in Bott-Tu
[
BT82
, Chp.III.Sec.14]
. Bott and Tu describe the case of a bicomplex, using very intuitive zig-zags

[
BT82
, Equation (14.12) p.164]
. Our setting is more general, so we explain what we mean.
We use the usual convention for spectral sequences that
p
denotes the column number. To avoid confusing
p
with the period of
S^{1}
-orbits, we instead let
\lambda_{0}=0<\lambda_{1}<\lambda_{2}<\ldots
denote the slopes for which
1
-orbits appear, corresponding to
\lambda_{p}
-periodic
S^{1}
-orbits of the
\varphi
action.
In our notation (compare
Figure

2
in
Section

10
),
F_{p}
corresponds to the subcomplex of
1
-orbits arising with slopes
\leq\lambda_{p}
, since those are the ones which appear as representatives for classes in columns
0,1,\ldots,p
of the
E_{0}
-page.

For
r\geq 1
, the edge differential
D_{r}
on a class
[f_{p}]
in the
p
-column of the
E_{r}
-page is

D_{r}[f_{p}]=[f_{p-r}]\;\;\textrm{ if }\;df_{p}\in f_{p-r}+dF_{p}\subset F_{p},

where
d:F_{p}\to F_{p}
is the original differential of
C^{*}
.
In the case we are most interested in, when
p=r
so
D_{r}[f_{p}]
hits the zeroth column,
this can be thought of as a zig-zag of arrows, because we seek classes
[g_{0}],[g_{1}],[g_{2}],\ldots,[g_{r}]
in columns
0,1,2,\ldots,r
of the
E_{0}
-page, such that

df_{r}=f_{0}+dg_{0}+dg_{1}+dg_{2}+\cdots+dg_{r}\qquad\textrm{ as an equation in }F_{r}.

If we abusively draw all arrows
\delta_{i}
on the
E_{0}
-page caused by
d=\delta_{0}+u\delta_{1}+\cdots
, and not just the vertical edge differential
D_{0}=[\delta_{0}]
, then the above equation is a zig-zag of arrows on the
E_{0}
-page from the classes
[g_{0}],\ldots,[g_{r}],[f_{r}]
which all cancel out except for the output
f_{0}
.
(The more general case, when
r<p
, is similar, with the
[g_{0}],\ldots,[g_{p-r-1}]
playing the role of cancelling out any error terms arising in the columns to the left of the
(p-r)
-column, in which the desired output
[f_{p-r}]
lives).

It is crucial in the above description to use the
E_{0}
-page, rather than the
E_{1}
-page because classes that die when passing from
E_{0}
to
E_{1}
may be crucial to link up a zig-zag as above.

Example 9.2
.

The following picture is the piece of the
E_{0}^{+}
-page from
Figure

3
in
Section

10
arising in rows
-2
and
-3
(which is the total degree value
p+q
of
(E_{0}^{+})^{p,q}
, in our convention). Each dot corresponds to a copy of the base field
\mathbb{K}
, and we label a basis by letters as shown.
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]

The bold red arrow is the
D_{0}=[\delta_{0}]
differential of the
E_{0}^{+}
-page, the green arrows are all the potentially-nonzero higher order
(\delta_{i})_{i\geq 1}
contributions to
d
in the setting of
Figure

3
. In general we do not know which of these arrows are non-zero, although a posteriori one finds that most are non-zero: we later prove that
g_{1}
kills
h_{1}
on
E_{0}^{+}
, and then since
E^{+}\Rightarrow E^{+}SH^{*}(T^{*}\mathbb{C}P^{1})=0
, we deduce that
g_{2}
must kill some
1
-dimensional subspace
S\subset\mathbb{K}[h_{0}]\oplus\mathbb{K}[f_{0}]
on
E_{2}^{+}
, and finally
f_{3}
must kill the
1
-dimensional space
(\mathbb{K}[h_{0}]\oplus\mathbb{K}[f_{0}])/S
on
E_{3}^{+}
. We explain how
D_{3}[f_{3}]=(\textrm{non-zero})\cdot[f_{0}]
is possible, even though there are no arrows
f_{3}\to f_{0}
.
To illustrate a simple example, let us assume that
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{Z}/2
and that all arrows are isomorphisms, except
g_{2}\to h_{1}
is zero (so
dg_{2}=h_{0}
and
D_{2}[g_{2}]=[h_{0}]
). Then:

D_{3}[f_{3}]=[f_{0}],\qquad\textrm{ since }df_{3}=f_{0}+dg_{1}+dg_{2}.

Note: on the
E_{1}
-page, only
h_{0},f_{0},g_{2},f_{3}
survive: in particular
h_{1}
is killed, so the
E_{1}
-page would not detect the existence of that zig-zag anymore.

Over a general base field
\mathbb{K}
it is more complicated. Write
\sim
to mean equality up to a non-zero constant. We will see later that
D_{0}[g_{1}]\sim[h_{1}]
, and we know the spectral sequence converges to zero.
If
D_{2}[g_{2}]\sim[h_{0}]
, then
D_{3}[f_{3}]\sim[f_{0}]
and it would follow that the arrow
g_{2}\to h_{1}
is zero, the arrow
g_{1}\to h_{0}
can be anything, and the remaining arrows are isomorphisms. Conversely, if
g_{2}\to h_{1}
is zero, then
D_{2}[g_{2}]\sim[h_{0}].

However, if
g_{2}\to h_{1}
is non-zero, a zig-zag with
g_{2}
shows
D_{3}[f_{3}]\sim[h_{0}]
, and
D_{2}[g_{2}]=\alpha[h_{0}]+\beta[f_{0}]
for some
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{K}
with
\beta\neq 0
, due to a zig-zag involving
g_{1}.

10.
Example 1:
\mathbb{C}

10.1.
Notation

Let
Y=\mathbb{C}
, with the standard
S^{1}
-action. Recall
\mathrm{char}\,\mathbb{K}=0
.
One can construct
H_{\lambda}=c(H)
to have a critical point
x_{0}
at
0
, and Morse–Bott manifolds
B_{k}\cong S^{1}
of non-constant
1
-orbits arising at
c^{\prime}(H)=k\in\{1,2,\ldots,\lfloor\lambda\rfloor\}
.
Let
x_{k}
and
y_{k}
denote the generators of
H^{*}(B_{k})[2k]
, in degrees
|x_{k}|=-2k
,
|y_{k}|=-2k+1
.
As
c_{1}(Y)=0
, we have a
\mathbb{Z}
-grading on Floer complexes.

Lemma 10.1
.

The Floer differential on
E^{\infty}FC^{*}(H_{\lambda})
is a
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module homomorphism
with

d(x_{k})\in\mathbb{K}y_{k}\;\;\textrm{ and }\;\;d(y_{k})\in\mathbb{K}x_{k-1}\oplus\mathbb{K}ux_{k}.

In particular,
\delta_{i}=0\textrm{ for }i\geq 2,\textrm{ so }d=\delta_{0}+u\delta_{1}
in (
38
), with
\delta_{1}(x_{k})=0.

Proof.

The Floer differential increases grading by
1
and cannot increase
H
by the filtration results from
[
RŽ23
]
, so
d(x_{k})\in\mathrm{span}_{\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]}(u^{-(k-1)}y_{1},\ldots,u^{-1}y_{k-1},y_{k})
,
d(y_{k})\in\mathrm{span}_{\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]}(u^{-(k-1)}x_{0},\ldots,x_{k-1},ux_{k})
.
However,
d
cannot decrease
u
-powers by (
38
), so the claim follows.
∎

10.2.
The case
\mathbf{(a,b)=(0,1)}

This case was studied in detail by Zhao
[
Zha17
, Sec.8.1]
:

(98)

d(x_{k})=0\quad\textrm{ and }\quad d(y_{k})=x_{k-1}+kux_{k}.

The non-equivariant case (equivalent to setting
u=0
) gives
HF^{*}(H_{k^{+}})=\mathbb{K}[x_{k}]
, so
SH^{*}(\mathbb{C})=0
.
For
\diamondsuit\in\{+,-,\infty\}
, the continuation map

E^{\diamondsuit}HF^{*}(H_{k^{+}})\to E^{\diamondsuit}HF^{*}(H_{(k+1)^{+}})
is therefore

(99)

[x_{k}]\mapsto[x_{k}]=-(k+1)u[x_{k+1}].

Thus, for
\diamondsuit=-,\infty,+
, the map (
99
) is respectively: an inclusion, an isomorphism, and zero (using
\text{char}\,\mathbb{K}=0
). Thus the
direct limit is
E^{-}\!SH^{*}(\mathbb{C})=\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)
,

E^{\infty}\!SH^{*}(\mathbb{C})=\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)
, and

E^{+}\!SH^{*}(\mathbb{C})=0
respectively.
By contrast,

E^{\diamondsuit}_{\textrm{telescope}}SH^{*}(\mathbb{C})=0
for all
\diamondsuit
: this follows from
SH^{*}(\mathbb{C})=0
for homological reasons
[
Zha19
, Cor.2.4]
;
e.g.
x_{0}
is a boundary as follows:
x_{0}=d(y_{1}-uy_{2}+2!u^{2}y_{3}-3!u^{3}y_{4}+\cdots)
.

The same calculation by Morse–Bott–Floer spectral sequence methods is as follows. The
E_{1}
-page in
Figure

2
arose from the “vertical differential”:
d_{0}(x_{k})=0
,
d_{0}(y_{k})=kux_{k}
(compare
Example

1.1
). For
E^{\infty}\!SH^{*}(\mathbb{C})
, all columns in
E_{1}
vanish except the
0
-th column
H^{*}(Y)\otimes\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)
(using
\mathrm{char}\,\mathbb{K}=0
).
For
E^{-}\!SH^{*}(\mathbb{C})
, the
k
-th column is
\mathbb{K}\cdot x_{k}
, so the spectral sequence collapsed to
E_{1}=E_{\infty}=\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\cdot[x_{0}]\oplus\mathbb{K}\cdot[x_{1}]\oplus\mathbb{K}\cdot[x_{2}]\oplus\cdots
. The
u
-action is
u\cdot[x_{k}]=[ux_{k}]=-\tfrac{1}{k}[x_{k-1}]
(for
k\geq 1
), so we obtain the
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module
E^{-}\!SH^{*}(\mathbb{C})\cong\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)
.

10.3.
The general free weight case
(a,b)

We will assume
(a,b)
is free; for
Y=\mathbb{C}
this means
a\notin b\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
. We recall that the Maslov index of the
S^{1}
-action on
Y=\mathbb{C}
is
\mu=1
.

Note that Zhao’s local Floer computation (
98
) was only for the case
(a,b)=(0,1)
.
For weights
(-a,1)
with
-a\leq 0
, Liebenschutz-Jones
[
LJ20
, Sec.8.1]
, working over
\mathbb{Z}
-coefficients, combined Zhao’s result with properties of the Seidel isomorphisms to obtain a result for
(-a,1)
. It turns out that
r_{-a,1}:E^{-}_{(-a,1)}QH^{*}(Y)\to E^{-}_{(-a-1,1)}QH^{*}(Y)
, as a map
\mathbb{Z}[u]\to\mathbb{Z}[u]
, is multiplication by
(a+1)u
(similarly, for
Y=\mathbb{C}^{n}
one gets multiplication by
[(a+1)u]^{n}
).

We wish to instead discuss the general free weight case, over characteristic zero, without appealing to local computations, as a means to illustrate the general theory.

Proposition 10.2
.

Let
(a,b)
be any weight with
a\notin b\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
.
Then

\begin{array}[]{ccccl}\!\!\!E^{-}\!SH^{*}(\mathbb{C})\cong\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!),&&\!\!\!\!\!\!E^{\infty}SH^{*}(\mathbb{C})\cong\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!),&&E^{+}\!SH^{*}(\mathbb{C})=0,\\
E^{-}HF^{*}(H_{k^{+}})\cong\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\!\cdot\![x_{k}],&&\!\!E^{+}HF^{*}(H_{k^{+}})=\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)\!\cdot\![x_{0}],&&E^{+}HF^{*}(H_{k^{+}})=(\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)/u^{1-k}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!])\!\cdot\![x_{0}]\end{array}

so
E^{\infty}HF^{*}(H_{k^{+}})\cong\mathbb{F}[2k].

The
x_{k}
agree up to
\mathbb{K}^{\times}
-rescaling with the
x_{k}
from (
95
), for degree reasons.

Proof.

This follows immediately from the general theory in
Section

7.3
.
∎

We deduced
Proposition

10.2
from the general theory. We now show how it can also be read-off from the Morse–Bott–Floer spectral sequence, in
Figure

2
.

Figure 2.

E_{0}
-page and
E_{1}
-page for the
S^{1}
-equivariant spectral sequence for
E^{\diamondsuit}SH^{*}(\mathbb{C})
for the three models
\diamondsuit=-,\infty,+
, and
Y=\mathbb{C}
. The smaller dots indicate that the
1
-orbit generator comes with a factor involving a non-zero power of
u
.

Lemma 10.3
.

The edge differential on higher columns of the
E_{0}^{\infty}
page goes from odd to even degrees, making higher columns acyclic, as drawn in
Figure

2
.

Proof.

By
Proposition

9.1
(2), each higher column of
E_{0}^{\infty}
is acyclic, so one possibility is that the arrows are as drawn in
Figure

2
: each arrow denotes an isomorphism
\mathbb{K}\to\mathbb{K}
.
However, we need to exclude another possibility: the arrows increase the degree by 1, but could a priori go from even to odd classes within a column.
Consider, by contradiction, the minimal
N\geq 1
such that column
N
has the arrows going from even to odd degrees. It follows that the arrows in column
N
preserve the power of
u
, so they arise from
\delta_{0}
in (
38
). Apply
Proposition

9.1
(3): remove all smaller dots, and consider just the resulting
E_{0}
-page of fat dots. By assumption, there is a (non-trivial) arrow between the two fat dots in column
N
. Therefore column
N
does not kill the lowest degree fat dot in column
N-1
, which therefore will survive to the limit as nothing else can cancel it for degree reasons (here the minimality assumption on
N
ensures that there is no cancellation occurring within column
N-1
on the
E_{0}
-page). This contradicts that the
E_{0}
-page of fat dots converges to zero:
SH^{*}(Y)=0
.
∎

By
Proposition

9.1
, from the
E_{0}^{\infty}
page we deduce the rest of the information about
E_{1}^{\infty},E_{0}^{\pm},E_{1}^{\pm}
shown in
Figure

2
.
The columns up to column number
k\in\mathbb{N}
give the spectral sequence for the Hamiltonian
H_{k^{+}}
.
Because all generators in the
E_{1}^{-}
,
E_{1}^{\infty}
pages are in the same parity, those spectral sequences have already converged. By the vanishing theorem,
Corollary

6.8
, we know that everything in the
E^{+}
-spectral sequence will die eventually in the limit, and we know that
E^{-}SH^{*}(Y)\cong E^{\infty}SH^{*}(Y)
. The
E_{1}^{-}
-page has a different
u
-module structure than
E^{-}SH^{*}(Y)
, nevertheless we can reconstruct the correct
u
-module structure a posteriori: we know that the
\mathbb{K}
-vector space generators must organise themselves so that
E^{-}SH^{*}(Y)\cong E^{\infty}SH^{*}(Y)\cong E^{\infty}QH^{*}(Y)
is a
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module isomorphism.
From this observation,
Proposition

10.2
follows, noting that
E^{+}HF^{*}(H_{k^{+}})
is the cokernel of
u\otimes 1:E^{-}HF^{*}(H_{k^{+}})\to E^{\infty}HF^{*}(H_{k^{+}})
by
Theorem

6.22
.

We now show that, a posteriori, we can partly reconstruct the Floer differential, analogously to (
98
).

Corollary 10.4
.

The equivariant Floer differential in
Lemma

10.1
satisfies

(100)

d(x_{k})=0\quad\textrm{ and }\quad d(y_{k})=\alpha_{k}x_{k-1}+\beta_{k}ux_{k},

where
\alpha_{k},\beta_{k}\in\mathbb{K}
are non-zero.

Proof.

Recall that the
E_{0}^{\infty}
-page differential is
\delta_{0}
.
By
Lemma

10.1
,

d(x_{k})=\delta_{0}(x_{k})\in\mathbb{K}y_{k}
, and there are no red arrows out of
[x_{k}]
on the
E_{0}^{\infty}
-page, so
d(x_{k})=0
.
By
Lemma

10.1
,

\delta_{0}(y_{k})=\alpha_{k}x_{k-1}
and
\delta_{1}(x_{k})=\beta_{k}x_{k}
(the latter is also known as the BV operator
\Delta(x_{k})
).
The
E_{0}^{\infty}
-page differential
d_{0}
is induced by
\delta_{0}
, and in
Figure

2
we have a red arrow that implies
d_{0}([y_{k}])=\beta_{k}u[x_{k}]
for
\beta_{k}\neq 0
(since the arrow is an isomorphism).
Finally,
\delta_{0}(y_{k})=\alpha_{k}x_{k-1}\neq 0
because
\delta_{0}
is the differential from the non-equivariant Floer complex, and only
y_{k}
can kill
x_{k-1}
, which it must since
SH^{*}(Y)=0
.
∎

Remark 10.5
.

A second proof of
\alpha_{k}\neq 0
follows from the
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module structure.
The
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module structure on the equivariant spectral sequence does not agree with the one on the limit. For
k\geq 1
,

(101)

u\cdot[x_{k}]=[ux_{k}]=[d_{0}(\beta_{k}^{-1}y_{k})],

so
[x_{k}]
is
u
-torsion when viewed in
E_{1}^{-}
with its natural
u
-action. However, by
Corollary

6.8
, we know that

E^{-}SH^{*}(\mathbb{C})
has no
u
-torsion, and the only way to prevent the analogue of (
101
) in
E^{-}SH^{*}(\mathbb{C})
is to ensure that
d(\beta_{k}^{-1}y_{k})
has a non-zero term involving
\alpha_{k}\neq 0
. It follows that the columns
“link up” via the
u
-action, when we instead consider the
u
-action on
E^{-}SH^{*}(\mathbb{C})
:

u\cdot[x_{k}]=[ux_{k}]=[d(\beta_{k}^{-1}y_{k})-\alpha_{k}\beta_{k}^{-1}x_{k-1}]=-\alpha_{k}\beta_{k}^{-1}\cdot[x_{k-1}]\textrm{ where }\alpha_{k}\beta_{k}^{-1}\neq 0.

Remark 10.6
.

A third proof of
\alpha_{k}\neq 0
uses the
E^{+}
-spectral sequence.
On the
E_{1}^{+}
-page, by (
100
),

[y_{1}]\mapsto d_{1}[y_{1}]=[d(y_{1})]=[\alpha_{1}x_{0}+\beta_{1}ux_{1}]=\alpha_{1}[x_{0}],

observing that
ux_{1}=0
over
\mathbb{F}
-coefficients. So the fact that
[y_{1}],[x_{0}]
kill each other off on the
E_{1}^{+}
-page is equivalent to the non-vanishing result
\alpha_{1}\neq 0
.
For
y_{2}
, the class
[y_{2}]
survives to the
E_{2}^{+}
-page, and one might be tempted by (
100
) to think that it will continue to survive. However, on the
E_{2}^{+}
page,
[y_{2}]
will kill
[u^{-1}x_{0}]
because the cohomology relations allow us to build a “zig-zag” map (
Section

9.3
):

[y_{2}]\mapsto[d(y_{2})]=[\alpha_{2}x_{1}+\beta ux_{2}]=[\alpha_{2}x_{1}]=[d(\alpha_{2}\beta_{1}^{-1}u^{-1}y_{1})-\alpha_{2}\alpha_{1}\beta_{1}^{-1}u^{-1}x_{0}]=-\alpha_{2}\alpha_{1}\beta_{1}^{-1}\cdot[u^{-1}x_{0}].

A similar argument will build a zig-zag map from
[y_{k}]
to
[u^{1-k}x_{0}]
(up to
\mathbb{K}^{\times}
-rescaling) contributing to the differential on the
E_{k}^{+}
-page.
Using
\beta_{k}\neq 0
, this discussion yields:

(102)

\left([u^{-k}x_{0}]\textrm{ gets killed on the }E_{k+1}^{+}\textrm{-page for each }k\geq 0\right)\Longleftrightarrow\left(\alpha_{k}\neq 0\textrm{ for all }k\geq 0\right).

If the left-hand side of (
102
) failed, then for grading reasons that
[u^{-k}x_{0}]
would survive to
E_{\infty}^{+}\cong E^{+}SH^{*}(Y)
, contradicting the vanishing in
Corollary

6.8
.

10.4.
The filtration polynomial for
Y=\mathbb{C}

Proposition 10.7
.

Let
(a,b)
be any weight with
a\notin b\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
.
Let
\lambda\in[k,k+1)
, for
k\geq 0\in\mathbb{N}.

Then
\mathscr{F}_{j}^{\lambda}=E^{-}QH^{*}(\mathbb{C})=\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\cdot x_{0}
for
j\leq k
, and
\mathscr{F}_{j}^{\lambda}=\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\cdot u^{j-k}x_{0}
for
j>k.

The
\lambda
-valuation of
x_{0}
is

\mathrm{val}^{\lambda}(x_{0})=k=\lfloor\lambda\rfloor

(cf.
Equation

60
).

The slice polynomial is:

s_{\lambda}(t)=1+t+\cdots+t^{k}

(cf.
Theorem

2.47
).

Proof.

By
Proposition

10.2
,

\mathscr{F}_{j}(E^{-}HF^{*}(H_{k^{+}}))=(u^{j}
-divisible elements of
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\cdot[x_{k}])
, so
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\cdot u^{j}x_{k}
.
For degree reasons, inside
E^{-}HF^{*}(H_{k^{+}})
we have:

\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\cdot u^{j}x_{k}=\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\cdot x_{k-j}\supset\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\cdot x_{0}\;\textrm{ for }j\leq k,\quad\textrm{ and }\quad\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\cdot u^{j}x_{k}=\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\cdot u^{j-k}x_{0}\;\textrm{ for }j\geq k.

By definition,
\mathscr{F}_{j}^{\lambda}=\mathscr{F}_{j}^{c_{\lambda}}(E^{-}QH^{*}(\mathbb{C}))

viewed inside
E^{-}HF^{*}(H_{k^{+}})

is the intersection between
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\cdot x_{0}
and
F_{j}(E^{-}HF^{*}(H_{k^{+}}))
, so the claim follows.

The slice polynomial also follows. It can also be computed directly using
Theorem

2.47
:
for grading reasons,
c_{\lambda}:\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\cdot x_{0}\to\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\cdot x_{k}
,
x_{0}\mapsto\gamma u^{k}x_{k}
for some
\gamma\in\mathbb{K}
, and
\gamma\neq 0
by
Theorem

6.16
, so
c_{\lambda}
has precisely one invariant factor:
u^{k}
.
∎

11.
Example 2:
T^{*}\mathbb{C}P^{1}

11.1.
The general free weight case for
Y=T^{*}\mathbb{C}P^{1}

Let
Y=T^{*}\mathbb{C}P^{1}\cong\mathrm{Tot}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{C}P^{1}}(-2))
with the standard
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action on fibres. Note
c_{1}(Y)=0
(cf.
[
Rit14
]
).
The weight
(a,b)
is free
\Leftrightarrow a\notin b\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
.

The Morse–Bott–Floer manifolds of non-constant
1
-orbits are
B_{k}\cong\mathbb{R}P^{3}
, in columns
k=1,2,\ldots
of the
E_{1}
-page, and they involve a degree shift of
2k\mu=2k
. Denote
[x_{k}]
the minimum of
B_{k}
(for an auxiliary Morse function), with grading
|x_{k}|=-2k
. The zeroth column has generators
[x_{0}],[x_{-1}]
in degrees
0,2
, generating the cohomology of the zero section,
\mathbb{C}P^{1}
.

Proposition 11.1
.

Let
(a,b)
be any weight with
a\notin b\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
.
Then

\hskip-21.52771pt\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\hskip-68.88867ptE^{-}\!SH^{*}(T^{*}\mathbb{C}P^{1})\cong\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{2},&&\hskip 12.91663ptE^{\infty}\!SH^{*}(T^{*}\mathbb{C}P^{1})\cong\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{2},&&\hskip-55.97205ptE^{+}\!SH^{*}(T^{*}\mathbb{C}P^{1})=0,\\
E^{-}\!HF^{*}(H_{k^{+}})\cong\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\cdot[x_{k}]\oplus\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\cdot[x_{k-1}],&&E^{\infty}\!HF^{*}(H_{k^{+}})\cong\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{2},&&E^{\infty}\!HF^{*}(H_{k^{+}})\cong\mathbb{F}[2k]\oplus\mathbb{F}[2k-2]\end{array}

The
x_{k}
agree up to
\mathbb{K}^{\times}
-rescaling with the
x_{k}
from (
95
), for degree reasons.

Proof.

This follows immediately from the general theory in
Section

7.3
.
∎

Figure 3.
Pages
E_{0}
and
E_{1}
of equivariant spectral sequences for
\diamondsuit=-,\infty,+
on
T^{*}\mathbb{C}P^{1}

Proposition

11.1
can also be read off directly from the Morse–Bott–Floer spectral sequence, in
Figure

3
, just like in the discussion for
Y=\mathbb{C}
below
Proposition

10.2
.
For
Y=T^{*}\mathbb{C}P^{1}
, the analogue of
Lemma

10.3
is immediate: the arrows on higher columns of
E_{0}^{\infty}
cannot go from even to odd classes, because that would decrease the
u
-power, contradicting
Proposition

9.1
(2).
The zeroth column bold dots are the two generators of
H^{*}(\mathbb{C}P^{1})\cong H^{*}(T^{*}\mathbb{C}P^{1})
(with coefficients in
\mathbb{K}
), and each of the other columns has bold dots that correspond to a copy of
H^{*}(\mathbb{R}P^{3})
, shifted in degrees appropriately (down by
2k\mu
, where
k
is the column number, and
\mu=1
is the Maslov number of the
S^{1}
-action).
All columns (except the zeroth) on the
E_{1}^{\infty}
-page must vanish by
Proposition

9.1
(2). Thus, the red arrows on the
E_{0}^{\infty}
-page in
Figure

3
are isomorphisms.
This determines the differentials on the
E_{0}^{-}
,
E_{0}^{+}
pages. As the
E_{1}^{-}
,
E_{1}^{\infty}
pages lie in even degree, they have converged. In the
E^{+}
-spectral sequence, everything eventually dies by
Corollary

6.8
. The edge differentials are not entirely determined,
for example the double arrow shown on the
E_{1}^{+}
-page is an injective map
\mathbb{K}\to\mathbb{K}^{2}
, but its
1
-dimensional image is not determined.

The
E_{1}^{-}
-page has a different
u
-module structure than the module
E^{-}SH^{*}(Y)
it has converged to; if we just consider columns up to column
k
, it has converged to
E^{-}HF^{*}(H_{k^{+}})
.
As
E^{-}HF^{*}(H_{k^{+}})
is torsion-free (
Theorem

6.22
), or using that
E^{-}SH^{*}(Y)\cong E^{\infty}QH^{*}(Y)
is free, we deduce a posteriori much of the
u
-action by degree considerations:
[x_{k}],[x_{k-1}]
must generate
E^{-}HF^{*}(H_{k^{+}})
as a
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module.
However, how precisely the
u
-action relates
[x_{k}],\ldots,[x_{0}]
is unclear, due to boundary cycle relations, with the exception of
32
32

32

no
\mathbb{K}
-linear combination of
x_{k-1}
and
ux_{k}
arises as a boundary cycle in
E^{-}CF^{*}(H_{k^{+}})
for degree reasons.

u\cdot[x_{k}]=[ux_{k}]
in
E^{-}HF^{*}(H_{k^{+}})
.

11.2.
The rotation elements for
Y=T^{*}\mathbb{C}P^{1}

We now describe
r_{a,b}
from
Corollary

7.1
.

We use the Hamiltonian
k^{+}H
, see
Section

7.2
. The
1
-orbits are the constant orbits at points of
\mathfrak{F}_{\min}=\mathbb{C}P^{1}
(zero section of
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{C}P^{1}}(-2)
). We pick an auxiliary Morse function on
\mathfrak{F}_{\min}
with two critical points, the minimum
x_{\min}
and the maximum
x_{\max}
.
Their grading is
|x_{\min}|=-2k\mu
and
|x_{\max}|=-2k\mu+2
, where
\mu=1
for
Y=T^{*}\mathbb{C}P^{1}
with the standard
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action on fibres.
The
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module basis
x_{\min},x_{\max}
of
E^{-}HF^{*}(k^{+}H)
plays a role analogous to the basis
x_{k},x_{k-1}
of
E^{-}HF^{*}(H_{k^{+}})
(also in degrees
-2k
,
-2k+2
), but need not coincide.
33
33

33

via the continuation isomorphism
E^{-}HF^{*}(k^{+}H)\cong E^{-}HF^{*}(H_{k^{+}})
,
x_{k}
is a
\mathbb{K}^{\times}
-rescaling of
x_{\min}
due to being in minimal grading, but
x_{k-1}
could be a non-obvious
\mathbb{K}
-linear combination of
ux_{\min}
and
x_{\max}
.

The critical points
x_{\min},x_{\max}
with those gradings can also be used as chain level generators for
E^{-}QH^{*}(Y)[2k\mu]
.

Lemma 11.2
.

In the bases
x_{\min},x_{\max}
(with respective gradings),
r_{a,b}
is a matrix

r_{a,b}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}Au&Bu^{2}\\
-2(1+\lambda_{+})&Cu\end{smallmatrix}\right)

for some
A,B,C\in\mathbb{K}
,
where
\lambda_{+}\in\mathbb{K}
involves only
T^{>0}
-terms and arises as
\mathcal{Q}_{\varphi}=(1+\lambda_{+})\mathrm{PD}[\mathbb{C}P^{1}]
in (
73
), and
\mathrm{PD}[\mathbb{C}P^{1}]=-2x_{\max}
.
The invariant factors of
r_{a,b}
are
(1,u^{2})
, cf. the first case in
Remark

7.6
, in particular the first map in (
81
) is not a naive inclusion since
r_{a,b}(x_{\min})=\textrm{unit}\cdot x_{\max}+Au\cdot x_{\min}
.

Proof.

On the domain of
r_{a,b}
, the basis
x_{\min},x_{\max}
lies respectively in grading
0,2
, whereas in the codomain it lies in grading
-2,0
. The powers of
u
are therefore dictated by the grading (see the
c_{1}(Y)=0
discussion in
Remark

7.5
).
The
Q_{\varphi}
class is known from
[
Rit14
]
.
Using
Example

2.1
: the lower left entry of
r_{a,b}
implies
1
is an invariant factor; the other invariant factor is
u^{2}
as the determinant is of order
u^{2}
and is non-zero since
r_{a,b}
is injective by
Corollary

7.1
.
∎

11.3.
The slice polynomial for
Y=T^{*}\mathbb{C}P^{1}

The slice polynomial
s_{\lambda}=s_{k}
for
T^{*}\mathbb{C}P^{1}
only depends on
k
for
\lambda\in[k,k+1)
, where
k\geq 0\in\mathbb{N}.
We will narrow it down to two possible cases:

\begin{split}N_{k}(t)&=2+2t+\cdots+2t^{k-1}+t^{k}+t^{k+1},\qquad(\textrm{so }c_{k^{+}}\textrm{ has invariant factors }(u^{k-1},u^{k+1})),\\
Z_{k}(t)&=2+2t+\cdots+2t^{k-1}+2t^{k},\qquad\qquad\;\;(\textrm{so }c_{k^{+}}\textrm{ has invariant factors }(u^{k},u^{k})).\end{split}

The two cases are not distinguished by the dimension
\dim_{\mathbb{K}}\mathscr{E}^{k}_{1}=2k
(see
Section

7.3
).

Proposition 11.3
.

Let
(a,b)
be any weight with
a\notin b\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
.
The slice polynomial is

s_{k}=Z_{k}\Leftrightarrow x_{0}\in\mathscr{F}_{k}^{\lambda}\Leftrightarrow[u^{-k+1}x_{0}]=0\in E^{+}HF^{*}(H_{k^{+}}),\textrm{ otherwise: }s_{k}=N_{k}\textrm{ and }x_{0}\in\mathscr{F}_{k-1}^{\lambda}.

If
s_{k}=Z_{k}
then
s_{k+1}=N_{k+1}
.
So
s_{k}=N_{k}
holds for infinitely many
k\in\mathbb{N}.

If
b=0
and
s_{k}=Z_{k}
, then
s_{mk}=Z_{mk}
for all
m\in\mathbb{N}.

Proof.

The (injective) image
c_{\lambda}(\mathscr{F}_{j}^{\lambda})\subset E^{-}HF^{*}(H_{k^{+}})
cannot be easily determined as the
u
-action can relate
x_{-1},x_{0},x_{k-1},x_{k}
in a complicated way (for
k\geq 1
):

(103)

\begin{array}[]{rclccc}x_{0}&=&A_{k}u^{k}x_{k}&+&B_{k}u^{k-1}x_{k-1}\\
x_{-1}&=&C_{k}u^{k+1}x_{k}&+&D_{k}u^{k}x_{k-1},\end{array}

where
A_{k},B_{k},C_{k},D_{k}
are in the underlying field
\mathbb{B}
(so do not involve
u
).
This identifies
c_{\lambda}
with

(104)

u^{k-1}\cdot\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A_{k}u&C_{k}u^{2}\\
B_{k}&D_{k}u\end{smallmatrix}\right):\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{2}\to\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{2},\;\;\textrm{ with }A_{k}D_{k}-B_{k}C_{k}\neq 0,

using injectivity of
c_{\lambda}
(
Theorem

6.16
).
By
Example

2.1
, the invariant factors of

c_{\lambda}
are

(u^{k-1},u^{k+1})
when
B_{k}\neq 0
, or
(u^{k},u^{k})
when
B_{k}=0
.

Using
Theorem

2.47
, we deduce the two possible slice polynomials:

B_{k}=0\Rightarrow s_{k}=Z_{k},\textrm{ and }B_{k}\neq 0\Rightarrow s_{k}=N_{K}.

There are
\alpha_{k+1},\beta_{k+1}
in the underlying field determined by the linear dependency relation

(105)

u^{2}x_{k+1}=\alpha_{k+1}ux_{k}+\beta_{k+1}x_{k-1},

and
\beta_{k+1}\neq 0
since
x_{k},x_{k+1}
are
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-linearly independent.
Thus, the continuation map
E^{-}HF^{*}(H_{k^{+}})\to E^{-}HF^{*}(H_{k+1}^{+})
in the bases
x_{k},x_{k-1}
and
x_{k+1},x_{k}
can be viewed as the matrix
M_{k}
:

(106)

M_{k}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&\;\;\;\;\;\tfrac{1}{\beta_{k+1}}u^{2}\\
1&\;\;-\tfrac{\alpha_{k+1}}{\beta_{k+1}}u\end{smallmatrix}\right):\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{2}\to\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{2},\textrm{ so }c_{\lambda+1}=M_{k}\cdot\eqref{Equation clambda matrix in TP1 case}=u^{k}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\tfrac{B_{k}}{\beta_{k+1}}u&\;\;\tfrac{D_{k}}{\beta_{k+1}}u^{2}\\
A_{k}-\tfrac{\alpha_{k+1}B_{k}}{\beta_{k+1}}&\;\;(C_{k}-\tfrac{\alpha_{k+1}D_{k}}{\beta_{k+1}})u\end{smallmatrix}\right).

Thus,
B_{k+1}=A_{k}-\tfrac{\alpha_{k+1}B_{k}}{\beta_{k+1}}
.
The second claim follows:
34
34

34

Another proof is that
s_{k}=Z_{k}
,
s_{k+1}=Z_{k+1}
would imply that
x_{0}\in\mathbb{K}^{\times}u^{k}x_{k}\cap\mathbb{K}^{\times}u^{k+1}x_{k+1}
, causing
x_{k},x_{k+1}
to be
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-linearly dependent, contradiction.

if
B_{k}=0
then
B_{k+1}=A_{k}\neq 0
, using (
104
).

Now the first claim. Note
u^{k+1}x_{k+1}=\alpha_{k+1}u^{k}x_{k}+\beta_{k+1}u^{k-1}x_{k-1}
by (
105
). By the previous paragraph,
B_{k+1}=0
precisely if
(A_{k},B_{k})
and
(\alpha_{k+1},\beta_{k+1})
are proportional, and so, by (
103
), precisely if
x_{0}
and
u^{k+1}x_{k+1}
are proportional (meaning
x_{0}\in\mathscr{F}_{k+1}^{\lambda+1}
). So
s_{k}=Z_{k}\Leftrightarrow B_{k}=0\Leftrightarrow x_{0}\in\mathscr{F}_{k}^{\lambda}
. The last condition is equivalent to
[u^{-k+1}x_{0}]=0\in E^{+}HF^{*}(H_{k^{+}})
by (
17
).

The third claim uses (
96
):

s_{k}=Z_{k}\Leftrightarrow x_{0}\in\mathscr{F}_{k}^{k^{+}}\Rightarrow x_{0}\in\mathscr{F}_{mk}^{(mk)^{+}}\Leftrightarrow s_{mk}=Z_{mk}
.
∎

Remark 11.4
.

For small
k
:

s_{0}=Z_{0}=2
and
s_{1}=N_{1}=2+t+t^{2}
. The first is because
c_{0^{+}}
is an isomorphism, the second uses
B_{1}=1
from (
106
). We do not know
s_{2}
, as we do not know how
u^{-1}x_{0}
dies in the spectral sequence for
E^{+}SH^{*}(Y)
. This involves precisely the zig-zags discussed in
Example

9.2
: if the arrow
g_{2}\to h_{1}
in that example is zero then
u^{-1}x_{0}
is killed by
u^{-1}x_{2}
(via the arrow
g_{2}\to h_{0}
) so we are in the case
B_{2}=0
and
s_{2}=Z_{2}
, otherwise we are in the case
B_{2}\neq 0,s_{2}=N_{2}.

More generally,
s_{k}=Z_{k}
requires a very special circumstance:
x_{0}\in\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{\times}\cdot u^{k}x_{k}
; intuitively
x_{0}
is “more divisibile” than expected.
The
u
-divisibility of the image of
y\in E^{-}QH^{2d}(Y)
in
E^{-}HF^{*}(H_{k^{+}})
is at least
u^{d+k-1}
since
y
lies in the
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-linear span of
x_{k-1},ux_{k}
. However, some
y
have one extra degree of
u
-divisibility: they lie in
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\cdot x_{k}
.
Viewed in the
2
-plane
A:=\mathrm{span}_{\mathbb{K}}(x_{0},u^{-1}x_{-1})\subset E^{\infty}QH^{0}(Y),

we get a line
M_{k}:=\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\cdot x_{k}\cap A
through
0
, consisting of classes that are “more divisible” than expected.
So
s_{k}=Z_{k}
(so
B_{k}=0
above) is equivalent to
x_{0}\in M_{k}
.
Note that
M_{k}\cap M_{k+1}=\{0\}
, otherwise we get a relation
u^{k}x_{k}=\textrm{unit}\cdot u^{k+1}x_{k+1}
contradicting the
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-linear independence of
x_{k},x_{k+1}
. So the result “
s_{k}=Z_{k}\Rightarrow s_{k+1}=N_{k}
” is equivalent to the observation:
x_{0}\notin M_{k}\cap M_{k+1}
.
We are curious to know what configuration of lines
M_{k}
in the plane one obtains as
k
varies.

12.
Example
2^{\prime}
:
T^{*}\mathbb{C}P^{1}
with a twisted action

Consider now
Y=T^{*}\mathbb{C}P^{1}
with a
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action which acts both on the fibres and on the base. This twisted action was described for
T^{*}\mathbb{C}P^{n-1}
in
[
RŽ24
, Example 7.6]
.
This was mostly already discussed in
Example

1.35
, but we add some additional explanations here.
In our case, the
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-fixed locus
\mathfrak{F}=\mathfrak{F}_{0}\sqcup\mathfrak{F}_{1}
consists of two fixed points lying in the zero section:
x_{0}=[1,0]
and
y_{0}=[0,1]
.
The weights associated to
\mathfrak{F}_{\min}=\mathfrak{F}_{0}=\{x_{0}\}
are
(1,1)
, and to
\mathfrak{F}_{1}=\{y_{0}\}
are
(-1,3).

Those two points correspond to generators for the Morse and the Floer complexes, in grading
|x_{0}|=0
and
|y_{0}|=2
, and they generate the
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module
E^{-}QH^{*}(Y)
(see the two fat dots in the zeroth column in
Figure

1
).
The Maslov index is
\mu=2
(the sum of the weights of any fixed component).
In particular,
Y
is a Conical Symplectic Resolution of weight
s=2
.
The fiber over
y_{0}
is a
\mathbb{Z}/3
-torsion line bundle
\mathcal{H}\to\mathfrak{F}_{1}
inside
T^{*}\mathbb{C}P^{1}
, giving rise to Morse–Bott-manifolds
B_{1/3},B_{2/3},B_{4/3},\ldots
diffeomorphic to
S^{1}
.
The only other Morse–Bott-manifolds of orbits, are the ones corresponding to full rotations:
B_{1},B_{2},B_{3},\ldots
which are diffeomorphic to a sphere subbundle of
T^{*}\mathbb{C}P^{1}
, thus
\mathbb{R}P^{3}
(cohomologically
S^{3}
, as
\mathrm{char}\,\mathbb{K}=0
).

We consider the equivariant Floer theory for any free weight
(a,b)
.
The Morse–Bott–Floer spectral sequence pages
E_{0}^{-}
and
E_{1}^{-}
are drawn in
Figure

1
up to the slope value
4/3
.
As in the previous Section, the fat dots are the generators arising on the non-equivariant spectral sequence so these must kill each other out as
SH^{*}(Y)=0
; from this the red arrows all follow by using the fact that the higher columns of the
E^{\infty}_{0}
spectral sequence page are acyclic (as we are in the free weight case).

By the general theory,
E^{-}SH^{*}(Y)\cong E^{\infty}SH^{*}(Y)\cong E^{\infty}QH^{*}(Y)
and
E^{+}SH^{*}(Y)=0
, because
c_{1}(Y)=0
and
(a,b)
is free.
Abbreviate
E_{\lambda}:=E^{-}HF^{*}(H_{\lambda^{+}})
.
Using the injectivity of the continuation maps, the Morse–Bott–Floer spectral sequence implies that

E_{\frac{1}{3}}\cong\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{2},\quad E_{\frac{2}{3}}\cong\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\oplus\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!][2],\quad E_{1}\cong\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!][2]\oplus\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!][4],\quad E_{\frac{4}{3}}\cong(\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{2})[4],\;\textrm{ etc.}

For slope values
p=k+\frac{1}{3}
, for
k\in\mathbb{N},
the filtration
\mathscr{F}^{p}
on
E^{-}QH^{*}(Y)
is essentially the filtration by grading (up to a translation by
4\lfloor p\rfloor
in the integer labelling).
Whereas for slopes
p=k+\frac{2}{3}
and
p\geq 1\in\mathbb{N}
it is not a filtration by grading: the
u
-valuation on
E^{-}QH^{*}(Y)
, compared to what it was for slope value
p^{-}
, has gone up by
1
on a specific rank one
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-submodule of
E^{-}QH^{*}(Y)
.

We now explain the proof of (
19
).
Call
x_{0},y_{0}
the two fixed points, where
x_{0}
is the minimum of the moment map, so the gradings are
|x_{0}|=0
,
|y_{0}|=2
(the two fat dots in the zeroth column of

Figure

1
). Ignoring gradings,
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{2}\cong E^{-}QH^{*}(Y)\hookrightarrow E^{-}HF^{*}(H_{\lambda})\hookrightarrow E^{-}SH^{*}(Y)\cong\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{2}
, so
c_{\lambda}^{*}:E^{-}QH^{*}(Y)\to E^{-}HF^{*}(H_{\lambda})
corresponds to a
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module homomorphism
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{2}\to\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{2}
.
If we now take into account gradings again, and observe
Figure

1
, we see that

c_{(1/3)^{+}}^{*}
must have invariant factors
(1,u).

For
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the continuation map

\psi:\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{2}\cong E^{-}HF^{*}(H_{(\frac{1}{3})^{+}})\to E^{-}HF^{*}(H_{(k+\frac{1}{3})^{+}})\cong(\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{2})[4k]

must have invariant factors
(u^{2k},u^{2k})
for grading reasons.
Since
c_{(k+\frac{1}{3})^{+}}=\psi\circ c_{(1/3)^{+}}
, it must have invariant factors
(u^{2k},u^{2k+1})
.
The continuation map

\psi^{\prime}:(\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{2})[4k]\cong E^{-}HF^{*}(H_{(k+\frac{1}{3})^{+}})\to E^{-}HF^{*}(H_{(k+\frac{2}{3})^{+}})\cong\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!][4k]\oplus\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!][4k+2]

must have invariant factors
(1,u)
, for grading reasons. Since
c_{(k+\frac{2}{3})^{+}}=\psi^{\prime}\circ c_{(k+\frac{1}{3})^{+}}
, the invariant factors can be
(u^{2k},u^{2k+2})
or
(u^{2k+1},u^{2k+1})
(these give rise respectively to the slice polynomials
N_{2k+1}
and
Z_{2k+1}
in (
19
)).
The map
ER_{k}:E^{-}QH^{*}(Y)\to E^{-}QH^{*}(Y)[2k\mu]
has grading
2k\mu=4k
, so in the basis
x_{0},y_{0}
it has the form

ER_{k}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A_{k}u^{2k}&C_{k}u^{2k+1}\\
B_{k}u^{2k-1}&D_{k}u^{2k}\end{smallmatrix}\right),

where
A_{k},B_{k},C_{k},D_{k}\in\mathbb{B}.

As it is an injective map,
\text{det}\,ER_{k}\neq 0
, so: if
B_{k}\neq 0
then the invariant factors are
(u^{2k-1},u^{2k+1})
, if
B_{k}=0
then the invariant factors are
(u^{2k},u^{2k}).

It follows that
s_{k}
is either
N_{2k}
or
Z_{2k},
which proves the options for
s_{k+1}
in (
19
).

Finally, we explain the “either … or …” in (
19
): the column with integer slope
k+1
on the
E_{1}
-page in
Figure

1
has two generators:
x_{\min}
and
u\cdot x_{\min}
(in gradings
-4k-4
,
-4k-2
), where
x_{\min}
is the minimum of an auxiliary Morse function on the Morse–Bott manifold
B_{k+1}\cong\mathbb{R}P^{3}.
This implies that
u^{2}\cdot x_{\min}
in
E^{-}HF^{*}(H_{(k+1)^{+}})
is an element in
E^{-}HF^{*}(H_{(k+\frac{2}{3})^{+}})
whose
u
-valuation has dropped by
2
. It also shows that the minimum of an auxiliary Morse function on the Morse–Bott manifold
B_{(k+\frac{2}{3})^{+}}\cong S^{1}
has not changed
u
-valuation value.
This proves that only one invariant factor changes, and it changes by a
u^{2}
factor, when passing from slope
(k+\frac{2}{3})^{+}
to
(k+1)^{+}
, so (
19
) follows.

13.
Example 3:
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{C}P^{1}}(-1)

Let
Y=\mathrm{Tot}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{C}P^{1}}(-1))
be the total space of the negative complex line bundle with Chern number
-1
, equivalently it is the blow-up of
\mathbb{C}P^{1}
at one point.
As explained in Ritter
[
Rit14
]
,
Y
is monotone with:

c_{1}(Y)=[\omega]\neq 0,\;\;QH^{*}(Y)\cong\mathbb{K}[\omega]/(\omega^{2}+T\omega),\;\;\textrm{ and }\;\;SH^{*}(Y)\cong QH^{*}(Y)/\ker[\omega]\cong\mathbb{K}.

Here
QH^{*}
and
SH^{*}
are
\mathbb{Z}
-graded, the Novikov variable
T\in\mathbb{K}
has grading
|T|=2
, so
\mathbb{K}
is graded. As
H^{*}(Y)\cong\mathbb{K}\oplus\mathbb{K}[-2]
, we have
SH^{*}_{\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}}(Y)\cong\mathbb{K}[1]
by the non-equivariant version of the LES (
76
).
We use the standard weight one
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action
\varphi
on fibres. The weight
(a,b)
is free precisely if
a\notin b\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}.

For
\mathcal{O}(-1)\to\mathbb{C}P^{n}
, weights
(-a,1)
with
-a\leq 0
, and working over
\mathbb{Z}
-coefficients, Liebenschutz-Jones
[
LJ20
, Sec.8.3]
gave a very explicit description of the
r_{(-a,1)}
maps on equivariant quantum cohomology, and of
E^{-}SH^{*}(Y)
.
The key ingredient was Liebenschutz-Jones’s intertwining formula.

We again wish to instead illustrate how the general theory applies, over charactersitic zero, for free weights.
Consider the first page of the Morse–Bott–Floer spectral sequence for
E^{-}SH^{*}(Y)
. Column
0
involves generators
x_{0},y_{0}\in H^{*}(\mathbb{C}P^{1})
; in columns
k\in\{1,2,\ldots\}
we have the cohomology generators
x_{k},y_{k}
for the Morse–Bott manifolds
B_{k}\cong S^{3}
with grading shift
2k\mu=2k
.
Thus the
\mathbb{Z}
-grading is:

|x_{0}|=0,\;|y_{0}|=2,\;|x_{k}|=-2k,\;|y_{k}|=-2k+3,\;|T|=2,\;|u|=2.

Lemma 13.1
.

Let
(a,b)
be any weight with
a\notin b\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
.

(107)

\begin{array}[]{lcccc}E^{-}\!SH^{*}(Y)\cong\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\oplus\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!),&&E^{\infty}\!SH^{*}(Y)\cong\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{2},&&E^{+}\!SH^{*}(Y)\cong\mathbb{F},\end{array}

where the
\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)
-summand in
E^{-}SH^{*}(Y)
is generated as a
\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)
-module by
[\omega+T\cdot 1]\in E^{-}QH^{*}(Y)
, whereas the
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
summand in
E^{-}SH^{*}(Y)
is generated as a
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module by
1\in E^{-}QH^{*}(Y)
. The latter generator also spans the
\mathbb{K}u^{0}
-summand of
\mathbb{F}
.

Proof.

By
Corollary

6.19
,
the
u^{0}
-part of
r_{a,b}
is the non-equivariant
r:QH^{*}(Y)\to QH^{*}(Y)[2]
, which by

[
Rit14
]
is multiplication by
Q=-[\omega]
.
We pick a basis of eigenvectors for
r
:

v_{1}=\omega
(eigenvalue
T
),
v_{2}=\omega+T\cdot 1
(eigenvalue
0
), thus

r=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}T&0\\
0&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.
Since
r_{a,b}
has degree two,
Remark

7.5
.(2) yields:

r_{a,b}=r+uA^{a,b},\textrm{ for a }2\times 2\textrm{ matrix }A^{a,b}\textrm{ with entries }A_{ij}^{a,b}\in\mathbb{B}\textrm{ (so not involving }T).

By
Theorem

6.16
,
r_{a,b}
is injective, so
0\neq\text{det}\,r_{a,b}\in A_{22}^{a,b}Tu+\mathbb{B}u^{2}
.

By
Example

2.1
,
r_{a,b}
either has invariant factors
(1,u)
(when
A_{22}^{a,b}\neq 0
) or
(1,u^{2})
(when
A_{22}^{a,b}=0
).
The composition
ER_{k}
from (
84
) of
k
such maps
r_{a,b}
, with varying
a
, therefore has
u^{0}
-part
r^{k}
causing an invariant factor
1
. By
Example

2.1
, the invariant factors of
ER_{k}
are therefore
(1,u^{m_{k}})
where

m_{k}:=\nu(\text{det}\,ER_{k})=\nu(\text{det}\,r_{a-(k-1)b,b})\cdot\ldots\cdot\nu(\text{det}\,r_{a-b,b})\cdot\nu(\text{det}\,r_{a,b}).

We deduce that
m_{k+1}-m_{k}:=\nu(r_{a-kb,b})
is either 1 or 2, depending on whether
A_{22}^{a-kb,b}
is non-zero or zero, respectively.
The claim follows from the general theory in
Section

7.3
: in our case,

j_{1}(k)=0\to 0
and
j_{2}(k)=m_{k}\to\infty
, as
k\to\infty
.
∎

Lemma 13.2
.

Let
(a,b)
be any weight with
a\notin b\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
.
Then as
\mathbb{K}
-vector spaces we have:

(108)

\begin{split}E^{-}SH^{*}(Y)&\cong\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]x_{0}\oplus\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]y_{0}\oplus\bigoplus_{i\geq 1}\mathbb{K}x_{i}\\
E^{-}HF^{*}(H_{k^{+}})&\cong\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]x_{0}\oplus\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]y_{0}\oplus\bigoplus_{1\leq i\leq k}\mathbb{K}x_{i}\end{split}

which inject into
E^{\infty}SH^{*}(Y)\cong E^{\infty}QH^{*}(Y)=\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)x_{0}\oplus\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)y_{0}
, and these injections are equal to an inclusion on the subspace
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]x_{0}\oplus\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]y_{0}
.

Proof.

We prove this from the Morse–Bott–Floer spectral sequence. This requires care because
\mathbb{K}
is now graded,
|T|=2
,
|T^{-1}|=-2
(this complication is paid off by ensuring a
\mathbb{Z}
-grading on Floer complexes).
In column zero,
x_{0}
represents
1
, and
y_{0}
represents
[\omega]
.
This gives rise to
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-modules
E^{-}QH^{*}(Y)=\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]x_{0}\oplus\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]y_{0}
and
E^{\infty}QH^{*}(Y)=\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)x_{0}\oplus\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)y_{0}
. The claims about the inclusions of
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]x_{0}\oplus\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]y_{0}
follow, because
the natural inclusion
E^{-}QH^{*}(Y)\hookrightarrow E^{\infty}QH^{*}(Y)
is the composite of the injections
E^{-}QH^{*}(Y)\hookrightarrow E^{-}HF^{*}(H_{k^{+}})\hookrightarrow E^{-}SH^{*}(Y)\hookrightarrow E^{\infty}SH^{*}(Y)\cong E^{\infty}QH^{*}(Y)
, cf. the proofs of
Corollary

6.24
and
Corollary

6.26
.

In column one, we have generators
x_{1},y_{1}
coming from the non-equivariant spectral sequence. Thus,
\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)x_{1}\oplus\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)y_{1}
is the first column in
E_{0}^{\infty}
. We want to find the contributions to the edge differential
d_{0}
for
E_{0}^{\infty}
occurring within column 1 and caused by the equivariant differentials
u^{i}\delta_{i}
for
i\geq 1
, see (
38
).
Note that the contributions to
u\delta_{1}(x_{1}),u\delta_{1}(y_{1})
within column 1 must land in
\mathbb{B}T^{>0}ux_{1}\oplus\mathbb{B}T^{>0}uy_{1}
because
\delta_{1}
does not involve
u
, and because non-constant Floer trajectories arise with strictly positive energy
E>0
, so they contribute with a strictly positive power
T^{E}
to
\delta_{1}
.
Note the grading
|\mathbb{B}T^{>0}ux_{1}|>0
and
|\mathbb{B}T^{>0}uy_{1}|>3
.
The
\mathbb{Z}
-grading also implies
|d_{0}(y_{1})|=|y_{1}|+1=2
and
|d_{0}(x_{1})|=|x_{1}|+1=-1
. Thus
u\delta_{1}(y_{1})\in\mathbb{B}Tux_{1}
and
u\delta_{1}(x_{1})=0
.
By
Proposition

9.1
(2), column 1 in
E_{0}^{\infty}
should be acyclic, therefore
u\delta_{1}(y_{1})=bTux_{1}
with
b\neq 0\in\mathbb{B}
, so that a non-trivial arrow
y_{1}\to ux_{1}
and its
u^{\pm 1}
-translates make the column acyclic. Using
Proposition

9.1
.(4),
it follows that in column 1 of
E_{0}^{-}
everything cancels except
x_{1}
(note
u^{-1}T^{-1}y_{1}
exists in
E_{0}^{\infty}
and kills
x_{1}=u\delta_{1}(b^{-1}u^{-1}T^{-1}y_{1})
, but it does not exist in
E_{0}^{-}
).
A similar argument applies to higher columns: copies
\mathbb{K}x_{i}
survive to
E_{1}^{-}
.
By
Proposition

9.1
.(1),
35
35

35

in this case the second part of
Proposition

9.1
.(1) is obvious: the
x_{i}
cannot kill each other off because they are in in even grading.

\oplus\mathbb{K}x_{i}
survives to the limit, thus the
E_{r}^{-}
spectral sequence has converged on page
E_{1}^{-}
. The first equation in (
13.2
) follows. The second equation follows because the spectral sequence, after omitting columns with slopes
>k
, converges to
E^{-}HF^{*}(H_{k^{+}}).

∎

Corollary 13.3
.

Let
(a,b)
be any weight with
a\notin b\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
. The slice polynomial is
s_{k}=2+t+\cdots+t^{k}
,
ER_{k}
has invariant factors
(1,u^{k})
, and the filtration polynomial is
f_{k}=1+t^{k}
.

Proof.

By the proof of
Lemma

13.1
,

s_{k}=2+t+\cdots+t^{m_{k}}
, using
Corollary

7.4
. By (
89
),

m_{k}=s_{k}(0)=\dim_{\mathbb{K}}\mathscr{E}^{k}_{1}=\dim_{\mathbb{K}}E^{-}HF^{*}(H_{k^{+}})/E^{-}QH^{*}(Y)=k,

using (
108
) in the last equality. The rest follows.
∎

Proposition 13.4
.

Let
(a,b)
be any weight with
a\notin b\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
.

(109)

\begin{array}[]{lcccc}E^{-}\!SH^{*}(Y)\cong\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\oplus\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!),&&\hskip-12.91663ptE^{\infty}\!SH^{*}(Y)\cong\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{2},&&\hskip-38.74988ptE^{+}\!SH^{*}(Y)\cong\mathbb{F},\\
E^{-}\!HF^{*}(H_{k^{+}}\!)=\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\!\cdot\![x_{0}]\oplus\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\!\cdot\![x_{k}],&&E^{\infty}\!HF^{*}(H_{k^{+}}\!)\cong\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{2},&&\hskip 4.30554ptE^{\infty}\!HF^{*}(H_{k^{+}}\!)\cong\mathbb{F}\oplus\mathbb{F}[2k]\end{array}

The
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
summand in
E^{-}SH^{*}(Y)
is generated as a
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module by
1

(
or
[\omega]
, or indeed any element whose
u^{0}
-part is not in
\mathbb{K}^{\times}\cdot[\omega+T\cdot 1])
. That generator also spans the
\mathbb{K}u^{0}
-summand of
\mathbb{F}
.

The
\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)
-summand in
E^{-}SH^{*}(Y)
is generated as a
\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)
-module by an element in
E^{-}QH^{*}(Y)
with
u^{0}
-part
[\omega+T\cdot 1]
, and such an element is unique up to
\mathbb{K}^{\times}
-rescaling.

Proof.

By the proof of
Lemma

13.1
, and using the notation
v_{1},v_{2}
from that proof, we know

ER_{k}(v_{1})=T^{k}v_{1}+u^{\geq 1}
-terms. So,
to build a Smith normal form for
ER_{k}
, we may use
v_{1}
as first basis vector on the domain, and
T^{-k}ER_{k}(v_{1})
as first basis vector on the codomain: this puts the invariant factor
1
in the first diagonal entry.
Corollary

2.18
then implies that
v_{1}
generates the
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-summand in
E^{-}SH^{*}(Y)
.

Since
ER_{k}(v_{2})
only involves
u^{\geq 1}
-terms, the same argument shows that any
v_{1}^{\prime}=\alpha v_{1}+\beta v_{2}
with
\alpha\in\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{\times}
,
\beta\in\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
can be used as first basis vector on the domain, by using
\alpha^{-1}T^{-k}ER_{k}(v_{1}^{\prime})
on the codomain. This proves the claim about the choice of generator for the
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-summand in
E^{-}SH^{*}(Y)
.

A posteriori, we know there exists a
w\in E^{-}SH^{*}(Y)
that generates the
\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)
-summand as a
\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)
-module. By
u^{\pm}
-rescaling, we may assume
w
lies in
E^{-}QH^{*}(Y)
, indeed we may also ensure it has non-zero
u^{0}
-part (so
\mathrm{ev}_{0}(w)\neq 0\in QH^{*}(Y)=\mathbb{K}x_{0}\oplus\mathbb{K}y_{0}
). With those requirements,
w
corresponds to an element in
\{0\}\times\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{\times}\subset\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\oplus\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)\cong E^{-}SH^{*}(Y).

Also, such a
w\in E^{-}QH^{*}(Y)
is not
u
-divisible in
E^{-}QH^{*}(Y)=\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]x_{0}\oplus\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]y_{0}
, whereas
ER_{k}(w)
is
u^{k}
-divisible for all
k\geq 1
.
We write
w=\alpha v_{1}+\beta v_{2}
in the basis
v_{1},v_{2}
, where
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
. By the proof of
Lemma

13.1
,
ER_{k}(\alpha v_{1}+\beta v_{2})=\alpha T^{k}v_{1}+u^{\geq 1}
-terms. Since
ER_{k}(w)=u^{\geq k}
-terms, we deduce
\alpha\in u\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
and
\beta\in\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{\times}.
Thus
w
lies in
E^{-}QH^{*}(Y)
with
u^{0}
-part
v_{2}
up to
\mathbb{K}^{\times}
-rescaling, as claimed.
Our construction of
w
also ensures that

\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]x_{0}\oplus\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]w=\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]x_{0}\oplus\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]y_{0}=E^{-}QH^{*}(Y).

We now prove
E^{-}HF^{*}(H_{k^{+}})=\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]x_{0}\oplus\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]x_{k}
.
The inclusion
E^{-}HF^{*}(H_{k^{+}})\hookrightarrow E^{-}HF^{*}(H_{k+1}^{+})
has
36
36

36

If we were working over
c_{1}(Y)=0
, so
\mathbb{K}
lies in grading zero, then we would have expected a codimension two map, because of the Seidel isomorphism
E^{-}HF^{*}(H_{(k+1)^{+}})\cong E^{-}HF^{*}(H_{k^{+}})[2]
. This is not a contradiction here, because in the monotone case the Novikov field
\mathbb{K}
is supported in all even integer gradings due to
|T^{\pm 1}|=\pm 2
.

\mathrm{codim}_{\mathbb{K}}=1
in view of the new class
x_{k+1}
, see (
108
).

By
Corollary

13.3
, the invariant factors
(1,u^{k})
of
ER_{k}
imply that
E^{-}HF^{*}(H_{k^{+}})\cong\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\oplus u^{-k}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
(see the general theory in
Section

7.3
).
So there is a generator of the
u^{-k}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
summand of the form

w_{-k}:=x_{k}+u^{\geq 1}\textrm{-terms, \qquad where }k\geq 1.

Thus,
\mathbb{K}^{\times}w_{-k}=\{
all generators of the
u^{-k}\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-summand
\}
, where
\mathbb{K}^{\times}=\mathbb{K}\setminus\{0\}
. So, for
k\geq 2
:

u\cdot w_{-k}\in\mathbb{K}^{\times}w_{-k+1}.

A posteriori, all these
w_{-k}
generate the
\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)
-summand of
E^{-}SH^{*}(Y)
, therefore
uw_{-1}
equals the above
w
up to
\mathbb{K}^{\times}
-rescaling,
thus
u^{k}w_{-k}\in\mathbb{K}^{\times}w
.
We also deduce that

u\cdot x_{k}\in\mathbb{K}^{\times}x_{k-1}+u^{\geq 1}\textrm{-terms, for }k\geq 2.

So
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]x_{0}\oplus\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]x_{k}
contains
x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
.
The claim about
E^{-}HF^{*}(H_{k^{+}})
follows, using (
108
).
∎

Remark 13.5
.

It is still unclear how the
x_{k}
are related to the classes
x_{k}=\mathcal{S}^{-k}(x_{0})
from (
95
): they are both in degree
-2k
, and it is reasonable to expect that they agree up to
u^{\geq 1}
-corrections and up to
\mathbb{K}^{\times}
-rescaling. We know
\mathcal{S}^{-k}(x_{0}),\mathcal{S}^{-k}(y_{0})
generate the
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module
E^{-}HF^{*}(H_{k^{+}})
, but the grading does not imply any conclusions because
|T^{\pm 1}|=\pm 2
can shift gradings arbitrarily.

14.
Equivariant symplectic cohomology in the monotone case

14.1.
The CY case

For symplectic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-manifolds with
c_{1}(Y)=0
, we showed in
Corollary

6.8
that
E^{-}SH^{*}(Y)\cong E^{\infty}SH^{*}(Y)
and
E^{+}SH^{*}(Y)=0
, for any weight
(a,b)
.

14.2.
The monotone case, for free weights

Now consider the monotone setting:
c_{1}(Y)\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}[\omega]
.
Let
(a,b)
be a free weight, and
m:=\dim_{\mathbb{K}}\,H^{*}(Y)
. The Novikov field
\mathbb{K}
is now graded.
Consider the
\mathbb{K}
-linear map
r
given by (non-equivariant) quantum product by
Q_{\varphi}
(
Remark

6.20
):

r:QH^{*}(Y)\to QH^{*}(Y)[2\mu],\qquad E_{0}:=\ker r^{m},\qquad W:=r^{m}(QH^{*}(Y)).

By
[
RŽ23
]
, leaning on
[
Rit14
,
Rit16
]
, the following commutative diagram of
\mathbb{K}
-linear maps holds,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces QH^{*}(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces SH^{*}(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
