where the rows are exact. Indeed
E_{0}:=\ker r^{m}
is the generalised
0
-eigenspace of
r
; and the image
W:=r^{m}(QH^{*}(Y))
has stabilised:
r(W)=W
, in other words
r:W\to W
is an isomorphism.

Theorem 14.1
.

Let
Y
be a monotone symplectic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-manifold, and
(a,b)
a free weight. Then we have the following
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module isomorphisms, compatibly with the short exact sequence in
Corollary

6.26
,

(110)

\begin{split}E^{-}SH^{*}(Y)&\cong SH^{*}(Y)[\![u]\!]\oplus E_{0}(\!(u)\!),\\
E^{\infty}SH^{*}(Y)&\cong SH^{*}(Y)(\!(u)\!)\oplus E_{0}(\!(u)\!)\cong E^{\infty}QH^{*}(Y),\\
E^{+}SH^{*}(Y)&\cong SH^{*}(Y)(\!(u)\!)/u\cdot SH^{*}(Y)[\![u]\!]\cong SH^{*}(Y)\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\mathbb{F}.\end{split}

Thus, letting
t:=\dim_{\mathbb{K}}\,SH^{*}(Y)
, and
m:=t+s=\dim_{\mathbb{K}}\,H^{*}(Y),

(111)

E^{-}SH^{*}(Y)\cong\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{t}\oplus\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{s},\quad E^{\infty}SH^{*}(Y)\cong E^{\infty}QH^{*}(Y)\cong\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{m},\quad E^{+}SH^{*}(Y)\cong\mathbb{F}^{t},

and the map
E^{-}c^{*}:E^{-}QH^{*}(Y)\to E^{-}SH^{*}(Y)
becomes the inclusion
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{t}\oplus\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{s}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{t}\oplus\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{s}
.

Proof.

To prove the claim, we construct the following commutative diagram of
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module homomorphisms (we omitted in the left column the grading shift
[-2]
):

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\infty}SH^{*}(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{+}SH^{*}(Y)}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W(\!(u)\!)\oplus E_{0}(\!(u)\!)}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(W\oplus E_{0})(\!(u)\!)/u(W\oplus E_{0})[\![u]\!]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{-}QH^{*}(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\infty}QH^{*}(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{+}QH^{*}(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathrm{localise}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{quotient}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

where all rows are exact and involve the obvious maps (see
Corollary

6.26
).

We can pick a
\mathbb{K}
-vector space basis
w_{1},\ldots,w_{t},v_{1},\ldots,v_{s}
for
QH^{*}(Y)
, where
w_{j}
are a basis for
W
, and
v_{j}
is a basis for
E_{0}\subset QH^{*}(Y)
. This induces a
\mathbb{K}
-linear isomorphism
QH^{*}(Y)\cong W\oplus E_{0}
.
By
Proposition

6.1
, we also obtain a non-canonical isomorphism

E^{\diamondsuit}QH^{*}(Y)\cong QH^{*}(Y)\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}E^{\diamondsuit}(\mathrm{point})\cong(W\oplus E_{0})\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}E^{\diamondsuit}(\mathrm{point}),

for
\diamondsuit\in\{-,\infty,+\}
, giving rise to the bottom two rows of the above commutative diagram.

In the basis
w_{1},\ldots,w_{t},v_{1},\ldots,v_{s}
, the matrix for
r^{m}
is a block-diagonal matrix: the first
t\times t
block corresponds to the isomorphism
r^{m}:W\to W
, the second
s\times s
block is the zero matrix corresponding to
r^{m}=0:E_{0}\to E_{0}
.
It follows that
ER_{m}
has the form described in (
26
). Similarly, any composite of
m
maps of type
r_{a,b}
, for varying
a
, will have the form described in (
26
).
Since
ER_{km}
is a composite of
k
such composites, it has the form
f_{k}\circ\cdots\circ f_{1}
described in
Lemma

2.2
. It follows that
ER_{k}
has invariant factors
1,\ldots,1,u^{\geq k},\ldots,u^{\geq k}
, with
t
copies of
1
. None of the
u^{\geq k}
invariant factors are
u^{\infty}:=0
because all
ER_{k}
maps are injective by
Corollary

7.4
, using that
(a,b)
is a free weight.

Thus, we are in the setting of the general theory described in
Section

7.3
, with:

j_{1}(km)=\cdots=j_{t}(km)=0,\qquad\textrm{ and }\qquad k\leq j_{t+1}(km)\leq\cdots\leq j_{t+s}(km).

The claim now easily follows from that general theory.
∎

Remark 14.2
.

A basis of
E^{-}QH^{*}(Y)
which (for some basis on the codomain) ensures that
E^{-}c^{*}:E^{-}QH^{*}(Y)\to E^{-}SH^{*}(Y)
is in Smith normal form can be chosen to be

w_{1},\ldots,w_{t},v_{1}+(u^{\geq 1}\textrm{-terms}),\ldots,v_{s}+(u^{\geq 1}\textrm{-terms}).

The vectors
v_{i}\in E_{0}
typically require such
u^{\geq 1}
-corrections. This is the same reason why in
Proposition

13.4
the generator of the
\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)
-summand in
E^{-}SH^{*}(Y)
is much trickier than the
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-summand.

14.3.
The monotone case, for non-free weights

Theorem 14.3
.

Let
Y
be a monotone symplectic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-manifold, and
(a,b)\neq(0,0)
any non-free weight.
Then (
110
) and (
111
) hold.
However,
E^{-}c^{*}:E^{-}QH^{*}(Y)\to E^{-}SH^{*}(Y)
may have kernel:
it corresponds to a map
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{t}\oplus\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{s}\to\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{t}\oplus\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)^{s}
with
\ker E^{-}c^{*}\subset\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{s}
.

Proof.

This follows by the same proof as
Theorem

14.1
, except that
ER_{km}
may have kernel, which corresponds to
u^{\geq k}
invariant factors being
u^{\infty}:=0
.
As
(a,b)\neq(0,0)
is non-free, we have
b\neq 0
.
Let
k:=\lceil\tfrac{a}{b}\rceil
.

Theorem

6.21
implies that
ER_{k-1}
is injective, since
k-1<\tfrac{a}{b}
.

ER_{k}
may have kernel, as the
E^{\infty}
-page for
E^{\infty}SH^{*}(Y)
may have a non-trivial contribution in the column for slope
\tfrac{a}{b}
, as explained in
Remark

6.14
.
From then on,
\ker ER_{k}=\ker ER_{k+\ell}
for all
\ell\geq 1
, i.e. no new kernel is created, because of
Theorem

6.21
.(1).
Call
n
the rank of that stabilised kernel.
We now assume that
km>\lceil\tfrac{a}{b}\rceil
.
We apply
Remark

2.3
to the map
ER_{km}
: part of the
v_{1},\ldots,v_{s}
basis for the
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{s}
summand, namely
v_{t+s-n+1},\ldots,v_{t+s}
, can be chosen to be a basis for the kernel.
To pass from
ER_{mk}
to
ER_{m(k+\ell)}
we compose with a continuation map that still has the form (
26
), and that can be turned into (
27
) by basis adjustments without changing the
v_{i}
(so only adjusting the
w_{j}
by adding higher corrections). In particular, the continuation map acting on the
v_{i}
sub-basis, modulo the
w_{j}+
(higher correction) sub-basis, gives rise to invariant factors
u^{\geq\ell},\ldots,u^{\geq\ell}
, and none of these are
u^{\infty}
because of
Theorem

6.21
.(1).
Thus, although the kernel caused the death of
0\oplus\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{n}\subset\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{s}
, that summand
0\oplus\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{n}
is reborn as the cokernel of
ER_{mk}
inside
E_{(a-mkb,b)}^{-}QH^{*}(Y)[2mk\mu]\cong\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{t}\oplus\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{s}
. This cokernel contributes generators to the direct limit. The subsequent continuation maps (for slopes
\lambda>mk>\tfrac{a}{b}
) cause the
u
-localisation of the
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{s}
-summand including the
u
-localisation of the reborn piece
0\oplus\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{n}\subset 0\oplus\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]^{s}.

The claim follows.
∎

Appendix A
Equivariant Seidel isomorphisms

A.1.
The general statement

Recall our ongoing notation:
Y
is any
symplectic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-manifold with almost complex structure
I
, the
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action
\varphi
has moment map
H:Y\to\mathbb{R}
, and
\mathrm{Fix}(\varphi)=\mathfrak{F}=\sqcup\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
denotes the connected components of the fixed locus, in particular
\mathfrak{F}_{\min}:=\min H.

We use the time-parameter
t\in S^{1}=[0,1]/(0\sim 1)
, and abbreviate
\varphi_{t}
to mean the
S^{1}
-flow by
\varphi
, whereas when we use the
\mathbb{R}_{+}
-action we will say so explicitly.
Following
[
RŽ23
]
, we use Hamiltonians
H_{\lambda}=c(H):Y\to\mathbb{R}
of slope
\lambda
at infinity (
c^{\prime}(H)=\lambda
for large
H
).

Write
E^{\diamondsuit}_{(a,b)}FC^{*}(H_{\lambda},I)
for the equivariant Floer complex for weight
(a,b)
using the equivariant model of type
\diamondsuit\in\{-,\infty,+\}
. Recall, from (
66
), this means Floer theory is defined equivariantly with respect to the
S^{1}
-action
(\theta\cdot_{(a,b)}x)(t)=\varphi_{a\theta}(x(t-b\theta))
on free loops
x(t)
in
Y
.
In particular,
\varphi
is acting as
\varphi^{a}
on the target of the reparametrised loop.
In this section, we will also use the symbol
E^{\diamondsuit}_{(\psi,b)}
: it means we change
\varphi
to another
S^{1}
-action
\psi
acting on
Y
, and the
S^{1}
-action on the free loopspace is

(\theta\cdot_{(\psi,b)}x)(t)=\psi_{\theta}(x(t-b\theta)).

Let
\sigma
be a Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-action on
Y
, with moment map
K:Y\to\mathbb{R}
.
When we say
\sigma

commutes with
\varphi
we mean that it commutes with the
S^{1}
-part of the action
\varphi
.
This ensures that arbitrary products of
\sigma,\varphi
are Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-actions.
We will prove that the following point exists,

(112)

x_{\min}\in\mathrm{Min}\,K|_{\mathfrak{F}_{\min}}\subset\mathfrak{F}_{\min}\cap\mathrm{Fix}(\sigma).

Here
\mathrm{Min}
is the minimal locus in
Y
where
K
attains its minimal value
\min K|_{\mathfrak{F}_{\min}}\in\mathbb{R}
.
Let
\mu_{\sigma}
be the
Maslov index
of the loop of symplectomorphisms
\sigma_{t}\in\mathrm{Aut}(T_{x_{\min}}Y).

If
\sigma
is pseudoholomorphic (
I\circ d\sigma_{t}=d\sigma_{t}\circ I
), then

\mu_{\sigma}
is the sum of the weights of the linearised
S^{1}
-action on
T_{x_{\min}}Y
(cf.
[
RŽ23
, Sec.5.1]
).
We define the “pull-back” data as follows:

(\sigma^{*}x)(t)=\sigma_{-t}x(t),\qquad\sigma^{*}H_{\lambda}:=H_{\lambda}-K,\qquad\sigma^{*}I=d\sigma_{t}^{-1}\circ I\circ d\sigma_{t}.

If
\sigma
is pseudoholomorphic (e.g. the
S^{1}
-part of a pseudoholomorphic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action) then
\sigma^{*}I=I
.

Theorem A.1
.
37
37

37

The left hand side of (
A.1
) is well-defined, and it is a general feature of the construction of (
A.1
) that the Floer complex on right hand side is automatically well-defined because there is a canonical bijection between
1
-orbits and between Floer trajectories, for the two respective choices of Floer data.
There is in fact a way to make both sides of the equation
\mathbb{Z}
-graded, and the map
\mathbb{Z}
-graded, by the capping discussion in
Section

A.2
.

For any Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-action
\sigma
that commutes with
\varphi
,
there is a chain isomorphism

(113)

\mathcal{S}_{\sigma}:E^{\diamondsuit}_{(\varphi^{a},b)}FC^{*}(H_{\lambda},I)\cong E^{\diamondsuit}_{(\sigma^{-b}\varphi^{a},b)}FC^{*}(\sigma^{*}H_{\lambda},\sigma^{*}I)[2\mu_{\sigma}],\qquad(\textrm{1-orbit }x)\mapsto\sigma^{*}x,

where
\mu_{\sigma}
is the Maslov index of
\sigma
. These isomorphisms commute with continuation maps
in the sense of
[
Sei97
, Cor.4.8]
and
[
Rit14
, Thm.18]
.
In the case
\sigma=\varphi
, we get an isomorphism on cohomology,

(114)

\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}:E^{\diamondsuit}_{(a,b)}HF^{*}(H_{\lambda},I)\cong E^{\diamondsuit}_{(a-b,b)}HF^{*}(H_{\lambda-1},I)[2\mu],\qquad(\textrm{1-orbit }x)\mapsto\varphi^{*}x,

where
\mu
is the Maslov index of
\varphi
.
At chain level, (
114
) holds if we replace
H_{\lambda-1}
by
\varphi^{*}H_{\lambda}=H_{\lambda}-H
.
In this case, compatibility with continuation maps is the commutative diagram:

(115)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\diamondsuit}_{(a,b)}HF^{*}(H_{\lambda})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\diamondsuit}_{(a,b)}HF^{*}(H_{\lambda+\lambda^{\prime}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\diamondsuit}_{(a-b,b)}HF^{*}(H_{\lambda-1})[2\mu]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\diamondsuit}_{(a-b,b)}HF^{*}(H_{\lambda+\lambda^{\prime}-1})[2\mu]}
continuation
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}}
continuation

A.2.
Comparison with the literature.

The non-equivariant version of (
113
) for closed symplectic manifolds is due to Seidel
[
Sei97
]
; the version for symplectic cohomology of convex symplectic manifolds is due to Ritter
[
Rit14
,
Rit16
]
. These were adapted to the
E^{-}
-equivariant setup by Liebenschutz-Jones
[
LJ20
,
LJ21
]
.
We are adapting it to symplectic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-manifolds, for all three equivariant versions
E^{\diamondsuit}
, using the Morse–Bott–Floer model setup from
[
RŽ24
, Appendix]
.

Seidel
[
Sei97
]
considers more generally loops of Hamiltonian diffeomorphisms. In that case
K_{t}
is time-dependent, and
\sigma^{*}H_{\lambda}=H_{\lambda}\circ\sigma_{t}-K_{t}\circ\sigma_{t}
. In our setup,
K=K_{t}
is time-independent, and the commutativity of
\sigma,\varphi
ensures
\mathfrak{F}_{\min}\cap\mathrm{Fix}(\sigma)\neq\emptyset
(proved later); it follows that
38
38

38

e.g. see
[
LJ20
, Proof of (5.2)]
.

\omega(X_{H},X_{K})=0
, so

(116)

H\circ\sigma_{t}=H\qquad\textrm{ and }\qquad K\circ\varphi_{t}=K.

In Seidel
[
Sei97
]
, the Floer chain complex only allows contractible Hamiltonian
1
-orbits, and in fact the generators of the chain complex require a choice of
capping
, a smooth map
\widetilde{x}:\mathbb{D}\to Y
bounding the
1
-orbit
x:S^{1}\to Y
.
One then identifies caps if their difference is a spherical classes in

G:=\pi_{2}(Y)/(\ker[\omega]\cap\ker c_{1}(Y)).

More precisely,
\widetilde{x}
lives in a (connected) covering space

(117)

\widetilde{\mathcal{L}_{0}Y}\to\mathcal{L}_{0}Y,\quad\widetilde{x}\mapsto x,

of the space of contractible free loops in
Y
, with abelian deck group
G
. The Novikov ring used by Seidel is more complicated than in this paper, because it involves using
G

[
Sei97
, Definition 3.10]
.
Seidel shows that
\sigma:\mathcal{L}_{0}Y\to\mathcal{L}_{0}Y
,
x\mapsto\sigma^{*}x
, admits a lift
\widetilde{\sigma}
to
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}_{0}Y}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{L}_{0}Y}
,
\widetilde{x}\mapsto\widetilde{\sigma}(\widetilde{x})
. This requires showing that
\sigma
preserves
\mathcal{L}_{0}Y
, using the Arnol’d conjecture
for closed symplectic manifolds
[
Sei97
, Lemma 2.2]
. In these conventions, the chain isomorphism (
113
) arises by linearly extending
\widetilde{x}\mapsto\widetilde{\sigma}(\widetilde{x}).

In our setup, we do not need to appeal to the Arnol’d conjecture:
\sigma
has a fixed point by (
112
), so
\sigma
fixes the
constant cap

c_{x_{\min}}
at
x_{\min}
, therefore
\sigma^{*}(\mathcal{L}_{0}Y)
cannot land in any other component of
\mathcal{L}Y
other than
\mathcal{L}_{0}Y
.
We choose a specific lift
\widetilde{\sigma}
, called
canonical lift
, determined by the condition

(118)

\widetilde{\sigma}(c_{x_{\min}})=c_{x_{\min}}.

This lift does not depend on the choice of
x_{\min}
, because we will see that
\mathrm{Min}\,K|_{\mathfrak{F}_{\min}}
is path-connected.

In our case, the
1
-orbits of
H_{\lambda}
correspond to orbits (of various periods
p\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
) of the
S^{1}
-flow
\varphi_{t}
, and such orbits admit
canonical caps

\widetilde{x}_{\mathrm{can}}
given by applying the
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action
\varphi
(see (
123
)).
However, for orbits on the right in (
113
) there are no canonical caps because
\sigma^{-b}\varphi^{a}
is just a Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-action, not a contracting
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action.
We also use a simpler Novikov ring: as in
[
Rit16
, Sec.2A,5B]
we have a formal variable
T
to keep track of
[\omega]
-values; when
c_{1}(Y)=0
or
Y
is monotone (
c_{1}(Y)\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}[\omega]
, placing
T
in a suitable positive degree) this ensures a
\mathbb{Z}
-grading; beyond that, one would need another formal variable to keep track of
c_{1}(Y)
-values or one makes do with a
\mathbb{Z}/2
-grading. We comment further on these technical issues in the two Remarks below.

We then declare that, on the left in (
113
),
x
comes with its canonical cap
\widetilde{x}_{\mathrm{can}}
, whereas on the right
\sigma^{*}x
comes with the
induced cap

\widetilde{\sigma}(\widetilde{x}_{\mathrm{can}})
determined by the canonical lift
\widetilde{\sigma}
.
From (
113
), one can recover the version for Seidel’s Novikov ring (see
Remark

A.3
), and the bijection on generators that induces (
113
) is correctly
\mathbb{Z}
-graded if we take into account these capping conventions.

Remark A.2
.

Suppose we wish to change cappings on the right in (
113
). Abbreviate
y=\sigma^{*}x
, suppose we change induced caps
\widetilde{y}_{\mathrm{ind}}
to new caps
\widetilde{y}_{\mathrm{new}}
. We get a spherical class
g_{y}:=\widetilde{y}_{\mathrm{ind}}\#(-\widetilde{y}_{\mathrm{new}})\in G
, with deck group action
\widetilde{y}_{\mathrm{ind}}=g_{y}(\widetilde{y}_{\mathrm{new}})
. Suppose we use the Novikov ring

\mathbb{K}(\!(S)\!)=\Big\{\textstyle\sum k_{n_{i}}S^{n_{i}}:k_{n_{i}}\in\mathbb{K},n_{i}\in\mathbb{Z},n_{i}\to\infty\Big\},

where
\mathbb{K}=\{\sum n_{j}T^{a_{j}}:a_{j}\in\mathbb{R},a_{j}\to\infty,n_{j}\in\mathbb{B}\}
, and
\mathbb{B}
is a choice of base field with
\mathrm{char}(\mathbb{B})=0
.
We place the formal variables
T,S
in degrees
|T|=0
,
|S|=2
. The above corresponds to a change of basis on the right in (
113
),

(119)

y\mapsto S^{c_{1}(Y)[g_{y}]}T^{[\omega](g_{y})}y.

This correctly keeps track of the
\mathbb{Z}
-grading, because the grading of
y
thought of as
\widetilde{y}_{\mathrm{new}}
has increased by
2c_{1}(Y)(g_{y})
compared to
\widetilde{y}_{\mathrm{ind}}
(using a general property of Conley-Zehnder indices when one glues in a sphere).
When
c_{1}(Y)=0
, one does not need
S
.
When
c_{1}(Y)\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}[\omega]
, say
c_{1}(Y)=k[\omega]
,
one can omit
S
by placing
T
in degree
|T|=2k
.
In all other cases, one can omit
S
at the cost of downgrading the
\mathbb{Z}
-grading to a
\mathbb{Z}/2
-grading.

Remark A.3
.

We briefly explain the passage between Seidel’s Novikov ring and ours.
Suppose
u
is a Floer trajectory from
x
to
y
, for a Hamiltonian
H_{\lambda}
.
Its energy is
E(u)=\int u^{*}\omega+H_{\lambda}(x)-H_{\lambda}(y)
.
Omitting the orientation sign, it contributes

T^{E(u)}x
to the Floer differential
d(y)
. Now consider cappings:
gluing a capping
\widetilde{x}
with
u
yields a capping of
y
: the one obtained by lifting
u
to the cover starting from
\widetilde{x}
.
If we already prescribed a capping
\widetilde{y}
, then over the Novikov ring using cappings the contribution to
d(\widetilde{y})
is
g^{-1}\cdot\widetilde{x}
, where
g=\widetilde{x}\#u\#(-\widetilde{y})\in G.

By construction,
g
is determined by the
[\omega],c_{1}(Y)
values on the spherical class
g
. The
c_{1}(Y)
value is determined by grading considerations. The
[\omega]
value is determined by
\int u^{*}\omega
, and
E(u)
recovers
\int u^{*}\omega
. In conclusion, we recover
g
.
The Hamiltonian contributions in
E(u)
are harmless as they are
u
-independent:
the change of basis
x\mapsto T^{H_{\lambda}(x)}x
induces a chain isomorphism, after which the above contribution
T^{E(u)}x
changes to
T^{\int u^{*}\omega}x
.

In conclusion, the passage from Seidel’s Novikov ring to our Novikov ring, means the following replacement of generators on the two respective sides of (
113
):

(120)

\widetilde{x}_{\mathrm{can}}\mapsto T^{-H_{\lambda}(x)}x\qquad\textrm{ and }\qquad\widetilde{\sigma}(\widetilde{x}_{\mathrm{can}})\mapsto T^{-(\sigma^{*}H_{\lambda})(\sigma^{*}x)}\sigma^{*}x.

Therefore, the more “natural” way to write (
113
) (which corresponds to
\widetilde{x}_{\mathrm{can}}\mapsto\widetilde{y}_{\mathrm{ind}}
), taking into account those basis changes, is:

(121)

x\mapsto T^{H_{\lambda}(x)-(\sigma^{*}H_{\lambda})(\sigma^{*}x)}\,\sigma^{*}x.

A.3.
Explanation of twist in the
S^{1}
-actions

Recall the
S^{1}
-action on free contractible loops,

(122)

\sigma^{*}:\mathcal{L}_{0}Y\to\mathcal{L}_{0}Y,\;(\sigma^{*}x)(t)=(\sigma^{-1}x)(t)=\sigma_{-t}x(t).

Lemma A.4
.

Let
\sigma
be a Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-action commuting with
\varphi.

Then

(1)

\sigma_{t}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})=\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
for all
t
, for all connected components
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
of
\mathfrak{F}=\mathrm{Fix}(\varphi)
,

(2)

\sigma
is a Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-action on the connected closed symplectic submanifold
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}\subset Y
,

(3)

\sigma^{*}:\{
constant
\varphi
-orbits in
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}\}\to\{
possibly non-constant 1-orbits for
\sigma^{-1}
in
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}\}
is surjective,

(4)

\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}\cap\mathrm{Fix}(\sigma)=\sqcup(
connected components of
\mathrm{Crit}(K|_{\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}}))
, one of which is
\mathrm{Min}\,K|_{\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}}
,

(5)

in particular
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}\cap\mathrm{Fix}(\sigma)\neq\emptyset
, and these are the constant orbits arising on the right in (3).

Proof.

By
[
RŽ23
]
,
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}\subset Y
is a closed symplectic submanifold. Commutativity of
\sigma,\varphi
implies that,
\sigma_{t}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})=\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
for each
t
: indeed, if
x\in\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
, then
\sigma_{t}(x)=\sigma_{t}(\varphi_{s}(x))=\varphi_{s}(\sigma_{t}(x))
, so
\sigma_{t}(x)\in\mathrm{Fix}(\varphi)=\mathfrak{F}
. Since
\sigma_{0}=\mathrm{id},
it follows that
\sigma_{t}(x)
is trapped in
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
. Thus
\sigma_{t}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})\subset\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
. By considering
\sigma_{-t}
we also obtain the reverse inclusion, so it is an equality.
Finally,
\mathrm{Min}\,K|_{\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}}
is non-empty as
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
is compact, and (as we assume
Y
is connected) it is connected e.g. by
[
Fra59
]
(cf.
[
RŽ23
, Sec.1]
).
∎

With
\sigma
as in the Lemma, we get an action on the Borel model by
(w,x)\mapsto(w,\sigma^{*}x)
. This action does not in general preserve the relation (
67
):

Lemma A.5
.

The following diagram commutes

\textstyle{(w,x)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma\textrm{-action}}
\scriptstyle{\theta\textrm{-action}}
\textstyle{(w,\sigma^{*}x)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta\textrm{-action}}
\textstyle{(\theta^{-1}\cdot w,\theta\cdot_{(a,b)}x)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma\textrm{-action}}
\textstyle{(\theta^{-1}w,\sigma^{*}(\theta\cdot_{(a,b)}x))=(\theta^{-1}w,\theta\cdot_{(a-b,b)}\sigma^{*}x)}

Note that in the target instead of using the
\varphi^{a}
action on
Y
, we are using the
\varphi^{a-b}
action on
Y
.

Suppose either
a>b\geq 0
, or
a<b\leq 0
. Then the weight
(a,b)
and the target weight
(a-b,b)
are either both free, or both not free.

Proof.

This follows from

\sigma_{t}^{-1}\varphi_{a\theta}(x(t-b\theta))=\varphi_{(a-b)\theta}(\sigma_{t-b\theta}^{-1}x(t-b\theta))
, using
\sigma_{t}=\varphi_{t}.

The final claim is immediate if
b=0
, so assume
b\neq 0
.
If
(a,b)
is not free, then
\tfrac{a}{b}=\tfrac{k}{m}
, some
k\geq 1
and some weight
m\geq 1
of
\varphi
, hence
\tfrac{a-b}{b}=\tfrac{k-m}{m}
, and
k-m>0
(so
\geq 1
) by the assumptions on
a,b
, so
(a-b,b)
is not free. If
(a-b,b)
is not free, then
\tfrac{a-b}{b}=\tfrac{k^{\prime}}{m}
, so
\tfrac{a}{b}=\tfrac{k^{\prime}+m}{m}
, and
k^{\prime}+m>k^{\prime}\geq 1
, so
(a,b)
is not free.
∎

Using the notation from
[
LJ20
]
that we already used in (
68
), the key idea from
[
Sei97
]
is that the pull-back data

\sigma^{*}H_{w,t}^{\mathrm{eq}}:=H_{w,t}^{\mathrm{eq}}\circ\sigma_{t}-K\circ\sigma_{t}
and
\sigma^{*}I=d\sigma_{t}^{-1}\circ I\circ d\sigma_{t},

relates the Floer action
1
-forms

(dA_{H_{w,t}^{\mathrm{eq}}})|_{x}=(dA_{\sigma^{*}H_{w,t}^{\mathrm{eq}}})|_{\sigma^{*}x}\circ d\sigma^{*}|_{x}:T_{x}\mathcal{L}Y\to\mathbb{R}.

Thus there is a bijection of
1
-orbits,
\mathrm{Zeros}(dA_{H_{w,t}^{\mathrm{eq}}})\to\mathrm{Zeros}(dA_{\sigma^{*}H_{w,t}^{\mathrm{eq}}})
,
x\mapsto\sigma^{*}x
. Similarly, the definition of
\sigma^{*}I
ensures a bijection of Floer trajectories via
u\mapsto\sigma^{*}u
, with
(\sigma^{*}u)(s,t)=\sigma_{-t}u(s,t)
.

Lemma A.6
.

Let
\sigma
be any Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-action
\sigma
that commutes with
\varphi
.
Suppose we use the weight
b
loop-action, and the action on
Y
by
\sigma^{*}\varphi^{a}=\sigma^{-b}\circ\varphi^{a}
(when
\sigma=\varphi
, this is
\varphi^{a-b}
).
Then the time-dependent Hamiltonian function
\sigma^{*}H^{\mathrm{eq}}_{w,t}:S^{\infty}\times Y\to\mathbb{R}
is
S^{1}
-equivariant.

Proof.

By definition of all
\theta
-actions in play, using commutativity of
\sigma,\varphi
, (
68
) and (
116
):

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\sigma^{*}H^{\mathrm{eq}}_{\theta^{-1}\cdot w,t+b\theta}\circ(\sigma^{*}\varphi^{a})|_{\theta}&=&H^{\mathrm{eq}}_{\theta^{-1}\cdot w,t+b\theta}\circ\sigma_{t+b\theta}\circ\sigma_{-b\theta}\circ\varphi_{a\theta}-K\circ\sigma_{t+b\theta}\circ\sigma_{-b\theta}\circ\varphi_{a\theta}\\
&=&H^{\mathrm{eq}}_{\theta^{-1}\cdot w,t+b\theta}\circ\varphi_{a\theta}\circ\sigma_{t}-K\circ\varphi_{a\theta}\circ\sigma_{t}\\
&=&H^{\mathrm{eq}}_{w,t}\circ\sigma_{t}-K\circ\sigma_{t}\\
&=&\sigma^{*}H^{\mathrm{eq}}_{w,t}.\qed\end{array}

A.4.
Further discussions of cappings.

We now continue the discussion from
Section

A.2
.
The cover (
117
) consists of all classes of cappings
\widetilde{x}
, with deck group action given by gluing in spherical classes from
G
.
Since (
117
) is a covering map, a (
G
-equivariant) lift

\widehat{\sigma}:\widetilde{\mathcal{L}_{0}Y}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{L}_{0}Y}
of the action (
122
) is uniquely determined by where it sends the constant cap
c_{x_{\min}}
at the
x_{\min}
from (
122
). So it is determined by a choice of capping
\widehat{\sigma}(c_{x_{\min}})=\widetilde{x_{\min}}
of
x_{\min}
.
Explicitly, if
\widetilde{\nu}:[0,1]\to\widetilde{\mathcal{L}_{0}Y}
is a path starting at
\widetilde{\nu}(0)=\widetilde{x_{\min}}
, then
\widehat{\sigma}(\widetilde{\nu}(s))=\widetilde{\nu}(s)
for all
s
.
The
canonical lift

\widetilde{\sigma}
is defined by requiring that it fixes the constant cap
c_{x_{\min}}
.
Since
\widetilde{x_{\min}}=gc_{x_{\min}}
for some
g\in G
, we see that the lifts are classified as the
G
-images of the canonical lift:
\widehat{\sigma}=g\widetilde{\sigma}
for
g\in G.

[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]

Lemma A.7
.

Let
\widetilde{x}:\mathbb{D}\to Y
be a capping of any loop
x
in
Y
.
Let
\gamma:[0,1]\to Y
be a path from
x_{\min}
to the centre of the cap,
z:=\widetilde{x}(0)
.
Let
\sigma^{*}\gamma
be the capping of the loop
\sigma^{*}z=\sigma_{-t}z
given by
\sigma_{-t}\gamma(s).

Let
\sigma^{*}\widetilde{x}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{L}_{0}Y}
be the obvious cylinder
\sigma_{-t}\widetilde{x}(re^{2\pi it})
joining
\sigma^{*}z
and
\sigma^{*}x
.
Then the canonical cap satisfies

\widetilde{\sigma}(\widetilde{x})=(\sigma^{*}\gamma)\#\sigma^{*}\widetilde{x}.

Also,
\sigma^{*}\gamma
depends on
z
,
\sigma
, not on
x_{\min},\gamma
.
If
\widetilde{x}=c_{x}
is the constant disc, then
\widetilde{\sigma}(c_{x})=\sigma^{*}\gamma.

If
x\in\mathrm{Fix}(\sigma)
, then
\widetilde{\sigma}(c_{x})=A^{\sigma}_{x}\,c_{x}
where
A^{\sigma}_{x}=\sigma^{*}\gamma\in G
is a spherical class dependent only on
\sigma,x
.

Proof.

We first prove that
\widetilde{x}=\gamma\#\widetilde{x}
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}_{0}Y}
.
The path
s_{0}\mapsto(\gamma)|_{[s_{0},1]}\#\widetilde{x}
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}_{0}Y}
, from

\gamma\#\widetilde{x}
to
\widetilde{x}
,
consists of cappings of
x
.
It must be a constant path because its projection via the covering map (
117
) is a constant path at
x
. Thus
\widetilde{x}=\gamma\#\widetilde{x}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{L}_{0}Y}
.

Concatenate the path
s_{0}\mapsto\sigma^{*}(\gamma|_{[0,s_{0}]})
with the path
r_{0}\mapsto\sigma_{-t}\widetilde{x}(re^{2\pi it})|_{r\in[0,r_{0}],t\in S^{1}}
to get a path
\widetilde{\nu}:[0,1]\to\widetilde{\mathcal{L}_{0}Y}
starting at
\sigma^{*}(\gamma|_{s=0})=c_{x_{\min}}
. By the previous discussion,
\widetilde{\sigma}(\widetilde{x})=\widetilde{\sigma}(\gamma\#\widetilde{x})=\widetilde{\nu}(1)
, and the equation in the claim follows. It follows, a posteriori, from the equation that
\sigma^{*}\gamma
does not depend on
\gamma
. Independence from
x_{\min}
holds because
\mathrm{Min}\,K|_{\mathfrak{F}_{\min}}
is connected by
Lemma

A.4
.
∎

The following picture should be helpful while reading the subsequent discussion.

Let
x
be a
1
-orbit of
H_{\lambda}
, so a
1
-orbit of
\varphi_{pt}
where
p=c^{\prime}(H(x))
.
Following
[
RŽ23
, Sec.4.3]
, the
canonical capping

D_{\varphi}(x)
of
x:[0,1]\to Y
is represented by the pseudo-holomorphic disc
\widetilde{x}_{\mathrm{can}}
obtained by applying the
\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}
-action; explicitly: we apply the
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action by
\varphi
,

(123)

\widetilde{x}_{\mathrm{can}}:\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}\times[0,1]\to Y,\;(s,t)\mapsto e^{2\pi p(s+it)}\cdot x(0),

and we add in the
convergence point

x_{0}
of
x
, which is the
\varphi
-fixed point obtained as
s\to-\infty
.
The convergence point
x_{0}
lies in some component
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}\subset\mathfrak{F}=\mathrm{Fix}(\varphi)
. We denote this
\alpha
by
\alpha(x)
.
Let
\gamma_{x_{0}}:[0,1]\to Y
be a path from
x_{\min}
to
x_{0}
. By
Lemma

A.7
, for

D(\sigma^{*}x_{0}):=\sigma^{*}\gamma_{x_{0}}
and
\sigma^{*}D_{\varphi}(x)=\sigma^{*}\widetilde{x}_{\mathrm{can}}
:

Corollary A.8
.

The cap
D(\sigma^{*}x_{0})\in\widetilde{\mathcal{L}_{0}Y}
only depends on
x_{0}
,
\sigma
, not on the choices
x_{\min},\gamma_{x_{0}}
, and

\widetilde{\sigma}(D_{\varphi}(x))=D(\sigma^{*}x_{0})\#\sigma^{*}D_{\varphi}(x).

Remark A.9
.

If the
S^{1}
-action
\sigma
commutes with the
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action
\varphi
(e.g. for commuting
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-actions
\sigma,\varphi
), then
\sigma^{*}D_{\varphi}(x)
is the capping of
\sigma^{*}x
obtained by applying the
\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}
-action by
\varphi
analogous to (
123
).

By
Lemma

A.4
,
\mathrm{Min}\,K|_{\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}}\subset\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}\cap\mathrm{Fix}(\sigma)
is connected.
Pick any
f_{\alpha}\in\mathrm{Min}\,K|_{\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}}
.
Pick a path
\gamma_{\alpha}:[0,1]\to Y
from
x_{\min}
to
f_{\alpha}
. By
Lemma

A.7
we get a spherical class,

A_{\alpha}:=A^{\sigma}_{f_{\alpha}}=\sigma^{*}\gamma_{\alpha}\in G.

Pick a path
\phi_{x_{0}}:[0,1]\to\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha(x)}
from
f_{\alpha(x)}
to
x_{0}
, to get a cap
F(\sigma^{*}x_{0}):=\sigma^{*}\phi_{x_{0}}\subset\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha(x)}
for
\sigma^{*}x_{0}
.
It lies in
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha(x)}
, by
Lemma

A.4
.(1).

Corollary A.10
.

The spherical class
A_{\alpha}\in G
only depends on
\alpha
,
\sigma
, not on the choices
x_{\min},f_{\alpha},\gamma_{\alpha}
.
The cap
F(\sigma^{*}x_{0})\in\widetilde{\mathcal{L}_{0}Y}
only depends on
x_{0}
,
\sigma
, not on the choices
f_{\alpha(x)},\phi_{\alpha(x)}
, and

(124)

\widetilde{\sigma}(D_{\varphi}(x))=A_{\alpha(x)}\#F(\sigma^{*}x_{0})\#\sigma^{*}D_{\varphi}(x).

Proof.

By
Lemma

A.7
,

\widetilde{\sigma}(c_{f_{\alpha}})=A_{\alpha}c_{f_{\alpha}}
and
A_{\alpha}
only depends on
\alpha,\sigma
. As
A_{\alpha}
does not depend on the choice of path, we can replace
\gamma_{\alpha(x)}
by
\gamma_{x_{0}}\#-\phi_{x_{0}}
to deduce
A_{\alpha(x)}=D(\sigma^{*}x_{0})\#-F(\sigma^{*}x_{0})
in
G.

By
Lemma

A.7
,

D(\sigma^{*}x_{0})
does not depend on the choice of path, so we can replace
\gamma_{x_{0}}
by
\gamma_{x_{0}}\#-\phi_{x_{0}}\#\phi_{x_{0}}
to deduce

D(\sigma^{*}x_{0})=D(\sigma^{*}x_{0})\#(-F(\sigma^{*}x_{0}))\#F(\sigma^{*}x_{0})\in\widetilde{\mathcal{L}_{0}Y}
.
The claim follows by
Corollary

A.8
.
∎

A.5.
Beyond cappings.

We continue the discussion in Remarks
A.2
-
A.3
.
Let
c
be a closed differential
2
-form representing
2c_{1}(Y)
.
Let
v:\mathbb{D}\to Y
be a smooth disc
v:\mathbb{D}\to Y
.
Abbreviate

\textstyle\omega(v):=\int v^{*}\omega,\qquad c(v):=\int v^{*}c,\qquad\intercal(v):=S^{c(v)}T^{\omega(v)}.

If
\widetilde{x}
is a capping of a
1
-orbit
x
, we can declare that

\intercal(\widetilde{x})x
(over our Novikov ring) correponds to the generator
\widetilde{x}
over Seidel’s Novikov ring.
In this convention,
x
is a generator over our Novikov ring but it typically does not correspond to a capping (it corresponds to the constant cap
c_{x}
when
x
is constant).
Under this convention,
abbreviating
\widetilde{x}_{\mathrm{can}}:=D_{\varphi}(x)
and
\widetilde{y}_{\mathrm{ind}}:=\widetilde{\sigma}(\widetilde{x}_{\mathrm{can}})
,
the map (
113
) becomes

x\mapsto\intercal(\widetilde{x}_{\mathrm{can}})^{-1}\intercal\!(\widetilde{y}_{\mathrm{ind}})\,y
.
For example, (
119
) would arise from the observation

\intercal(\widetilde{y}_{\mathrm{ind}})\,y=\intercal(g_{y})\intercal\!(\widetilde{y}_{\mathrm{new}})\,y.

If we wish to apply the convention (
121
), then (
113
) becomes

(125)

x\mapsto T^{H_{\lambda}(x)-(\sigma^{*}H_{\lambda})(\sigma^{*}x)}\,\intercal(\widetilde{x}_{\mathrm{can}})^{-1}\intercal\!(\widetilde{y}_{\mathrm{ind}})\,y.

This formula seems complicated, but has the advantage of being completely independent of cappings. Another motivation comes from applications where
c_{1}(Y)=0
or
c_{1}(Y)\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}[\omega]
: there we can omit the
S
-variable, and it is the messy (
125
) which will in fact yield the neatest expression, in
(
126
).
To obtain this, we first require formulas for the
[\omega]
-values of cappings.

Lemma A.11
.

For any
1
-orbit
x
of
H_{\lambda}
, arising at slope
p=c^{\prime}(H(x)),
we have:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\omega(D_{\varphi}(x))&=&H_{\lambda}(x)-pH(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha(x)}),\\[4.2679pt]
\omega(\sigma^{*}D_{\varphi}(x))&=&(\sigma^{*}H_{\lambda})(\sigma^{*}x)-pH(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha(x)})+K(x_{0}),\\[4.2679pt]
\omega(D(\sigma^{*}x_{0}))&=&\min K|_{\mathfrak{F}_{\min}}-K(x_{0}),\\[4.2679pt]
\omega(\widetilde{\sigma}(D_{\varphi}(x)))&=&\min K|_{\mathfrak{F}_{\min}}+(\sigma^{*}H_{\lambda})(\sigma^{*}x)-pH(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha(x)}).\end{array}

Moreover,

\omega(A_{\alpha(x)})=\min K|_{\mathfrak{F}_{\min}}-\min K|_{\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha(x)}}

and

\omega(F(\sigma^{*}x_{0}))=\min K|_{\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha(x)}}-K(x_{0})
.

Proof.

Let
\psi_{t}
be the Hamiltonian flow for some time-independent Hamiltonian
K_{\psi}:Y\to\mathbb{R}
.
Let
u(s,t)=(\psi^{*}\gamma)(s,t):=\psi_{-t}\gamma(s),

for a smooth curve
\gamma
in
Y
from
y_{-}
to
y_{+}
.
Then
\partial_{t}u=-X_{K_{\psi}}|_{\gamma(s)}
, so

\textstyle\omega(u):=\int u^{*}\omega=\int\omega(\partial_{s}u,\partial_{t}u)\,ds\,dt=-\int dK_{\psi}(\partial_{s}u)\,ds\,dt=-\int\partial_{s}(K_{\psi}\circ u)\,ds\,dt=K_{\psi}(y_{-})-K_{\psi}(y_{+}).

The case
D_{\varphi}(x)
from (
123
) corresponds to
\psi_{-t}=\varphi_{pt}
. We take
K_{\psi}=-pH
, the curve
\gamma(s)=e^{2\pi ps}\cdot x(0)
for
s\in(-\infty,0]
, with
y_{-}=x_{0}
(the convergence point as
s\to-\infty
), and
y_{+}=x(0)
. Then
\omega(D_{\varphi}(x))=pH(x(0))-pH(x_{0})
, and the claim follows (the first equation).

The case
\sigma^{*}D_{\varphi}(x)
corresponds to taking
\psi_{-t}=\sigma_{-t}\varphi_{pt}
, so we take
K_{\psi}=K-pH
, and the same data
\gamma
,
y_{-}
,
y_{+}
as above. Then
\omega(\sigma^{*}D_{\varphi}(x))=(pH-K)(x(0))-(pH-K)(x_{0})
, which yields the claim.

The case
\omega(D(\sigma^{*}x_{0}))
uses
\psi_{t}=\sigma_{t}
,
K_{\psi}=K
,
\gamma=\gamma_{x_{0}}.
So
\omega(D(\sigma^{*}x_{0}))=K(x_{\min})-K(x_{0}).

By
Corollary

A.8
,
\omega(\widetilde{\sigma}(D_{\varphi}(x)))=\omega(\sigma^{*}D_{\varphi}(x))+\omega(D(\sigma^{*}x_{0}))
. The last claim uses
Corollary

A.10
.
∎

Corollary A.12
.

In the convention (
125
), but ignoring the formal
S
variable, (
113
) becomes

(126)

x\mapsto T^{\min K|_{\mathfrak{F}_{\min}}}\,\sigma^{*}x.

In particular, (
114
) becomes

x\mapsto T^{H(\mathfrak{F}_{\min})}\,\varphi^{*}x
.

Proof.

Omitting
S
,

\intercal(\widetilde{x}_{\mathrm{can}})=T^{H_{\lambda}(x)-pH(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha(x)})}
,
and

\intercal(\widetilde{y}_{\mathrm{ind}})=T^{\min K|_{\mathfrak{F}_{\min}}+(\sigma^{*}H_{\lambda})(\sigma^{*}x)-pH(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha(x)})}
, by
Lemma

A.11
.
Then

\intercal(\widetilde{x}_{\mathrm{can}})^{-1}\intercal(\widetilde{y}_{\mathrm{ind}})=T^{(\sigma^{*}H_{\lambda})(\sigma^{*}x)-H_{\lambda}(x)+\min K|_{\mathfrak{F}_{\min}}}
.
Thus in
(
125
), all
T
-factors clear except the
T^{\min K|_{\mathfrak{F}_{\min}}}
factor.
∎

A.6.
Three commuting actions and compositions.

Let
\sigma,\tau
be commuting Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-actions, commuting with
\varphi
.
Let
K_{\sigma},K_{\tau}
be their moment maps. Then
K_{\sigma\tau}:=K_{\sigma}+K_{\tau}
is a moment map for
\sigma\tau
.
The restriction of these moment maps to
\mathfrak{F}_{\min}
may have different minimal loci in general.
What we used to denote
x_{\min},c_{x_{\min}}
will now be denoted
x_{\sigma},c_{\sigma}
to emphasize the dependence on
\sigma
.
By the same argument as in the proof of
Lemma

A.4
,
\tau_{t}
preserves

M_{\sigma}:=\mathrm{Min}\,K_{\sigma}|_{\mathfrak{F}_{\min}}
for each
t
, and
\mathrm{Min}\,K_{\tau}|_{M_{\sigma}}\subset\mathfrak{F}_{\min}\cap\mathrm{Fix}(\sigma)\cap\mathrm{Fix}(\tau)\subset\mathrm{Fix}(\sigma\tau)
is a closed connected symplectic submanifold.
We pick:

x_{\sigma}\in\mathrm{Min}\,K_{\tau}|_{M_{\sigma}},\qquad x_{\tau}\in\mathrm{Min}\,K_{\sigma}|_{M_{\tau}},\qquad x_{\sigma\tau}\in\mathrm{Min}\,K_{\sigma}|_{M_{\sigma\tau}}.

All three points lie in
\mathfrak{F}_{\min}
and are fixed by
\sigma,\tau,\sigma\tau
.
Pick a path
\gamma^{\sigma}_{\sigma\tau}
from
x_{\sigma}
to
x_{\sigma\tau}
, and a path

\gamma^{\tau}_{\sigma\tau}
from
x_{\tau}
to
x_{\sigma\tau}
.
By
Lemma

A.7
we obtain the following spherical classes in
G
, independent of choices:

A^{\sigma}_{\sigma\tau}:=\sigma^{*}\gamma^{\sigma}_{\sigma\tau},\qquad A^{\tau}_{\sigma\tau}:=\tau^{*}\gamma^{\tau}_{\sigma\tau},\qquad A_{\sigma,\tau}:=A^{\sigma}_{\sigma\tau}+A^{\tau}_{\sigma\tau}.

Lemma A.13
.

The canonical lifts are related by the
G
-action as follows,

\widetilde{\sigma}\widetilde{\tau}=A_{\sigma,\tau}\cdot\widetilde{\sigma\tau}.

In particular, the maps from (
A.1
) satisfy:

\mathcal{S}_{\sigma}\mathcal{S}_{\tau}=T^{[\omega](A_{\sigma,\tau})}\mathcal{S}_{\sigma\tau}[a_{\sigma,\tau}],

where we shift the image of the right map down in grading by
a_{\sigma,\tau}:=2c_{1}(Y)(A_{\sigma,\tau}).

From the case
\tau=\sigma
we deduce that, for any
N\in\mathbb{N}
,

\widetilde{\sigma}^{N}=\widetilde{\sigma^{N}}\qquad\textrm{ and }\qquad\mathcal{S}_{\sigma}^{N}=\mathcal{S}_{\sigma^{N}}.

For
\tau=\sigma^{-1}
, we get
\widetilde{\sigma^{-1}}=A\widetilde{\sigma}^{-1}
and
\mathcal{S}_{\sigma^{-1}}=T^{[\omega](A)}\mathcal{S}_{\sigma}^{-1}[a]
, where
A:=A^{\sigma}_{\sigma^{-1}}
,
a:=2c_{1}(Y)(A)
.

Proof.

By
Lemma

A.7
,

\widetilde{\tau}(c_{\sigma\tau})=A^{\tau}_{\sigma\tau}\cdot c_{\sigma\tau}
,

\widetilde{\sigma}(c_{\sigma\tau})=A^{\sigma}_{\sigma\tau}\cdot c_{\sigma\tau},

and

\widetilde{\sigma\tau}(c_{\sigma\tau})=c_{\sigma\tau}.

By
G
-equivariance,

\widetilde{\sigma}\widetilde{\tau}(c_{\sigma\tau})=\widetilde{\sigma}(A^{\tau}_{\sigma\tau}\cdot c_{\sigma\tau})=A^{\tau}_{\sigma\tau}\cdot\widetilde{\sigma}(c_{\sigma\tau})=A^{\tau}_{\sigma\tau}\cdot(A^{\sigma}_{\sigma\tau}\cdot c_{\sigma\tau})=A_{\sigma,\tau}\cdot c_{\sigma\tau}=A_{\sigma,\tau}\cdot\widetilde{\sigma\tau}(c_{\sigma\tau}).

Since
A_{\sigma,\tau}\cdot\widetilde{\sigma\tau}
and
\widetilde{\sigma}\widetilde{\tau}
agree on
c_{\sigma\tau}
and are both lifts of
\sigma\tau
, it follows that

\widetilde{\sigma}\widetilde{\tau}=A_{\sigma,\tau}\cdot\widetilde{\sigma\tau}
, as required.

The grading shift in (
113
) is by twice the Maslov index
\mu_{\sigma}=\mathrm{Maslov}(\widetilde{\sigma})

[
Sei97
]
. We will need a general property from
[
Sei97
]
about these indices: for any
g\in G
and any lift
\widehat{\sigma}
,

\mathrm{Maslov}(g\cdot\widehat{\sigma})=c_{1}(Y)(g)+\mathrm{Maslov}(\widehat{\sigma}).

The maps
\mathcal{S}_{\sigma}\mathcal{S}_{\tau}
and
\mathcal{S}_{\sigma\tau}
are defined by the actions respectively of
\widetilde{\sigma}\circ\widetilde{\tau}
and
\widetilde{\sigma\tau}
on the covering (
117
).
So although (
113
) seems to suggest that both maps are just
x\mapsto(\sigma\tau)^{*}x=\sigma^{*}\tau^{*}x
on chain level generators, this need not hold for
\mathcal{S}_{\sigma}\mathcal{S}_{\tau}
because
\mathcal{S}_{\tau}
outputs induced caps, not canonical caps.
The equation
\widetilde{\sigma}\widetilde{\tau}=A_{\sigma,\tau}\cdot\widetilde{\sigma\tau}
shows that the discrepancy between cappings is the action of an element
A_{\sigma,\tau}\in G
independent of the input
1
-orbit
x
. Over Seidel’s Novikov ring, this proves the equation
\mathcal{S}_{\sigma}\mathcal{S}_{\tau}=A_{\sigma,\tau}\cdot\mathcal{S}_{\sigma\tau}
.
Over our Novikov ring, if we use both
T,S
variables, this
G
-action would be encoded by:

\mathcal{S}_{\sigma}\mathcal{S}_{\tau}=S^{c_{1}(Y)(A_{\sigma,\tau})}T^{[\omega](A_{\sigma,\tau})}\mathcal{S}_{\sigma\tau}
.
The
S
factor keeps track of gradings, and since that factor is independent of the input
x
, we can omit the
S
-factor at the cost of applying a grading shift. We apply the above Maslov index property to the case
g=A_{\sigma,\tau}
and
\widehat{\sigma}=\widetilde{\sigma\tau}
to deduce that the difference between the grading shifts caused by
\mathcal{S}_{\sigma}\mathcal{S}_{\tau}
and
\mathcal{S}_{\sigma\tau}
is
2c_{1}(Y)(A_{\sigma,\tau})
, as required.

For
\tau=\sigma
we can pick
x_{\sigma}=x_{\tau}=x_{\sigma\tau}
, so the
A
-classes vanish, and by induction we obtain the statement about
\widetilde{\sigma}^{N}
.
For
\tau=\sigma^{-1}
, the
A
-shift is caused because
x_{\sigma^{-1}}\in\mathrm{min}(-K_{\sigma})|_{\mathfrak{F}_{\min}}=\mathrm{max}K_{\sigma}|_{\mathfrak{F}_{\min}}

whereas
x_{\sigma}\in\mathrm{min}K_{\sigma}|_{\mathfrak{F}_{\min}}
.
We pick
x_{\sigma\tau}=x_{\mathrm{id}}=x_{\tau}
. So
A^{\tau}_{\sigma\tau}=0
, thus
A_{\sigma,\tau}=A^{\sigma}_{\sigma\tau}=A^{\sigma}_{\tau}
. The final claim now follows from the general one, using
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{id}}=\mathrm{id}
.
∎

A.7.
Adapting the Seidel isomorphism to the Morse–Bott–Floer setup

Let
\sigma
be a Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-action commuting with
\varphi
. Using terminology and conventions from
[
RŽ24
]
, let
B=B_{p,\beta}
be a Morse–Bott–Floer manifold of
1
-orbits of
H_{\lambda}=c(H)
, where
p=c^{\prime}(H)
(called the
S^{1}
-period, or slope, of
B
). Recall that we can view
B
in two ways: as a collection of
1
-orbits
x(t)
, or we can identify
B
with the collection of initial points
B_{0}=(B_{p,\beta})_{0}:=\{x(0):x\in B\}\subset Y
. The reverse map is
B_{0}\to B
,
y\mapsto x_{y}
, where
x_{y}(t)=\varphi_{p\cdot t}(y)
.
We assume the reader is familiar with the Morse–Bott–Floer model for Floer cohomology from the Appendix in
[
RŽ24
]
.
When we construct auxiliary Morse data for
B
, what we actually mean is Morse data for
B_{0}
: a generic Morse function
f=f_{p,\beta}:B_{0}\to\mathbb{R}
and we consider the negative gradient flowlines of
f
(using the ambient Riemannian metric
g:=\omega(\cdot,I\cdot)
restricted to
B_{0}
). Equivalently, this means that we are working with
\varphi
-invariant constructions on
B
(e.g. the only sections of
TB
allowed are
\varphi
-invariant vector fields along
1
-orbits).

Lemma A.14
.

The obvious identification of Morse–Bott–Floer manifolds

B\to\sigma^{*}B:=\{\sigma^{*}x:x\in B\},\;\;x\mapsto\sigma^{*}x,

corresponds to the identity map, once we identify these manifolds with the collections of initial points:

\{y=x(0)\in Y:x\in B\}=:B_{0}=(\sigma^{*}B)_{0}:=\{(\sigma^{*}x)(0)=x(0)\in Y:\sigma^{*}x\in\sigma^{*}B\}.

Under this identification, all auxiliary data (Riemannian metrics, Morse functions, negative gradient trajectories) used for
B,\sigma^{*}B
can be chosen to be the same, by viewing it as data on
B_{0}=(\sigma^{*}B)_{0}
.

Proof.

As
\sigma
is a Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-action,

\sigma_{t}
satisfies
\sigma_{0}=\mathrm{id}
, so
(\sigma^{*}x)(0)=\sigma_{-0}x(0)=x(0).

The claim is therefore an obvious formal consequence of the above discussion.
39
39

39

A point of confusion may arise when viewing this as an equivariant construction along
1
-orbits.
The flow defining the Morse–Bott–Floer manifold
\sigma^{*}B
is
\psi:=\sigma^{*}\mathrm{Flow}^{t}_{X_{H_{\lambda}}}
, which restricts to
\psi_{p\cdot t}:=\sigma_{-t}\varphi_{p\cdot t}
on
B_{0}
.
A negative gradient flowline
\gamma(s)
in
B_{0}
corresponds to a
\varphi
-invariant flowline
\varphi_{pt}\gamma(s)
of
1
-orbits in
B
(with
s
-derivative
-d\varphi_{pt}\nabla f
).
This maps to a
\psi
-invariant flowline
\psi_{p\cdot t}\gamma(s)
of
1
-orbits in
\sigma^{*}B
(with
s
-derivative
-d\psi_{pt}\nabla f
).
We do not need
\sigma^{*}I=I
(i.e.
\sigma
-invariance of
g=\omega(\cdot,I\cdot)
), we only need
I,g
to be invariant at
t=0
, which is clear:
\sigma_{0}=\mathrm{id}
and
d\sigma_{0}=\mathrm{id}
.
If, at a given time
t
, one wishes
-d\psi_{pt}\nabla f
to be the negative gradient of
(\psi^{*}f)_{t}:=f\circ\psi_{-p\cdot t}
, then in general one must use the
t
-dependent Riemannian metric

(\psi^{*}g)_{t}:=\omega(\cdot,(\sigma^{*}I)_{t}\cdot)
which is time-independent only when
\sigma^{*}I=I
.

∎

Corollary A.15
.

Assuming we use the same auxiliary Morse–Bott–Floer data for the Morse–Bott–Floer manifolds, in the sense of
Lemma

A.14
, the map (
113
) is more precisely defined by linearly extending the map on generators determined by the identity maps
y\mapsto y
for
y\in\mathrm{Crit}(f:B_{0}\to\mathbb{R})
, equivalently:

(\textrm{critical 1-orbit }x(t)=\varphi_{p\cdot t}(y)\textrm{ in }B_{p,\beta})\mapsto(\textrm{critical 1-orbit }(\sigma^{*}x)(t)=\sigma_{-t}\varphi_{p\cdot t}(y)\textrm{ in }\sigma^{*}B_{p,\beta}),

where we recall from
[
RŽ24
, Sec.A.1]
that critical
1
-orbit
x(t)
means
y=x(0)\in\mathrm{Crit}(f_{p,\beta}:B_{p,\beta}\to\mathbb{R}).
